A coherent differential PCF Thanks: This work was partly supported by the ANR project Probabilistic Programming Semantics (PPS) ANR-19-CE48-0014.
Thomas Ehrhard ORCID:0000-0001-5231-5504

Abstract
. The categorical models of the differential lambda-calculus are additive categories because of the Leibniz rule which requires the summation of two expressions and therefore the differential lambda-calculus features unrestricted sums of terms. A natural and simple operational interpretation of such sums of terms is by finite non-determinism although other interpretations, based for instance on quantum superposition, might also be possible. In a previous paper we introduced a categorical framework for differentiation which features a partial form of additivity and is compatible with deterministic denotational models such as coherence spaces and probabilistic models such as probabilistic coherence spaces. Based on this categorical theory we develop a syntax of a deterministic version of the differential lambda-calculus. One nice feature of this new approach to differentiation is that it is compatible with general fixpoints of terms, so our language is actually a differential extension of PCF for which we provide a fully deterministic operational semantics by means of an adapted version of the Krivine Machine.



Aug. 25, 2022Oct. 26, 2023

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 19, Issue 4, Paper 7

1.
Introduction

The differential lambda-calculus
[
ER03
]
extends the
(typed) lambda-calculus with a syntactic notion of differentiation
which is compatible with the basic intuition of differentiation in
Calculus: given
f:E\to F
a sufficiently regular function between
two
\mathbb{R}
-vector spaces (finite dimensional, or Banach…),

f^{\prime}:E\to({E}\multimap{F})
where
{E}\multimap{F}
is the space of linear (and
continuous if we are in infinite dimension) maps
E\to F
such
that
f(x+u)=f(x)+f^{\prime}(x)\cdot u+o(\|{u}\|{})
.
More generally
f^{\prime}(x)
is such that

y\mapsto f(x)+f^{\prime}(x)\cdot(y-x)
is the best affine approximation of

f
which coincides with
f
at
x
.

Syntactically this means that, given a term
M
such that
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
and a term
N
such that

{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{A}
we introduce a term
\mathsf{D}M\cdot N
such
that
{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{D}M\cdot N}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
. Intuitively
M

represents a function
f:A\to B
,
N
an element
u
of
A

and
\mathsf{D}M\cdot N
represents the function
g:A\to B
such
that
g(x)=f^{\prime}(x)\cdot u
.
This syntactic presentation is a convenient way to express the fact
that the derivative has the same regularity as the differentiated
function (intuitively terms represent “smooth” maps, so their
derivatives are themselves smooth) and allows easy iteration of
differentiation (
n
-th derivatives).

Differentiation is inherently related to the algebraic operation of

addition
and the associated operation of subtraction, this is
obvious in the definition of derivative we have all been taught at
school:

f^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{f(x+\varepsilon)-f(x)}{\varepsilon}
. More algebraically this connection manifests itself for instance by
the
Leibniz Rule

(fg)^{\prime}=f^{\prime}g+fg^{\prime}
.
In the differential lambda-calculus, beyond the ordinary

\beta
-reduction, there is a
differential

\beta
-reduction
:
\mathsf{D}(\lambda x^{A}\,{M})\cdot N\mathrel{\to}\frac{\partial M}{\partial x}\cdot N
which uses a
linear substitution

\frac{\partial M}{\partial x}\cdot N
defined by induction on

M
. The definition of this operation involves the Leibniz Rule
1
1

1

Actually the Leibniz Rule is not really related to
multiplication but more fundamentally to the fact that the parameter
of a function can be used more than once that is, to the logical
rule of contraction.
in the syntactic constructs where the variable
x
can be used at
various places, the most typical example being:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial({P})\,{Q}}{\partial x}\cdot N=({\frac{\partial P}{\partial x}\cdot N})\,{Q}+({\mathsf{D}P\cdot\left(\frac{\partial Q}{\partial x}\cdot N\right)})\,{Q}

(1)

which is a combination of the Leibniz Rule and of the Chain
Rule.
Because of this feature of the definition of

\frac{\partial M}{\partial x}\cdot N
we had to extend the syntax
of the lambda-calculus with an addition operation typed as follows

{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{0}}:{A}
{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{1}}:{A}
{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{0}+M_{1}}:{A}

(2)

This rule is most natural if we have in mind the usual mathematical
intuitions about differentiation.
However the lambda-calculus is not only a nice and convenient syntax
for denoting mathematical functions, it is also an expressive
programming language featuring crucial properties of determinism.
But the most natural operational interpretation of this
+

operation is a kind of non-deterministic superposition
2
2

2

Other interpretations might be possible as well, such as,
perhaps, quantum states superposition, but we did not explore this
option yet. It seems clear that “probabilistic superposition” is
not a possible option since it requires non-negative coefficients
whose sum is
\leq 1
: indeed
\mathbf{Pcoh}
is not a model of
differential
LL
.
.
For instance if our calculus has a type
\iota
of integers with

{\Gamma}\vdash{\underline{n}}:{\iota}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
(numerals) then we
can write terms like
\underline{42}+\underline{57}
which is a superposition of
two values (nothing to do with
\underline{99}
of course!).

In the setting of
differentiable programming
where programs can
be formally differentiated (see for instance
[
MP21
]
for
a functional presentation), an addition on terms also plays a crucial
role for the same reason (Leibniz Rule).
This is made possible by the presence of a ground type
\rho
of
real numbers (which can be equipped with the usual addition of
numbers) and by the fact that any type is of shape

{A_{1}}\Rightarrow{(\cdots({A_{n}}\Rightarrow{\rho})\cdots)}
and therefore
inherits from
\rho
a canonical operation of addition defined
pointwise.
For instance, if the language has also a type
\iota
of integers,
it is not clear at all how the differential of a term of type

{\rho}\Rightarrow{\iota}
or of type
{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}
could be defined.
In differentiable programming, differentiation is fundamentally
defined for terms of type

{\rho}\Rightarrow{(\cdots({\rho}\Rightarrow{\rho})\cdots)}
, and extended to
higher types by a method close to logical relations (by induction
on types), in sharp contrast with coherent differentiation where
derivatives can be taken with respect to parameters
of any
type
, and does not rely on any specific numerical datatype.

A natural question is then whether differentiation requires such a
general addition operation on terms and is therefore incompatible with
determinism.
In
[
Ehr23
]
we have provided a semantic negative answer to
this question, based on a new categorical setting that we call

coherent differentiation
.
The basic idea is to replace
additive categories
3
3

3

Categories enriched over commutative monoids.
4
4

4

Or
left-additive categories
which are used for
axiomatizing differentiation in a general cartesian category
\mathcal{C}

which is not necessarily the Kleisli category of a model of
LL
,
see
[
BCS09
,
CC14
]
.
Observe that, even in this apparently weaker setting,
left-additivity also entails that any two morphisms

f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathcal{C}(X,Y)
can be added, whatever be the objects
X,Y

of
\mathcal{C}
so that these categories also feature the kind of
“non-determinism” we are trying to eliminate.
Axiomatizing coherent differentiation in general cartesian
categories requires a more general kind of summability structure
which is presented in another article
[
EW23
]
.
with categories equipped with a weaker structure that we call a

summability structure
: such a category
\mathcal{L}
is endowed with
an endofunctor
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
which intuitively maps any object
X
to
the object
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X
of pairs
(x_{0},x_{1})
such that
x_{0}+x_{1}
is
well defined. This functor comes with natural transformations

\operatorname{\pi}_{0},\operatorname{\pi}_{1},\sigma\in\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X,X)
satisfying suitable
axioms (intuitively they map
(x_{0},x_{1})
to
x_{0}
,
x_{1}
and

x_{0}+x_{1}
respectively).
Thanks to these axioms it is possible to equip
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
with a monad
structure.

Then assuming that
\mathcal{L}
is a resource category (that is, a
cartesian symmetric monoidal category equipped with a resource
modality comonad
\oc{\_}
), the differential structure is
axiomatized as a natural morphism

\operatorname{\partial}_{X}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}{\oc{X}})
which is a
distributive law between the functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
and the comonad

\oc{\_}
, and also between the monad
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
and the functor

\oc{\_}
.
This allows one to extend the monad
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
to the Kleisli category

{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
into a monad that we denote as

(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}},\zeta,\theta)
.
The category
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
has the same objects as
\mathcal{L}
but an element
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
should not be considered as a linear morphism

X\to Y
as in
\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
, but as a morphism which is only
“smooth” (and actually analytic in several concrete models).
The functor
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
acts exactly as
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
on objects but its
action on morphisms implements differentiation: given

f\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
, considered as a smooth map
X\to Y
, the
smooth map

\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}f=(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}f)\,\operatorname{\partial}_{X}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X\,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}Y)
intuitively maps
(x_{0},x_{1})
to
(f(x_{0}),f^{\prime}(x_{0})\cdot x_{1})
.
The basic observation at the core of this work is indeed that, if

x_{0}+x_{1}
is defined, then so must be
f(x_{0})+f^{\prime}(x_{0})\cdot x_{1}
,
as the beginning of the Taylor expansion of
f(x_{0}+x_{1})
at
x_{0}
.
The monad structure of
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
accounts for addition: intuitively the natural transformation
\theta_{X}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}^{2}X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X)
, which is linear
5
5

5

In the sense that it is obtained from a morphism of

\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X)
by composition with the counit

\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}X}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}X},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}X)
of the comonad

\oc{\_}
.
, maps
((x_{00},x_{01}),(x_{10},x_{11}))
to
(x_{00},x_{01}+x_{10})
and
\zeta_{X}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X)

maps
x
to
(x,0)
.
The naturality of these two transformations in
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
expresses
exactly that the differential is linear with respect to this
0
and
to this addition.

The major benefit of using the operation
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
for presenting the
derivative of morphisms of
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
is that doing so we preserve the
information of summability: we know that the two components of

\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}f
can be added without requiring that
all
pairs of
morphisms can be added.
The categorical axiomatization described in
[
Ehr23
]
involves
other natural linear transformations:

\operatorname{\pi}_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X,X)
for
i=0,1
(the two obvious
projections),
\operatorname{\iota}_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X)
for
i=0,1
(the two injections,

\operatorname{\iota}_{0}=\zeta
and
\operatorname{\iota}_{1}
maps
x
to
(0,x)
) and the “canonical flip”
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}^{2}X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}^{2}X)
which
maps
((x_{00},x_{01}),(x_{10},x_{11}))
to
((x_{00},x_{10}),(x_{01},x_{11}))
.

As explained in the introduction of
[
Ehr23
]
this categorical
axiomatization has strong formal similarities with the
tangent
categories
axiomatization of the differential calculus on smooth
manifolds
[
Ros84
]
— of which differential categories are a
special case as shown in
[
CC14
]
—.
One crucial difference is that, though tangent categories are not
additive in general, they feature an unrestricted addition operation
available in “tangent spaces” (axiomatized by means of a pullback)
which is not available in the coherent differential setting.

1.1.
Contents

In the present paper we propose a differential lambda-calculus which
uses the idea of coherent differentiation, but now at the syntactical
level.

1.1.1.
Semantic motivations

Since this work arose from semantic considerations, we first provide
in Section
3
a summary of the categorical
framework for coherent differentiation introduced
in
[
Ehr23
]
.
Our goal is also to make the paper as self-contained as possible.
In this section, we present moreover two concrete instances of this
general notion: the relational model and the probabilistic coherence
space model.
We describe in these well-known models of
LL
various categorical
constructions of coherent differentiation.
These concrete models are also used technically in the paper, the
first for proving that our reduction rules are “complete” for
reducing the terms in Section
6
(see
Section
1.1.5
the explanation of this word in
this context)
and the second — probabilistic coherence spaces
(PCS
[
DE11
]
) — for proving denotationally that this
machine is deterministic in Section
7
.

This latter model has been a major incentive for introducing coherent
differentiation.
It is a model of
LL
, and the morphism of the Kleisli category of
its resource comonad
\oc{\_}
can be seen as analytic functions.
For instance, in PCS, the type of booleans is interpreted as
the set
B
of all sub-probability distributions

x=(x_{\mathbf{t}},x_{\mathbf{f}})
on the booleans (meaning that

x_{\mathbf{t}},x_{\mathbf{f}}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with
x_{\mathbf{t}}+x_{\mathbf{f}}\leq 1
) and a
morphism from
B
to
B
is a function
f:B\to B
with

f(x)=(f_{\mathbf{t}}(x),f_{\mathbf{f}}(x))
such that

\displaystyle f_{\mathbf{t}}(x)=\sum_{n,p\in{\mathbb{N}}}a_{n,p}x_{\mathbf{t}}^{n}x_{\mathbf{f}}^{p}\text{\quad and\quad}f_{\mathbf{f}}(x)=\sum_{n,p\in{\mathbb{N}}}b_{n,p}x_{\mathbf{t}}^{n}x_{\mathbf{f}}^{p}

for uniquely determined
6
6

6

The coefficients can be retrieved from
f
by taking
partial derivatives.

non-negative
coefficients

(a_{n,p},b_{n,p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})_{n,p\in{\mathbb{N}}}
.
Of course
B
has a natural addition operation, but this addition is
only partially defined because the sum of two sub-probability
distributions is not always a sub-probability distribution.
Nevertheless the morphisms of the Kleisli category being analytic in a
rather standard mathematical sense, it is possible to compute their
derivative, and the purpose of coherent differentiation is precisely
to give a meaning to such derivatives in a partially additive setting.

1.1.2.
The choice of
PCF

The categorical framework introduced in
[
Ehr23
]
and
summarized in Section
3
relies on the
semantics of
LL
, and this is deeply motivated by the fact
that, so far, all concrete known models of coherent differentiation
are models of
LL
.
As explained above, in such a model
\mathcal{L}
of
LL
,
differentiation is a distributive law which allows to extend
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}

to a differentiation functor on the cartesian closed Kleisli category

{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
.
Moreover it is shown in
[
Ehr23
]
that, when
\mathcal{L}
is a

Lafont category
(that is, has a cofree modality comonad), a
very simple condition suffices to guarantee that it is a model of
coherent differentiation.
In particular the two models which will play a technical role in
the present paper — the relational model
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
and the probabilistic
coherence space model
\mathbf{Pcoh}
— are both Lafont categories.

This fact has guided us in the choice of the
\lambda
-calculus to
be extended with coherent differential constructs: it seemed natural to
take a language whose translation in such models is as simple as
possible.
From this point of view
PCF

[
Plo77
]
is a reasonable option.
It is a simply typed call-by-name calculus and thus can be interpreted
by means of the standard and simple Girard translation of the

\lambda
-calculus in
LL
whose target is the Kleisli
category
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
.
This choice allows us also to stress a major feature of this new
presentation of differentiation, which is the fact that it is fully
compatible with
general recursive definitions
whose
formalization in
PCF
is particularly simple and natural.
This is deeply related to the limitation we put on the
+

operation: contrarily to the ordinary differential lambda-calculus,
our typing rules do not allow one to write a term such that

\lambda x^{\iota}\,{(x+\underline{42})}
which obviously has no fixpoint
(
\iota
is the type of integers).

Other choices of syntax such as the pure
\lambda
-calculus or
call-by-push-value
[
Lev06
]
would have been possible but would
have required additional categorical ingredients, such as the use of a
reflexive object or of the Eilenberg-Moore
category as in
[
ET19
]
.
Of course we are very interested in studying coherent differentiation
in such languages; this will be the object of further work.

1.1.3.
Syntax

So our calculus is a differential
extension of
PCF
7
7

7

More precisely, of a version of
PCF
extended with a

\mathsf{let}
operation restricted to the unique ground type

\iota
of integers; this is particularly relevant for
probabilistic extensions of this calculus in the spirit
of
[
EPT18a
]
, which is perfectly possible since
it admits
\mathbf{Pcoh}
as a natural denotational model.
where the
main novelty is a differentiation operation on terms: if
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
then
{\Gamma}\vdash{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}}
and this
requires a new type construct
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}
.
The semantics tells us that we should have
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{({A}\Rightarrow{B})}=({A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}})
and we can consider this
equation as a
definition
of
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{({A}\Rightarrow{B})}
.
So the only basic differential construct on types that we need is

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
for all
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
This differential term construction induces a new redex, namely the
term
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{(\lambda x^{A}\,{M})}
(for a term
M
such that

{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{M}:{B}
) and we stipulate that it reduces to
\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})}
where the term
\partial({x},{M})
is
defined by induction on
M
and satisfies

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{M})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
.

This inductive definition of
\partial({x},{M})
involves the use of
constructs
\theta^{d}(N)
,
\operatorname{\iota}_{0}^{d}(N)

etc.
which syntactically
account for the natural transformations alluded to above.
The additional superscript
d
is required to express the fact that
the corresponding operations are applied under
d
applications of
the
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}{}
functor.
For instance in the rule

\partial({x},{({N})\,{P}})=({\theta^{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{N})})})\,{\partial({x},{P})}
,
the
\theta^{0}(\_)
construction implements the addition involved in
the Leibniz Rule required by the fact that the variable
x
may
occur in both
N
and
P
, as in the
Equation (
1
) of the differential
lambda-calculus.
We also stipulate that

\partial({x},{\theta^{d}(N)})=\theta^{d+1}(\partial({x},{N}))
which shows why the

d
superscripts are required.
The same superscripts appear in the basic “arithmetic” constructs

\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(N)
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(N)
,
\mathsf{if}^{d}(N,P_{0},P_{1})
and also in the
aforementioned
\mathsf{let}
construct
\mathsf{let}^{d}(y,N,M)
.
When applying the
\partial({x},{\_})
to these constructs, the
d

superscript is similarly incremented, for instance

\partial({x},{\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(N)})=\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{N}))
.
The
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}(\_)
construct is required because, guided by the
semantics, we set

\partial({x},{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{N}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{N})})
.
Notice that the simple typing rules are not sufficient to “guess”
this rule since in this situation we have
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{N}:{{B}\Rightarrow{C}}
and hence
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{N})}}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{B}}}
and also

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{0}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{N})})}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{B}}}
, the semantic analysis that we
develop in Section
5
is really mandatory.
The syntax also contains a
projection
construct
\operatorname{\pi}_{r}^{d}(M)
where
r\in\{0,1\}
and
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
is the depth
we are now acquainted with. This construct allows one to “extract”
the first (when
r=0
) or second (when
r=1
) component of a term
of type
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}
which represents a kind of summable pair
8
8

8

From our
LL
point of view it is important to keep in mind
that it is not a
multiplicative pair
in which both components
are actually available, but an
additive pair
which offers two
possible options among which one must be chosen: this is precisely
the purpose of our projection construct.
.
The language also contains a
+
operation
9
9

9

Of course there is also its associated “neutral” constant

0
.
on terms which is used in a single reduction rule, namely
\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(\theta^{d}(M))\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(M))+\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(M))
.
So we can understand the construction
\theta^{d}(\_)
as a tag
identifying a place where a sum will have to be introduced when we
will need to extract a component from a “summable
4
-tuple” of
type
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{A}
for some type
A
and the reduction rules show
how these tags are produced and modified during computations.
The
+
term construct is still necessary, just as in the original
differential lambda-calculus of
[
ER03
]
, but thanks to
the
\theta^{d}(\_)
construct we can prove subject reduction

without
the very strong typing (
2
)
which was the source of the non-determinism of the differential
lambda-calculus
10
10

10

To be more precise, as it becomes apparent in
Section
6.1
where a specific reduction strategy
is presented by means of a Krivine machine, we manage to reduce the
use of the
+
to the ground type
\iota
of integers.
This is similar to what happens in differentiable
programming
[
MP21
]
, the main differences being that
our addition is not at all the arithmetic addition of the ground
type and that we allow differentiation wrt. parameters of all types,
not only ground types.
.

1.1.4.
Categorical semantics and soundness

After having presented the syntax of our calculus
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
in
Section
4
, we provide and study in
Section
5
additional categorical constructs which
are definable in the general framework of
Section
3
.
Based on these constructs, we present a general categorical semantics
of our calculus
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
in the cartesian closed category associated
with such a model by the familiar Kleisli construction associated with
the
\oc{\_}
functor and to prove that this interpretation is
invariant under the
\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}
reduction relation (soundness).
This requires to prove a number of categorical equations involving in
particular
additive strengths
11
11

11

The adjective “additive” refers to the fact that this
strength deals with the additive operation of cartesian product and
not to the multiplicative operation of tensor product.
It is shown in
[
Ehr23
]
that the monad
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
has
multiplicative strengths as well which are deeply related to these
additive strengths.
Notice that both strong monads are commutative.

\psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}^{0}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X_{0}\mathrel{\&}X_{1},\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}\left({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}\right))
and

\psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}^{1}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X_{0}\mathrel{\&}\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X_{1},\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}\left({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}\right))
of the monad
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
on
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
.
These strengths are linear and extremely easy to define because

\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
commutes with
\mathrel{\&}
, but their properties are not so
straightforward due to the definition of the functor
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
which
involves the distributive law
\operatorname{\partial}
.
Their main purpose is to define
partial derivatives
: given

f\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}},Y)
the first partial derivative of

f
is
(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}f)\mathrel{\circ}\psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}^{0}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X_{0}\mathrel{\&}X_{1},Y)
and the second partial derivative is
(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}f)\mathrel{\circ}\psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}^{1}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X_{0}\mathrel{\&}\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X_{1},Y)
.
These constructions are of course crucial in the interpretation of

\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
since, for instance,
\partial({x},{M})
must be interpreted as a
derivative with respect to the variable
x
but not to the other
variables occurring in
M
.
Notice that we use
\mathrel{\circ}
for the composition operation in

{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
whereas composition in
\mathcal{L}
is denoted as simple
juxtaposition.

Thanks to this series of categorical lemmas we can prove soundness for
the
\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}
rewriting, meaning that if

M\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}M^{\prime}
then
M
and
M^{\prime}
have the same
interpretation.
The proof is lengthy due mainly to the number of rewriting rules.

1.1.5.
Intersection types, Krivine machine and completeness of
the reduction

We address in Section
6
the major issue of

completeness
:
is our rewriting system
\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}
sufficiently rich for
performing all “required” reductions?
To give a precise meaning to this question, we need an external
reference, independent from our rewriting system.
Denotational models provide us precisely with such a reference, so we
choose the most basic denotational model of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
which is

\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
(see Section
3
complemented
by
6.3.1
) and we present the associated
interpretation of terms by means of a
non-idempotent
intersection typing system
for
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
.
Our completeness expresses that if the interpretation in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
of a
closed term
M
of type
\iota
contains an integer
\nu
then

M
reduces to
\underline{\nu}
using the
\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}
reduction
relation.
As usual in this kind of situation we prove this property for a

particular reduction strategy
that we prefer to present as an
abstract machine in Krivine style.
A state, or
command
, of this machine is a triple
c=(\delta,M,s)
where

•

M
is a closed term of type
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}
where
F
is a
type which is
sharp
in the sense that it is not of shape

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}
; in other words
F=({A_{1}}\Rightarrow{\cdots{{}\Rightarrow{A_{k}}}\Rightarrow{\iota}})
,

•

\delta=\langle{r_{1},\dots,r_{d}}\rangle
is a sequence of length
d

of elements of
\{0,1\}
called
access word

•

and
s
is a stack of type
F\vdash\iota
, meaning that it
represents an evaluation context
\langle{s}\rangle
of type
\iota

whose “hole” has type
F
.

Then the command
c
represents the term
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{r_{1}}^{0}(\cdots\operatorname{\pi}_{r_{d}}^{0}(M)\cdots)]
which is closed of type
\iota
.

We introduce a set of reduction rules
\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}
for this
machine and prove that these reduction rules are simulated in the

\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}
rewriting system through the
c\mapsto\langle c\rangle

translation: this shows that we can really consider this machine just
as a convenient way to express a specific reduction strategy for
applying the reduction
\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}
.

More precisely this rewriting system acts on
finite multisets of
commands
which are summable in the sense that the interpretations
of their elements are summable in any model.
We extend the semantics (and, accordingly, the intersection typing
system) to stacks and commands and prove that, if a command is
typeable in the intersection typing system (and then its type is a
natural number
\nu
) then its
\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}
reduction leads to
a summable multiset
C
of commands which contains the constant

\underline{\nu}
(or more precisely the command

(\langle{}\rangle,\underline{\nu},())
), thus proving our
completeness result.
This proof follows essentially the standard pattern of a reducibility
argument, complicated by the fact that we have to take into account
arbitrary iterations of the
\partial({x},{M})
construct.
This method is developed in Section
6.3.3
.

1.1.6.
Probabilistic semantics and determinism

In Section
7
we prove that the integer

\nu
above is unique, thus showing that the reduction on commands
is essentially deterministic.
To this end we use the fact that the
LL
model of probabilistic
coherence spaces introduced in
[
DE11
]
is a model of
coherent differentiation.
In that model the type
\iota
is interpreted as the set of all
sub-probability distributions on
{\mathbb{N}}
and we observe
12
12

12

This is due to the fact that the formalism
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}

considered here has no construct for generating random integers as
in
[
EPT18a
]
.
that a summable multiset of commands must be interpreted as such a
sub-probability distribution where
all probabilities belong to

{\mathbb{N}}
and hence is either equal to
0
or concentrated on a
single element
\nu
of
{\mathbb{N}}
.
So all the elements
c^{\prime}
of
C
distinct from the command

(\langle{}\rangle,\underline{\nu},())
must have an
\emptyset

interpretation in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
and hence cannot reduce to a value. In
spite of this strong result, the fact that the rewriting system for
this machine has still to deal with multisets of states means that it
still contains a little bit of non-determinism.

Finally using an idea suggested by Guillaume Geoffroy, we get rid of
this non-determinism by slightly modifying our Krivine machine.
The main change consists in making the access word writable. We can
prove simulation results relating this new machine with the original
one which allow one to prove that the new machine, whilst being fully
deterministic, computes the same thing as the original one, in the
same number of steps.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Notations

Given a set
I
and
i,j\in I
, remember that

\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{i},{j}}\in\{0,1\}
(the Kronecker delta) takes value
1

if
i=j
and
0
otherwise.

We use
{{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
for
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{0\}
.

Given a sequence, or word,
\alpha=\langle{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\rangle
, we set
\mathsf{len}(\alpha)=k
.
We use simple juxtaposition to denote the concatenation of sequences
and also
i\langle{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\rangle=\langle{i,i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\rangle
.
We use
i
for the one-element sequence
\langle{i}\rangle
when there are
no ambiguities.
We use the following notation for circular permutations:

\underrightarrow{\alpha}=\langle{i_{k},i_{1},\dots,i_{k-1}}\rangle
and
\underleftarrow{\alpha}=\langle{i_{2},\dots,i_{k},i_{1}}\rangle
.
Of course if
\mathsf{len}(\alpha)=2
we have
\underrightarrow{\alpha}=\underleftarrow{\alpha}
.
We also use
\overline{\alpha}
for the reversed word (that is, if

\alpha=\langle{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\rangle
then

\overline{\alpha}=\langle{i_{k},\dots,i_{1}}\rangle
).

We use
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({I})
for the set of finite multisets of elements of a set

I
.
A multiset is a function
m:I\to{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\operatorname{\mathsf{supp}}({m})=\{i\in I\mid m(i)\not=0\}
is finite. We use additive
notations for operations on multisets (
0
for the empty multiset,

m+p
for their pointwise sum).
We use
[{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}]
for the multiset
m
such that

m(i)=\#{\{j\in{\mathbb{N}}\mid i_{j}=i\}}
.
If
m=[{j_{1},\dots,j_{n}}]\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({J})
and
i\in I
we set

{i}\ast{m}=[{(i,j_{1}),\dots,(i,j_{n})}]\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({I\times J})
.

We use
I\uplus J
to denote
I\cup J
when
I\cap J=\emptyset
.

Our default notation for composition of morphisms in categories is by
simple juxtaposition, and we use
X
to denote the identity morphism

X\to X
.
In a category-theoretic context, the notation
t:F\Rightarrow G
means
that
t
is a natural transformation from the functor
F
to the
functor
G
.

2.2.
Rewriting

Let
\mathcal{T}=(\underline{\mathcal{T}},\to_{\mathcal{T}})
be a rewriting system, that is

\underline{\mathcal{T}}
is a set and
\mathord{\to_{\mathcal{T}}}\subseteq\underline{\mathcal{T}}^{2}
.
We assume that
\underline{\mathcal{T}}
contains a distinguished element
0
and
that there is a binary operation
+
on
\underline{\mathcal{T}}
:
given
t_{1},t_{2}\in\underline{\mathcal{T}}
there is an element

t_{1}+t_{2}\in\underline{\mathcal{T}}
.
We make no further assumptions, in particular we do not assume that,
equipped with
0
and
+
, the set
\underline{\mathcal{T}}
is a monoid.
We define a rewriting system
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathcal{T}})
by

\underline{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathcal{T}})}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\underline{\mathcal{T}}})
and rewriting relation defined by
the following rules

[{0}]\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathcal{T}})}}[{\,}]

[{t_{1}+t_{2}}]\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathcal{T}})}}[{t_{1},t_{2}}]

t\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{T}}}t^{\prime}
[{t}]\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathcal{T}})}}[{t^{\prime}}]

S\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathcal{T}})}}S^{\prime}
S+T\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathcal{T}})}}S^{\prime}+T

In other words, we have
S\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathcal{T}})}}S^{\prime}
iff one of the
following conditions hold.

•

S=S_{0}+[{0}]
and
S^{\prime}=S_{0}
.

•

S=S_{0}+[{t}]
,
t\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{T}}}t^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime}=S_{0}+[{t^{\prime}}]
.

•

S=S_{0}+[{t_{0}+t_{1}}]
and
S^{\prime}=S_{0}+[{t_{0},t_{1}}]
.

3.
Semantic motivations

Since the main goal of this paper is to shed some light on the
operational content of coherent differentiation, which was introduced
as a categorical refinement of the semantics of
LL

in
[
Ehr23
]
(to which we refer for more details) and
motivated by concrete denotational models, we provide first a bird’s
eye view on this categorical setting.
We give then two concrete instances of this notion: the relational
semantics and the probabilistic coherence space semantics of
LL
.
The more specific semantic operations and properties which are used
for interpreting our extension
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
of
PCF
will be described in
Section
5
.

3.1.
A summary of summable differential categories

A
summable category
is a tuple
(\mathcal{L},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}},\operatorname{\pi}_{0},\operatorname{\pi}_{1},\sigma)
where
\mathcal{L}
is a category with zero morphisms
13
13

13

This means that if
X,Y
are objects then there is a
morphism
0\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
such that
f\,0=0
and

0\,g=0
.
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}:\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}
is a functor and
\operatorname{\pi}_{0},\operatorname{\pi}_{1},\sigma:\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\Rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}
are natural transformations
such that
\operatorname{\pi}_{0},\operatorname{\pi}_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X,X)
are jointly monic: this means
that a morphism
f\in\mathcal{L}(X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}Y)
is fully characterized by
\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\,f
and
\operatorname{\pi}_{1}\,f
. Then we say that
f_{0},f_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
are
summable
if there
is
h\in\mathcal{L}(X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}Y)
such that
\operatorname{\pi}_{i}\,h=f_{i}
for

i=0,1
. This
h
is unique and is denoted
\langle{f_{0},f_{1}}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}}
. In that
situation the sum
f_{0}+f_{1}
of
f_{0},f_{1}
is defined as
f_{0}+f_{1}=\sigma\,\langle{f_{0},f_{1}}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}}
.
There are further axioms (
S-com
), (
S-zero
) and (
S-witness
)
in
[
Ehr23
]
which imply in particular that, equipped with
this partially defined addition, any homset
\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
is a partial
commutative monoid with
0
as neutral element, and the naturality of

\operatorname{\pi}_{0}
,
\operatorname{\pi}_{1}
and
\sigma
implies that composition commutes
with this partially defined addition, that is,
\mathcal{L}
is a “partially
additive” category (
ie.
a category enriched in
partial
commutative monoids
in the sense of
[
PDT10
]
).
These axioms also imply that there is a natural transformation
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}:\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}\Rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}
uniquely characterized by
\operatorname{\pi}_{i}\,\operatorname{\pi}_{j}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}=\operatorname{\pi}_{j}\,\operatorname{\pi}_{i}
for all
i,j\in\{0,1\}
, it is called the (canonical)

flip
.
One also defines uniquely two natural injections
\operatorname{\iota}_{0}=\langle{X,0}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}},\operatorname{\iota}_{1}=\langle{0,X}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}}:X\to\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X
.

3.1.1.
The associated monad, its monoidal strength and its
commutativity

Under these assumptions
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
has a monad structure with unit
\operatorname{\iota}_{0}:\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}\Rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
and multiplication

\tau:\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}\Rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
characterized by
\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\,\tau=\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\,\operatorname{\pi}_{0}
and
\operatorname{\pi}_{1}\,\tau=\operatorname{\pi}_{1}\,\operatorname{\pi}_{0}+\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\,\operatorname{\pi}_{1}
from which it follows easily that
\tau\,\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}=\tau
.
When
\mathcal{L}
is symmetric monoidal (with monoidal unit
1
and
monoidal product
\otimes
) a further axiom (
S
\otimes
-dist
) expresses
in
[
Ehr23
]
that
\otimes
distributes over the sum of
morphisms, when defined. It is then possible to define a tensorial
strength

\operatorname{\varphi}^{0}_{X_{0},X_{1}}\in\mathcal{L}({(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{0})}\otimes{X_{1}},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}({X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}}))
which is a natural
transformation satisfying further commutations expressing its
compatibility with the
\otimes
monoidal structure of
\mathcal{L}
. Using
the symmetry iso of the monoidal structure of
\mathcal{L}
one can define
then

\operatorname{\varphi}^{1}_{X_{0},X_{1}}\in\mathcal{L}({X_{0}}\otimes{(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{1})},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}({X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}}))
from
\operatorname{\varphi}^{0}
.
This strength is fully characterized by
\operatorname{\pi}_{i}\,\operatorname{\varphi}_{X_{0},X_{1}}^{0}={\operatorname{\pi}_{i}}\otimes{X_{1}}
for

i=0,1
. Equipped with this strength the monad

(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}},\operatorname{\iota}_{0},\tau)
is a commutative monad. More precisely the
following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{0}}\otimes{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}({X_{0}}\otimes{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}({X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}({X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\varphi}^{0}_{X_{0},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\varphi}^{1}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{0},X_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\operatorname{\varphi}^{1}_{X_{0},X_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\operatorname{\varphi}^{0}_{X_{0},X_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{{X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}}}}

as indeed
\operatorname{\pi}_{i}\,\operatorname{\pi}_{j}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\operatorname{\varphi}^{1}_{X_{0},X_{1}})\,\operatorname{\varphi}^{0}_{X_{0},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{1}}={\operatorname{\pi}_{j}}\otimes{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}}
and
\operatorname{\pi}_{i}\,\operatorname{\pi}_{j}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\operatorname{\varphi}^{0}_{X_{0},X_{1}})\,\operatorname{\varphi}^{1}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{0},X_{1}}={\operatorname{\pi}_{i}}\otimes{\operatorname{\pi}_{j}}
. The induced natural symmetric lax monoidality morphism
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{X_{0},X_{1}}=\tau\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\operatorname{\varphi}^{0}_{X_{0},X_{1}})\,\operatorname{\varphi}^{1}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{0},X_{1}}=\tau(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\operatorname{\varphi}^{1}_{X_{0},X_{1}})\,\operatorname{\varphi}^{0}_{X_{0},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{1}}\in\mathcal{L}({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{0}}\otimes{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{1}},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}({X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}}))
is fully characterized by

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{X_{0},X_{1}}={\operatorname{\pi}_{0}}\otimes{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}}\text{\quad and\quad}\operatorname{\pi}_{1}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{X_{0},X_{1}}={\operatorname{\pi}_{1}}\otimes{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}}+{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}}\otimes{\operatorname{\pi}_{1}}

and its
0
-ary version is simply
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}^{0}=\operatorname{\iota}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(1,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}1)
.

If the SMC
\mathcal{L}
is also closed then we require in
[
Ehr23
]
to
the summability structure to satisfy a further condition (
S
\otimes
-fun
). We
use
({X}\multimap{Y},\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}\in\mathcal{L}({\left({X}\multimap{Y}\right)}\otimes{X},Y))
for the internal hom object of
X
and
Y
in
\mathcal{L}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}f\in\mathcal{L}(Z,{X}\multimap{Y})
for the curried version of
f\in\mathcal{L}({Z}\otimes{X},Y)
. With these notations, (
S
\otimes
-fun
) says that
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\left({X}\multimap{Y}\right)
and
{X}\multimap{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}Y}
are isomorphic (more precisely the morphism
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\left({X}\multimap{Y}\right)\to\left({X}\multimap{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}Y}\right)
that one can define using
\operatorname{\varphi}^{0}_{{X}\multimap{Y},X}
is an iso).
Intuitively this means that, on morphisms, summability is defined
“pointwise”.

3.2.
Differentiation as a double distributive law on a
resource category

We assume now that
\mathcal{L}
is a resource category which means that
\mathcal{L}

is a symmetric monoidal closed category which is also cartesian (with
cartesian product
(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i},(\mathsf{pr}_{i})_{i\in I})
for any finite family of
objects
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
of
\mathcal{L}
, the case
I=\emptyset
yielding
the terminal object
\top
of
\mathcal{L}
.
Being a resource category means also that
\mathcal{L}
is equipped with a

resource comonad
, that is a tuple

(\oc{\_},\operatorname{\mathsf{der}},\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}},\mathsf{m}^{0},\mathsf{m}^{2})
where
\oc{\_}
is a
functor
\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}
which is a comonad with counit
\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}
(for

dereliction
) and comultiplication
\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}
(for

digging
), and
\mathsf{m}^{0}\in\mathcal{L}(1,\oc{\top})
and

\mathsf{m}^{2}\in\mathcal{L}({\oc{X}}\otimes{\oc{Y}},\oc{({X}\mathrel{\&}{Y})})
are the
Seely isomorphisms subject to conditions that we do not recall here,
see for instance
[
Mel09
]
, apart for the following which
explains how
\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}
interacts with
\mathsf{m}^{2}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oc{X_{0}}}\otimes{\oc{X_{0}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oc\oc{X_{0}}}\otimes{\oc\oc{X_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\left({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc\oc{\left({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\left({\oc{X_{0}}}\mathrel{\&}{\oc{X_{1}}}\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X_{0}}}\otimes{\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}^{2}_{X_{0},X_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}^{2}_{\oc{X_{0}},\oc{X_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{{X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\langle{\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{0}},\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}}\rangle}}

(3)

Then
\oc{\_}
inherits a
lax
symmetric monoidality on the SMC

(\mathcal{L},\mathord{\otimes})
.
This means that one can define
\mu^{0}\in\mathcal{L}(1,\oc{1})
and
\mu^{2}_{X_{0},X_{1}}\in\mathcal{L}({\oc{X_{0}}}\otimes{\oc{X_{1}}},\oc{({X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}})})

satisfying suitable coherence commutations.
Explicitly these morphisms are given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\top}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc\oc{\top}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{\top}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{({\mathsf{m}^{0}})^{-1}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oc{X_{0}}}\otimes{\oc{X_{1}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\left({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc\oc{\left({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}\right)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{({\oc{X_{0}}}\otimes{\oc{X_{1}}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{({{X_{0}}}\otimes{{X_{1}}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{m}^{2}_{X_{0},X_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{{X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{({\mathsf{m}^{2}_{X_{0},X_{1}}})^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{({\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{X_{0}}}\otimes{\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{X_{1}}})}}

And by combining these morphisms in an arbitrary way one can define
uniquely
\mu^{n}_{X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1}}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{X_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\oc{X_{n-1}},\oc({X_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n-1}}))

thanks to the coherence diagrams that we left implicit.

Remember that, using
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
for the Kleisli category of this comonad
where we use the notation
g\mathrel{\circ}f
to denote composition of
morphisms, we have a functor
\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}:\mathcal{L}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
defined by
\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}(X)=X
for objects and, given
f\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
, we set
\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}f=f\,\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{X}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
.
Functoriality results from the fact that
(\oc{\_},\operatorname{\mathsf{der}},\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}})
is
a comonad.
The intuition is that this functor allows one to see morphisms of

\mathcal{L}
(considered as linear) as morphisms in
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}

where morphisms are not linear in general.
This is why this functor is often faithful but of course not full in
general.

The Kleisli category
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
is cartesian with cartesian product of a
family
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
of objects of
\mathcal{L}
given by
(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i},(\mathsf{pr}^{\mathsf{K}}_{i}=\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}(\mathsf{pr}_{i}))_{i\in I})
. Given a
family of morphisms
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(Y,X_{i})
for
i\in I
, the morphism
\langle{f_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{Y},\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i})={\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(Y,\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i})
is uniquely characterized by the fact
that
\mathsf{pr}^{\mathsf{K}}_{j}\mathrel{\circ}\langle{f_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}=f_{j}
for each
j\in I
.

Notice that if now
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X_{i},Y_{i})
for each
i\in I
we can define functorially
\mathop{\&^{\mathsf{K}}}_{i\in I}f_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i},\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}Y_{i})

by

\displaystyle\mathop{\&^{\mathsf{K}}}_{i\in I}f_{i}=\langle{f_{i}\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{pr}^{\mathsf{K}}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}=\langle{f_{i}\,\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{i}}}\rangle_{i\in I}=(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}f_{i})\,\langle{\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{i}}}\rangle_{i\in I}\,.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
and
g\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(Y,Z)
, we have
g\mathrel{\circ}\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}(f)=g\,\oc{f}
.

Proof 3.2
.

We have
\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{X}}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{\oc{X}}
.

Given
f\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{X},Y)
we set
f^{!}=\oc{f}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{X},\oc{Y})
which is
sometimes called the
\oc
-lifting or the
promotion
of

f
.
Given
f\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{X_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\oc{X_{n-1}},Y)
one can define more generally
f^{!}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{X_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\oc{X_{n-1}},\oc{Y})
using
\mu^{n}_{X_{0},\dots,X_{n-1}}
.
Notice also that if
f\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
one has
\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}(f)^{!}=\oc{f}
.

We assume furthermore that
\mathcal{L}
is equipped with a summability
structure satisfying the axioms summarized in
Section
3.1
(we use the same notations) and we
also assume that the summability functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
preserves cartesian
products and to simplify notations we assume that it preserves them
strictly, that is

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i})=\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{i}\text{\quad and \quad}\forall i\in I\ \operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\mathsf{pr}_{i}=\mathsf{pr}_{i}\,.

(4)

A
coherent differential structure
on such a summable resource
category consists of a natural transformation
\operatorname{\partial}_{X}\in\mathcal{L}(\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\oc{X})
satisfying a few
axioms that we recall here.

(
\partial
-local
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\partial}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\pi}_{0}}

(

)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{X}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\partial}_{X}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}X}}

That is,
\operatorname{\partial}
is a distributive law between the monad
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
and

\oc{\_}
.
This means essentially that derivatives commute with sums and with

0

This allows one to extend the comonad
\oc{\_}

\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
which is again a resource category.
It can be understood as an

\mathcal{L}
;
this construction, as well as its syntactical outcomes, will be
studied in further work.

(

)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\partial}_{\oc{X}}}

That is,
\operatorname{\partial}
is a distributive law between the comonad
\oc{\_}

and the functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
.
This allows one to extend
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
to an endofunctor on

.

(

-

)

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{\oc{X_{0}},\oc{X_{1}}}}

Notice that our assumption that
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
preserves
\mathrel{\&}
strictly (in
the sense of (
4
)) means that the
morphism
\langle{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}{\mathsf{pr}_{0}},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}}\rangle
is the identity.

Intuitively, this diagram expresses that a derivative wrt. a pair of
variables (
\operatorname{\partial}_{{X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}}
) can be expressed as a sum of
partial derivatives (
\operatorname{\partial}_{X_{0}}
and
\operatorname{\partial}_{X_{1}}
):
this is deeply related to the Leibniz Rule.

(
\partial
-Schwarz
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}\oc{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}^{2}\oc{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\partial}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\operatorname{\partial}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\partial}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\operatorname{\partial}_{X}}

This expresses that the second derivative is a symmetric bilinear function.

When a resource category
\mathcal{L}
is equipped with a summability
structure
(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}},\operatorname{\pi}_{0},\operatorname{\pi}_{1},\sigma)
and a natural
transformation
\operatorname{\partial}
satisfying these axioms, it is called a

differential summable resource category
.

3.2.1.
The induced coherent differentiation monad

Thanks to
3.2
we extend the functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
to a functor
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}:{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}\to{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
on the Kleisli category of the comonad

\oc{\_}
as follows:

\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X=\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X
and, if
f\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
then
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}f=(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}f)\,\operatorname{\partial}_{X}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}Y)
.
Then we can define
\zeta_{X}=\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}{\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X)
and

\theta_{X}=\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}{\tau}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}^{2}X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X)
and the
condition
3.2
entails that these morphisms are natural;
the intuitive meaning of that condition is that the differential of a
map of the Kleisli category is linear in the sense that it commutes
with the algebraic operation represented by
\zeta
and

\theta
.
These natural transformations are easily seen to equip
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
with a
monad structure.

3.2.2.
The elementary situation

In the models of coherent differentiation we know for the time being,
the summability and differential structures arise from more basic
properties
14
14

14

And not from an additional
structure
:
it is interesting to observe that the summability structure we are
describing in this section, when it exists, is completely
characterized by pre-existing structures of the category
\mathcal{L}
, so
having such a summability structure is indeed a
property
of

\mathcal{L}
.
of one specific object of a resource category with zero-morphisms

\mathcal{L}
, namely

\mathbb{D}={1}\mathrel{\&}{1}\,.

Notice first that it is always possible to define

\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with},\overline{\pi}_{1}^{\with},\Delta^{\with}\in\mathcal{L}(1,\mathbb{D})
by

\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with}=\langle{1,0}\rangle
,
\overline{\pi}_{1}^{\with}=\langle{0,1}\rangle
and

\Delta^{\with}=\langle{1,1}\rangle
.
We say that
\mathcal{L}
is
elementarily summable
if the two
following properties hold.
Our first assumption is that
\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with},\overline{\pi}_{1}^{\with}
are jointly epic,
meaning that morphisms
f\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{D},X)
are fully characterized by

f\,\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with},f\,\overline{\pi}_{1}^{\with}\in\mathcal{L}(1,X)
.
Our second assumption, corresponding to (
S-witness
) of
[
Ehr23
]
,
is that if
f_{0},f_{1},g\in\mathcal{L}({X}\otimes{\mathbb{D}},Y)
are such that the
following diagrams commute

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}\otimes{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}\otimes{\Delta^{\with}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}\otimes{\overline{\pi}_{r}^{\with}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

for
r=0,1
then there is a (necessarily unique by our first
assumption) morphism
h\in\mathcal{L}({{X}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}\otimes{\mathbb{D}},Y)
such that
the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{X}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}\otimes{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{X}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{X}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}\otimes{\overline{\pi}_{r}^{\with}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{r}}

for
r=0,1
.
Under these assumptions, the functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}=({\mathbb{D}}\multimap{\_})
,
equipped with natural transformations

\operatorname{\pi}_{0},\operatorname{\pi}_{1},\sigma:\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\Rightarrow\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}
easily defined by
precomposition with
\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with},\overline{\pi}_{1}^{\with},\Delta^{\with}
, is a summability structure
on
\mathcal{L}
which is called
the elementary summability structure

of
\mathcal{L}
.

It is also possible to equip
\mathbb{D}
with a structure of
cocommutative comonoid, with counit
\mathsf{pr}_{0}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{D},1)
and
comultiplication
\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{D},{\mathbb{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{D}})
fully
characterized by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1}\otimes{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}^{-1}={\rho}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with}}\otimes{\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{1}\otimes{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\pi}_{1}^{\with}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lambda}^{-1}={\rho}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with}}\otimes{\overline{\pi}_{1}^{\with}}+{\overline{\pi}_{1}^{\with}}\otimes{\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with}}}

where the sum is defined in terms of the elementary summability
structure, and exists by our assumptions.

It is proven in
[
Ehr23
]
that endowing this elementary
summability structure
(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}},\operatorname{\pi}_{0},\operatorname{\pi}_{1},\sigma)
with a
coherent differentiation structure amounts to equipping
\mathbb{D}
with
a
\oc
-coalgebra structure
\tilde{\partial}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathbb{D},\oc{\mathbb{D}})

which satisfies also some compatibility with the comonoid structure

(\mathsf{pr}_{0},\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}})
of
\mathbb{D}
.
We don’t need here to go into these technicalities, we only need to
know that, when
\mathcal{L}
is a Lafont
category
[
Mel09
,
Ehr23
]
, such a
\tilde{\partial}
always
exists and is induced by the comonoid structure of
\mathbb{D}
:
remember indeed that a resource category is Lafont if, roughly
speaking, any cocommutative comonoid is a
\oc
-coalgebra, in a
unique way. See
[
Ehr23
]
,
Theorem 21 for more details.

So, for a Lafont resource category with zero-morphisms, being an
elementary coherent differential category is a property, not an
additional structure: it simply means that it satisfies the two
conditions of elementary summability.

3.2.3.
Scott
summable categories

One distinctive feature of the present approach to differentiation is
its built-in compatibility with general recursion at all types.
In the models which motivated this work, general recursion is
implemented by means of fixpoint operators which arise from a cpo
structure of homsets as usual in domain-theoretic models of

PCF

[
Plo77
]
.
We explain how the associated order relation is induced by the
summability structure.

Let
(\mathcal{L},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}})
be a summable category.
Let
f_{0},f_{1}\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
, we write
f_{0}\leq f_{1}
if there exists

h\in\mathcal{L}(X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}Y)
such that
\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\,=f_{0}
and

\sigma\,h=f_{1}
.
In other words:
there is
g\in\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
such that
f_{0},g
are summable and
f_{1}=f_{0}+g
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The relation
\leq
on
\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
is a preorder relation for which

0
is the least element.

Definition 3.4
.

The summable category
(\mathcal{L},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}})
is
Scott
if, equipped
with
\leq
, any homset
\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
is a poset (with
0
as least
element as we have seen), which is
\omega
-complete is the sense
that any monotone
\omega
-sequence of elements of
\mathcal{L}(X,Y)
has a
least upper bound
15
15

15

On purpose we do not ask all directed sets to have a lub
as it is usual in domain theory because we have in mind
probability based models where such a requirement would prevent us
from applying the monotone convergence theorem.
.
Morphism composition is required to be continuous.
This means in particular that
\mathcal{L}
is enriched in
\omega
-cpos.
Moreover the
\otimes
tensor is assumed to be locally continuous (in
both arguments) when
\mathcal{L}
is an SMC.
Next, the functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}
must be locally continuous, and, in the
case where
\mathcal{L}
is a resource category, the functor
\oc{\_}
is
also assumed to be locally continuous.

Assume that
\mathcal{L}
is a Scott summable resource category.
In the CCC
{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}
, for any object
X
, we can define a sequence of
morphisms
\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{X}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}({X}\Rightarrow{X},X)
by induction as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{X}_{0}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{X}_{n+1}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}\mathrel{\circ}\langle{{X}\Rightarrow{X},\mathcal{Y}^{X}_{n}}\rangle

and an easy induction, using the minimality of
0
and the fact that
all categorical operations are monotone wrt.
\leq
, shows that the
sequence
(\mathcal{Y}^{X}_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is monotone.
We set

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{X}=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathcal{Y}^{X}_{n}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}({X}\Rightarrow{X},X)

and by continuity of all categorical operations we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}^{X}=\operatorname{\mathsf{Ev}}\mathrel{\circ}\langle{{X}\Rightarrow{X},\mathcal{Y}^{X}}\rangle

(5)

which means that
\mathcal{Y}^{X}
is a fixpoint operator.

We will illustrate now these general definitions in two concrete
models of
LL
which will also be essential in the technical
developments of this paper.

3.3.
The relational model

The simplest example of a resource category is the category
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
of
sets and relations which is a well known model of classical
LL
.
In
[
Ehr23
]
it is proven that
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is also an
elementary differential summable resource category;
actually we proved this result for the category
\mathbf{nCoh}
of non-uniform
coherence spaces, but in that category the operations on morphisms are
exactly the same as in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
and the operations on objects are the
same as in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
as far as the webs are concerned
16
16

16

A non-uniform coherence space
X
is a triple consisting of
a set
|{X}|
, the web of
X
, and two disjoint binary symmetric
relations on that web, the strict coherence and the strict
incoherence relations.
Remember that
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is a model of
differential
LL

— which is not the case of
\mathbf{nCoh}
—, so it is not surprising
at all that it is also a coherent differential category.
.

Let us briefly recall the definition of this model of
LL
. An object
of
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is a set and
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X,Y)={\mathcal{P}}\left({X\times Y}\right)
, composition being
the usual composition of relations denoted
v\,u
when

u\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X,Y)
and
v\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(Y,Z)
.
The identity morphism is the diagonal relation.
The isos in this category are the bijections.

This category
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is symmetric monoidal with

1=\{\ast\}
as tensorial unit and

{X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}}=X_{0}\times X_{1}
, and given
u_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X_{i},Y_{i})
for

i=0,1
, one sets
{u_{0}}\otimes{u_{1}}=\{((a_{0},a_{1}),(b_{0},b_{1}))\mid(a_{i},b_{i})\in u_{i}\text{
for }i=0,1)\}
defining a functor
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}^{2}\to\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
which has obvious
natural isos
\lambda_{X}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}({1}\otimes{X},X)
,
\rho_{X}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}({X}\otimes{1},X)
,
\alpha_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}({({X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}})}\otimes{X_{2}},{X_{0}}\otimes{({X_{1}}\otimes{X_{2}})})

and
\gamma_{X_{0},X_{1}}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}({X_{0}}\otimes{X_{1}},{X_{1}}\otimes{X_{0}})
.
This SMC is closed with internal hom from
X
to
Y
the pair
({X}\multimap{Y},\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}})
where
{X}\multimap{Y}=X\times Y
and

\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}=\{(((a,b),a),b)\mid a\in X\text{ and }b\in Y\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}({\left({X}\multimap{Y}\right)}\otimes{X},Y)
. Given any morphism
u\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}({Z}\otimes{X},Y)
, the associated morphism (Curry transpose of

u
)
\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}u\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(Z,{X}\multimap{Y})
is simply
\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}u=\{(a,(b,c))\mid((a,b),c)\in u\}
.
This SMCC is
\ast
-autonomous with dualizing object
{\mathord{\perp}}=1
, so that
the “linear negation” of an object
X
is simply
X
.

The category
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is cartesian: the cartesian product of a family

(X_{i})_{i\in I}
of objects of
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is
(\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i},(\mathsf{pr}_{i})_{i\in I})
where
\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i}=\bigcup_{i\in I}\{i\}\times X_{i}
and
\mathsf{pr}_{i}=\{((i,a),a)\mid i\in I\text{ and }a\in X_{i}\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\mathop{\&}_{j\in I}X_{j},X_{i})
is the
i
th projection.
The tupling of a family of morphisms
(u_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(Y,X_{i}))_{i\in I}
is the morphism
\langle{u_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(Y,\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i})
given by
\langle{u_{i}}\rangle_{i\in I}=\{(b,(i,a))\mid i\in I\text{ and }(b,a)\in u_{i}\}
.
The coproduct
(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}X_{i},(\overline{\pi}_{i})_{i\in I})
also exists and is given by
\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}X_{i}=\mathop{\&}_{i\in I}X_{i}
and
\overline{\pi}_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X_{i},\mathop{\oplus}_{j\in I}X_{j})
is given by
\overline{\pi}_{i}=\{(a,(i,a))\mid i\in I\text{ and }a\in X_{i}\}
;
it is the
i
th injection.
The cotupling of morphisms
(u_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X_{i},Y))_{i\in I}
is
\left[{u_{i}}\right]_{i\in I}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\in I}X_{i},Y)
given by
\left[{u_{i}}\right]_{i\in I}=\{((i,a),b)\mid(a,b)\in u_{i}\}
.
Notice that the terminal (and initial) object of
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is

\top=0=\emptyset
.

The SMC
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is a Lafont category
[
Mel09
,
Ehr23
]
. The
exponential functor is given by
\oc{X}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X})
and, if

s\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X,Y)
then
\oc{s}=\{([{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}],[{b_{1},\dots,b_{k}}])\mid k\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{
and }\forall i\ (a_{i},b_{i})\in s)\}
, defining a functor
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}\to\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
.
The comonad structure of that functor is given by the natural
transformations
\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{X}=\{([{a}],a)\mid a\in X\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\oc{X},X)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}=\{([{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}],m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n})\mid n\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{
and }m_{1},\dots,m_{n}\in\oc{X}\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\oc{X},\oc\oc{X})
.
The Seely isomorphisms are
\mathsf{m}^{0}=\{(\ast,[\,])\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(1,\oc{\top})
and

\mathsf{m}^{2}_{X,Y}=\{(([{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}],[{b_{1},\dots,b_{k}}]),[{(1,a_{1}),\dots,(1,a_{n})}],(2,b_{1}),\dots,(2,b_{k}))\mid\\
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in X\text{ and }b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\in Y\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}({\oc{X}}\otimes{\oc{Y}},\oc({{X}\mathrel{\&}{Y}}))\,.

The Kleisli category
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}
can be
directly described as
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}(X,Y)=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X})\times Y
, with identity
\operatorname{\mathsf{Id}}_{X}=\{([{a}],a)\mid a\in X\}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}(X,X)
and, given
u\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
and
v\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}(Y,Z)
, composition given by

v\mathrel{\circ}u=\{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k},c)\mid k\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{ and }\exists b_{1},\dots,b_{k}\in Y\ ([{b_{1},\dots,b_{k}}],c)\in v\text{ and }(m_{i},b_{i})\in u\text{ for }i=1,\dots,k\}
.

It is easy to see that
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is elementarily summable (see
Section
3.2.2
) and has therefore exactly
one coherent differential structure.
The cocommutative comonoid structure of

\mathbb{D}={1}\mathrel{\&}{1}=\{0,1\}
has counit
\mathsf{pr}_{0}=\{(0,\ast)\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\mathbb{D},1)
and comultiplication

\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}}=\{(0,(0,0)),(1,(1,0)),(1,(0,1))\}\\
=\{(r,(r_{0},r_{1}))\mid r,r_{0},r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }r=r_{0}+r_{1})\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\mathbb{D},{\mathbb{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{D}})

as explained in Section 5.1 of
[
Ehr23
]
which, by the Lafont property, induces on
\mathbb{D}
the
\oc
-coalgebra
structure
\tilde{\partial}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\mathbb{D},\oc{\mathbb{D}})
given by

\tilde{\partial}=\{(0,k[{0}])\mid k\in{\mathbb{N}}\}\cup\{(1,[{1}]+k[{0}])\mid k\in{\mathbb{N}}\}\\
=\{(r,[{r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}])\in\mathbb{D}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathbb{D}})\mid r=\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}\}\,.

As explained in Section
3.2.2
, in this
elementary setting, the associated summability functor is

\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}=({\mathbb{D}}\multimap{\_})\,,

and is therefore given by
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X=\{0,1\}\times X
and, for
u\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X,Y)
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}u=\{((i,a),(i,b))\mid i\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }(a,b)\in u\}
.
The associated natural transformations are
\operatorname{\pi}_{i}=\{((i,a),a)\mid a\in X\},\sigma=\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\cup\operatorname{\pi}_{1}=\{((i,a),a)\mid i\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }a\in X\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X,X)
and the two injections are
\operatorname{\iota}_{i}=\{(a,(i,a))\mid a\in X\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X)
for
i=0,1
. So any two morphisms
f_{0},f_{1}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X,Y)
are summable, and their
sum is
f_{0}\cup f_{1}
.

The monad structure of
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}
has

\operatorname{\iota}_{0}=\{(a,(0,a))\mid a\in X\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X)
as unit and

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\{((0,0,a),(0,a))\mid a\in X\}\cup\{((1,0,a),(1,a))\mid a\in X\}\cup\{((0,1,a),(1,a))\mid a\in X\}

\displaystyle=\{((r_{0},r_{1},a),(r,a))\mid a\in X,\ r,r_{0},r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }r=r_{0}+r_{1}\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}^{2}X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X)

as multiplication. The flip is
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}=\{((r_{0},r_{1},a),(r_{1},r_{0},a))\mid r_{0},r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }a\in X\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}^{2}X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}^{2}X)
.

Notice that there is a natural bijection between
\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X})\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X})
, mapping the multiset
[{(0,a_{1}),\dots,(0,a_{l}),(1,b_{1}),\dots,(1,b_{r})}]
to
([{a_{1},\dots,a_{l}}],[{b_{1},\dots,b_{r}}])
.
The natural transformation
\operatorname{\partial}_{X}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}{\oc{X}})
is defined as the
Curry transpose of the following composition of morphisms

(see
[
Ehr23
]
,
Theorem 20 and hence

\operatorname{\partial}_{X}=\{((m,[\,]),(0,m))\mid m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X})\}\\
\cup\{((m,[{a}]),(1,m+[{a}]))\mid m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X})\text{ and }a\in X\}\,.

It will also be convenient to write equivalently

\operatorname{\partial}_{X}=\{(m^{\prime},(r,m))\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathbb{D}\times X})\times(\mathbb{D}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X}))\mid\\
m=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}],\ m^{\prime}=[{(r_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(r_{k},a_{k})}]\text{ and }r=r_{1}+\cdots+r_{k}\}\,.

Therefore the functor
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}:{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}\to{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}
, which is defined
on objects by
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X=\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X
and on morphisms by
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}u=(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}u)\,\operatorname{\partial}_{X}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}Y)

for
u\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
satisfies

\displaystyle\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}u

\displaystyle=\{((m,[\,]),(0,b))\mid(m,b)\in u\}\cup\{((m,[{a}]),(1,b))\mid(m+[{a}],b)\in u\}

\displaystyle=\{(m^{\prime},(r,b))\mid m^{\prime}=[{(r_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(r_{k},a_{k})}]\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\mathbb{D}\times X}),

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\hskip 60.00009pt([{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}],b)\in u\text{ and }r=r_{1}+\cdots+r_{k}\in\mathbb{D}\}\,.

Then the monad structure of
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}
is extended to
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}

by
\zeta_{X}=\{([{a}],(0,a))\mid a\in X\}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}(X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X)
and

\displaystyle\theta_{X}

\displaystyle=\{([{(0,0,a)}],(0,a))\mid a\in X\}

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\cup\{([{(1,0,a)}],(1,a))\mid a\in X\}\cup\{([{(0,1,a)}],(1,a))\mid a\in X\}

\displaystyle=\{([{(r_{0},r_{1},a)}],(r,a))\mid a\in X,\ r,r_{0},r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }r=r_{0}+r_{1}\}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}^{2}X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X)\,.

Concerning fixpoints, observe that
(\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}})
is Scott (in
the sense of Section
3.2.3
) with
\subseteq
as
associated order relation on morphisms. One checks easily that the
(\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{X}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}({X}\Rightarrow{X},X))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
are given by
\mathcal{Y}_{0}^{X}=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{Y}_{n+1}^{X}=\{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k}+[{([{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}],a)}],a)\mid k\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{ and }(m_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(m_{k},a_{k})\in\mathcal{Y}_{n}^{X}\}
so that
\mathcal{Y}^{X}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}({X}\Rightarrow{X},X)
is the least set such that for all
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
(m_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(m_{k},a_{k})\in\mathcal{Y}^{X}
and
a\in X
, one has
(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k}+[{([{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}],a)}],a)\in\mathcal{Y}^{X}
.

3.4.
Probabilistic coherence spaces as an elementary coherent
differential category

We present now the model which motivated this whole investigation, the
model of probabilistic coherence spaces (PCS
[
DE11
]
),
and explain why it is an elementary model of coherent differentiation.
One can see
\mathbf{Pcoh}
as obtained by adding to the purely combinatorial
skeleton
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
some “numerical flesh” whose purpose is to
describe probabilities of computational events.

Given an at most countable set
A
and
u,u^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{A}
, we set

\langle u\mid u^{\prime}\rangle=\sum_{a\in A}u_{a}u^{\prime}_{a}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
where
\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}
is the
completed half real line. Given
P\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{A}
, we define

P^{\mathord{\perp}}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{A}
as

\displaystyle P^{\mathord{\perp}}=\{u^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{A}\mid\forall u\in P\ \langle u\mid u^{\prime}\rangle\leq 1\}\,.

Observe that if
P
satisfies

\forall a\in A\,\exists x\in P\ x_{a}>0
and

\forall a\in A\,\exists m\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\forall x\in P\ x_{a}\leq m

then
P^{\mathord{\perp}}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{I}
and
P^{\mathord{\perp}}
satisfies the same two
properties that we call
local boundedness
which can also be
rephrased as

\displaystyle\forall a\in A\quad 0<\sup_{x\in P}x_{a}<\infty\,.

A probabilistic pre-coherence space (pre-PCS) is a pair

X=(|{X}|,\mathsf{P}{X})
where
|{X}|
is an at most countable
set
17
17

17

This restriction is not technically necessary, but very
meaningful from a philosophic point of view; the non countable case
should be handled via measurable spaces and then one has to consider
more general objects as in
[
EPT18b
]
for
instance.
and
\mathsf{P}{X}\subseteq\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{|{X}|}
satisfies

\mathsf{P}{X}^{\bot\bot}=\mathsf{P}{X}
. A probabilistic coherence space (PCS) is a
pre-PCS
X
such that
\mathsf{P}{X}
is locally bounded.

Given a PCS
X
and
x\in\mathsf{P}{X}
we set

\|{x}\|_{X}=\sup_{x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{X^{\mathord{\perp}}}}\langle x\mid x^{\prime}\rangle\in[0,1]
. This
operation obeys the usual properties of a norm:
\|{x}\|=0\Rightarrow x=0
,
\|{x_{0}+x_{1}}\|\leq\|{x_{0}}\|+\|{x_{1}}\|
and
\|{\lambda x}\|=\lambda\|{x}\|
for all
\lambda\in[0,1]
.

Remark 3.5
.

Given
x\in\mathsf{P}{X}
and
a\in|{X}|
we use the notations

x_{a}
or
x(a)
for the corresponding element of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
depending on the context. In some situations
x_{i}
can denote an
element of
\mathsf{P}{X}
and in such a situation we will prefer the
notation
x_{i}(a)
to denote the
a
-component of
x_{i}
to
avoid the ugly
{x_{i}}_{a}
.

Given
t\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{A\times B}
considered as a matrix (where
A
and

B
are at most countable sets) and
u\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{A}
, we define

{t}\cdot{u}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{B}
by

({t}\cdot{u})_{b}=\sum_{a\in A}t_{a,b}u_{a}
(usual formula for applying
a matrix to a vector), and if
s\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{B\times C}
we define the
product
18
18

18

We write this product in the reverse order wrt. the usual
algebraic conventions on matrices, because it is the notion of
composition in our category and we respect the standard order of
factors when writing a composition in a category.
This is a well known and unfortunate mismatch of conventions.

{s}\,{t}\in\overline{\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}}^{A\times C}
of the matrix
s
and
t
as usual
by
({s}\,{t})_{a,c}=\sum_{b\in B}t_{a,b}s_{b,c}
. This is an
associative operation.

Let
X
and
Y
be PCSs, a morphism from
X
to
Y
is a matrix

t\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{X}|\times|{Y}|}
such that

\forall x\in\mathsf{P}{X}\ {t}\cdot{x}\in\mathsf{P}{Y}
. It is clear that the
identity (diagonal) matrix is a morphism from
X
to
X
and that the matrix
product of two morphisms is a morphism and therefore, PCSs equipped
with this notion of morphism form a category
\mathbf{Pcoh}
.

The condition
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
is equivalent to

\forall x\in\mathsf{P}{X}\,\forall y^{\prime}\in\mathsf{P}{Y^{\mathord{\perp}}}\ \langle{t}\cdot{x}\mid y^{\prime}\rangle\leq 1

and observe that
\langle{t}\cdot{x}\mid y^{\prime}\rangle=\langle t\mid{x}\otimes{y^{\prime}}\rangle
where

({x}\otimes{y^{\prime}})_{(a,b)}=x_{a}y^{\prime}_{b}
. We define

{X}\multimap{Y}=(|{X}|\times|{Y}|,\{t\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{{X}\multimap{Y}}|}\mid\forall x\in\mathsf{P}{X}\ {t}\cdot{x}\in\mathsf{P}{Y}\})
