: this is a
pre-PCS by this observation, and checking that it is indeed a PCS is
easy.

We define then
{X}\otimes{Y}=\left({X}\multimap{Y^{\mathord{\perp}}}\right)^{\mathord{\perp}}
; this is a PCS which satisfies

\mathsf{P}{({X}\otimes{Z})}=\{{x}\otimes{z}\mid x\in\mathsf{P}{X}\text{ and }z\in\mathsf{P}{Z}\}^{\bot\bot}

where
({x}\otimes{z})_{(a,c)}=x_{a}z_{c}
.
Then it is easy to see that we have equipped in that way the category

\mathbf{Pcoh}
with a symmetric monoidal structure for which it is

\ast
-autonomous with the dualizing object

{\mathord{\perp}}=1=(\{*\},[0,1])
, which coincides with the unit of
\otimes
.
The
\ast
-autonomy follows easily from the observation that

({X}\multimap{{\mathord{\perp}}})\simeq X^{\mathord{\perp}}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Given
s,t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{k},Y)
, if for all
(x_{i}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{i}})_{i=1}^{k}
one has
{s}\cdot{(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})}={t}\cdot{(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k})}

then
s=t
.

The category
\mathbf{Pcoh}
is cartesian: if
(X_{j})_{j\in J}
is an at most
countable family of PCSs, then

(\mathop{\&}_{j\in J}X_{j},(\mathsf{pr}_{j})_{j\in J})
is the cartesian product of
the
X_{j}
’s, with

|{\mathop{\&}_{j\in J}X_{j}}|=\mathop{\cup}_{j\in J}\{j\}\times|{X_{j}}|
,

(\mathsf{pr}_{j})_{(k,a),a^{\prime}}=1
if
j=k
and
a=a^{\prime}
and

(\mathsf{pr}_{j})_{(k,a),a^{\prime}}=0
otherwise, and

x\in\mathsf{P}{(\mathop{\&}_{j\in J}X_{j})}
if
{\mathsf{pr}_{j}}\cdot{x}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{j}}

for each
j\in J
(for
x\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\mathop{\&}_{j\in J}X_{j}}|}
). Given
(t_{j}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(Y,X_{j}))_{j\in J}
, the unique
morphism
t=\langle{t_{j}}\rangle_{j\in J}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(Y,\mathop{\&}_{j\in J}X_{j})
such
that
\mathsf{pr}_{j}\,t=t_{j}
is simply defined by

t_{b,(j,a)}=(t_{j})_{a,b}
. The dual operation
\mathop{\oplus}_{j\in J}X_{j}
,
which is a coproduct, is characterized by

|{\mathop{\oplus}_{j\in J}X_{j}}|=\mathop{\cup}_{j\in J}\{j\}\times|{X_{j}}|
and

x\in\mathsf{P}{(\mathop{\oplus}_{j\in J}X_{j})}
if
x\in\mathsf{P}{(\mathop{\&}_{j\in J}X_{j})}

and
\sum_{j\in J}\|{{\mathsf{pr}_{j}}\cdot{x}}\|_{X_{j}}\leq 1
.

A particular case is
\mathsf{N}=\mathop{\oplus}_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{\nu}
where

X_{\nu}=1
for each
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
. So that
|{\mathsf{N}}|={\mathbb{N}}
and

x\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{{\mathbb{N}}}
belongs to
\mathsf{P}{\mathsf{N}}
if

\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}}x_{\nu}\leq 1
(that is,
x
is a sub-probability
distribution on
{\mathbb{N}}
). For each
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
we have

\mathsf{e}_{\nu}\in\mathsf{P}{\mathsf{N}}
which is the distribution concentrated on the
integer
\nu
. There are successor and predecessor morphisms

\overline{\mathsf{suc}},\overline{\mathsf{pred}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{N})
given by

\overline{\mathsf{suc}}_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{\nu+1},{\nu^{\prime}}}
(see Section
2.1
) and
\overline{\mathsf{pred}}_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}=1
if
\nu=\nu^{\prime}=0
or
\nu=\nu^{\prime}+1
(and

\overline{\mathsf{pred}}_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}=0
in all other cases). An element of

\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{N})
is a (sub)stochastic matrix and the very idea of
this model is to represent programs as transformations of this kind,
and their generalizations.

As to the exponentials, one sets
|{\oc{X}}|=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|})
and

\mathsf{P}{(\oc{X})}=\{x^{!}\mid x\in\mathsf{P}{X}\}^{\bot\bot}
where, given

m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|})
,
x^{!}_{m}=x^{m}=\prod_{a\in|{X}|}x_{a}^{m(a)}
. A
morphism

t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X},Y)=\mathsf{P}{({\oc{X}}\multimap{Y})}
is
completely characterized by the associated analytic function

\displaystyle\widehat{t}:\mathsf{P}{X}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{P}{Y}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto{t}\cdot{x^{!}}=\sum_{m\in|{\oc{X}}|,b\in|{Y}|}t_{m,b}x^{m}\,\mathsf{e}_{b}\,.

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
t\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{{\oc{X_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\oc{X_{k}}}\multimap{Y}}|}
.
One has
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\oc{X_{k}},Y)
iff for all
(x_{i}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{i}})_{i=1}^{k}
one has
{t}\cdot{(x_{1}^{!}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}^{!})}\in\mathsf{P}{Y}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

If
s,t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\oc{X_{k}},Y)

satisfy
{s}\cdot{(x_{1}^{!}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}^{!})}={t}\cdot{(x_{1}^{!}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{k}^{!})}
for all
(x_{i}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{i}})_{i=1}^{k}
then
s=t
.

This very useful property uses crucially the local boundedness
property of PCSs.

Then given
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
, we explain now how to define

\oc{t}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X},\oc{Y})
.
Let
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|})
and
p\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{Y}|})
.
We use
\mathsf{L}(m,p)
for the set of all

r\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|\times|{Y}|})
such that

\displaystyle\forall a\in|{X}|\ m(a)=\sum_{b\in|{Y}|}r(a,b)\text{\quad and\quad}\forall b\in|{Y}|\ p(b)=\sum_{a\in|{X}|}r(a,b)\,.

Notice that if
r\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({|{X}|\times|{Y}|})
then
\#r=\#m=\#p
so that
\mathsf{L}(m,p)
is non-empty
iff
\#m=\#p
.
When
r\in\mathsf{L}(n,p)
we set

\displaystyle\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{p}{r}=\prod_{b\in|{Y}|}\frac{{p(b)}!}{\prod_{a\in|{X}|}{r(a,b)}!}

which belongs to
{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{0\}
; this is a generalized
multinomial coefficient.
Then we have

\displaystyle(\oc{t})_{m,p}=\sum_{r\in\mathsf{L}(m,p)}\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{p}{r}t^{r}

where we recall that
t^{r}=\prod_{(a,b)\in|{X}|\times|{Y}|}t_{a,b}^{r(a,b)}
.
The main feature of this definition is that for all
x\in\mathsf{P}{X}
one
has
\widehat{\oc{t}}(x)={\oc{t}}\cdot{x^{!}}=({t}\cdot{x})^{!}
. This property fully characterizes
\oc{t}
. The comonad structure is given by
\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{X}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{{\oc{X}}\multimap{X}}|}
given by
(\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{X})_{m,a}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{m},{[{a}]}}
so that
\forall x\in\mathsf{P}{X}\ {\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{X}}\cdot{x^{!}}=x\in\mathsf{P}{X}
and
therefore
\operatorname{\mathsf{der}}_{X}\in\mathsf{P}{(}\oc{X},X)
.
Similarly one defines
\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{{\oc{X}}\multimap{\oc\oc{X}}}|}
by
(\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X})_{(m,[{m_{1},\dots,m_{n}}])}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{m},{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{n}}}
so
that
\forall x\in\mathsf{P}{X}\ {\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}}\cdot{x^{!}}={x}^{!!}
and
hence, again,
\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}\in\mathsf{P}{(}\oc{X},\oc\oc{X})
.
The equations required to prove that
(\oc{\_},\operatorname{\mathsf{der}},\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}})
is
indeed a comonad result from Lemma
3.8
.
For instance, let
t\in\mathsf{P}{(}X,Y)
, we have
{(\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{Y}\,\oc{t})}\cdot{x^{!}}={\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{Y}}\cdot{({t}\cdot{x}^{!})}={\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}}\cdot{({t}\cdot{x})^{!}}={({t}\cdot{x})}^{!!}
and
{(\oc\oc{t}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X})}\cdot{x^{!}}={\oc\oc{t}}\cdot{({\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}}\cdot{x^{!}})}={\oc\oc{t}}\cdot{{x}^{!!}}=({\oc{t}}\cdot{x^{!}})^{!}={({t}\cdot{x})}^{!!}
which
shows that
\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}
is a natural transformation.
As another example, we have
{(\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{\oc{X}}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X})}\cdot{x^{!}}={\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{\oc{X}}}\cdot{{x}^{!!}}={x}^{!!!}
and
{(\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X})}\cdot{x^{!}}={\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}}}\cdot{{x}^{!!}}=({\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}}\cdot{x^{!}})^{!}=({x}^{!!})^{!}={x}^{!!!}
and hence
\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{\oc{X}}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}=\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{dig}}_{X}
which is one of the required comonad commutations.
The others are proven similarly.

The monoidality Seely isomorphisms
\mathsf{m}^{0}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(1,\oc{\top})
and
\mathsf{m}^{2}_{X_{1},X_{2}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\oc{X_{1}}}\otimes{\oc{X_{2}}},\oc({{X_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{2}}}))
are given by
\mathsf{m}^{0}_{\ast,[\,]}=1
and
\mathsf{m}^{2}_{((m_{1},m_{2}),m)}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{{1}\ast{m_{1}}+{2}\ast{m_{2}}},{m}}
where, for a multiset
m=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}]
we set
{i}\ast{m}=[{(i,a_{1}),\dots,(i,a_{k})}]
, see
Section
2.1
.
It is obvious that
\mathsf{m}^{0}
is an iso. To check that

\mathsf{m}^{2}_{X_{1},x_{2}}
is a morphism we use
Lemma
3.7
: let
x_{i}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{i}}
for

i=1,2
, one has
{\mathsf{m}^{2}_{X_{1},X_{2}}}\cdot{({x_{1}^{!}}\otimes{x_{2}^{!}})}=\langle{x_{1},x_{2}}\rangle^{!}\in\mathsf{P}{\oc({{X_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{2}}})}
. Conversely, defining

s\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{{\oc({{X_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{2}}})}\multimap{({\oc{X_{1}}}\otimes{\oc{X_{2}}})}}
by
s_{m,(m_{1},m_{2})}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{{1}\ast{m_{1}}+{2}\ast{m_{2}}},{m}}
we
have
{s}\cdot{\langle{x_{1},x_{2}}\rangle^{!}}={x_{1}^{!}}\otimes{x_{2}^{!}}\in\mathsf{P}{({\oc{X_{1}}}\otimes{\oc{X_{2}}})}
for all

x_{i}\in\mathsf{P}{X_{i}}
(
i=1,2
), and hence
s\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc({{X_{1}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{2}}}),({\oc{X_{1}}}\otimes{\oc{X_{2}}}))
. It is obvious that
s
is the inverse of
\mathsf{m}^{2}_{X_{1},X_{2}}
which
is therefore an iso in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
.
Proving that it is natural and that it satisfies all the required
commutations for turning
\mathbf{Pcoh}
into a model of
LL
is routine
(using crucially Lemma
3.8
).

The induced lax monoidality
\mu^{k}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{X_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\oc{X_{k}},\oc{(X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{k})})
is such that
(\mu^{k})_{(m_{1},\dots,m_{k}),m}=1

if
m=[{(a_{1}^{1},\dots,a_{k}^{1}),\dots,(a_{1}^{n},\dots,a_{k}^{n}))}]
and
(m_{i}=[{a_{i}^{1},\dots,a_{i}^{n}}])_{i=1}^{k}
, and
(\mu^{k})_{(m_{1},\dots,m_{k}),m}=0
otherwise.

Theorem 3.9
.

The SMC
\mathbf{Pcoh}
is a Lafont category.

Proof 3.10
.

This is the object of
[
CEPT17
]
.

3.4.1.
Elementary differential structure of
\mathbf{Pcoh}

The category
\mathbf{Pcoh}
has zero-morphisms (we have the
0
matrix
in
\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
for any two objects
X
and
Y
).

The object
\mathbb{D}={1}\mathrel{\&}{1}
can be described as
|{\mathbb{D}}|=\{0,1\}
and
\mathsf{P}{\mathbb{D}}=[0,1]^{2}
.
Then the morphisms
(\overline{\pi}_{r}^{\with}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(1,\mathbb{D}))_{r=0,1}
are
characterized by
{\overline{\pi}_{0}^{\with}}\cdot{u}=(u,0)
and
{\overline{\pi}_{1}^{\with}}\cdot{u}=(0,u)
for
u\in\mathsf{P}{1}=[0,1]
. These two morphisms are jointly epic
because, for any
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathbb{D},X)
and
(u_{0},u_{1})\in\mathsf{P}{\mathbb{D}}

one has
{t}\cdot{(u_{0},u_{1})}={t}\cdot{(u_{0},0)}+{t}\cdot{(0,u_{1})}
by
linearity of
t
.

Given a PCS
X
, the PCS
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X=\left({\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X}\right)
is
characterized by
|{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X}|=\{0,1\}\times|{X}|
and
\mathsf{P}{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X}=\{(x_{0},x_{1})\in\mathsf{P}{X}^{2}\mid x_{0}+x_{1}\in\mathsf{P}{X}\}
(to
be more precise, an element
x\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X}|}
belongs
to
\mathsf{P}{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X}
if
x_{0}+x_{1}\in\mathsf{P}{X}
, where

x_{r}\in\mathsf{P}{X}
is given by
x_{r}(a)=x(r,a)
, we refer to
Remark
3.5
for the notation).
Given
x\in\mathsf{P}{(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X)}
, the morphism

x\,\Delta^{\with}\in\,\mathbf{Pcoh}(1,X)
, considered as an element of

\mathsf{P}{X}
, is simply
x_{0}+x_{1}
.
So the natural transformations
\operatorname{\pi}_{0},\operatorname{\pi}_{1},\sigma\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X,X)
are characterized by
{\operatorname{\pi}_{r}}\cdot{x}=x_{r}
and
{\sigma}\cdot{x}=x_{0}+x_{1}
.

Therefore two morphisms
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
are summable iff
\forall x\in\mathsf{P}{X}\ {s_{0}}\cdot{x}+{s_{1}}\cdot{x}\in\mathsf{P}{Y}
which
is equivalent to
s_{0}+s_{1}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
since
{s_{0}}\cdot{x}+{s_{1}}\cdot{x}={(s_{0}+s_{1})}\cdot{x}
.
Then the witness of summability is
\langle{s_{0},s_{1}}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}Y)
characterized by
{\langle{s_{0},s_{1}}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}}}\cdot{x}=({s_{0}}\cdot{x},{s_{1}}\cdot{x})
.
Let
s_{00},s_{01},s_{10},s_{11}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
be morphisms such that

(s_{00},s_{01})
and
(s_{10},s_{11})
are summable, and moreover

(s_{00}+s_{01},s_{10}+s_{11})
is summable.
Then the witnesses

\langle{s_{00},s_{01}}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}},\langle{s_{10},s_{11}}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X)
are
summable because
\langle{s_{00},s_{01}}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}}+\langle{s_{10},s_{11}}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}}=\langle{s_{00}+s_{10},s_{01}+s_{11}}\rangle_{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}}
as easily checked.
So (
S-witness
) holds (see Section
3.2.2

and
[
Ehr23
]
) which shows that
\mathbf{Pcoh}
is an elementary
summable category.

As explained in Section
3.2.2

and
[
Ehr23
]
Section 5.1
\mathbb{D}
is equipped with a commutative comonoid structure given by
the two
\mathbf{Pcoh}
morphisms
\mathsf{pr}_{0}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathbb{D},1)
and
\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathbb{D},{\mathbb{D}}\otimes{\mathbb{D}})
, which are given by
(\mathsf{pr}_{0})_{r,\ast}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{r},{0}}
and
\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}}_{r,(r_{0},r_{1})}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{r},{r_{0}+r_{1}}}
for
r,r_{0},r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}
.
Therefore, by Theorem
3.9
,
\mathbb{D}
has an induced

\oc
-coalgebra structure
\tilde{\partial}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathbb{D},\oc{\mathbb{D}})
,
which is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{\partial}_{r,[{r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}]}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{r},{\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{k}}}\,,

in other words
\tilde{\partial}_{0,[{r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}]}
is equal to
1
if
all the
r_{i}
’s are
=0
and to
0
otherwise.
And
\tilde{\partial}_{1,[{r_{1},\dots,r_{k}}]}
is equal to
1
if exactly one
among the
r_{i}
’s is equal to
1
an all the others are equal to

0
, and to
0
otherwise.

By Theorem 21 of
[
Ehr23
]

we know that
\tilde{\partial}
(denoted
\delta
in that paper)
defines a coherent differential structure on
\mathbf{Pcoh}
.
Let us describe explicitly the associated natural
\operatorname{\partial}_{X}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X},\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}{\oc{X}})
.
We know that
\operatorname{\partial}_{X}=\operatorname{\mathsf{cur}}d
where
d\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\oc{\left({\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X}\right)}\otimes\mathbb{D},\oc{X})
is defined as
the following composition of morphisms in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oc({{\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X}})}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oc({{\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X}})}\otimes{\oc{\mathbb{D}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc({{\left({\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X}\right)}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\oc({{\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X}})}\otimes{\tilde{\partial}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{2}_{{\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X},\mathbb{D}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}}}

.

Let

d^{\prime}=\mu^{2}_{{\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X},\mathbb{D}}\,({\oc({{\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X}})}\otimes{\tilde{\partial}})\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\oc({{\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X}})}\otimes{\mathbb{D}},\oc({{\left({\mathbb{D}}\multimap{X}\right)}\otimes{\mathbb{D}}}))
, we have

\displaystyle d^{\prime}_{([{(r_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(r_{n},a_{n}))}],r),m}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }m=[{((r_{1},a_{1}),r^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,((r_{n},a_{n}),r^{\prime}_{n})}]\text{ and }r=r^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+r^{\prime}_{n}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

On the other hand, to have

(\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}})_{[{((r_{1},a_{1}),r^{\prime}_{1}),\dots,((r_{n},a_{n}),r^{\prime}_{n})}],p}\not=0
we need
(r_{i}=r^{\prime}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and
p=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}]
.
So to have
d_{([{(r_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(r_{n},a_{n}))}],r),p}\not=0
we need
r=r_{1}+\cdots+r_{n}
and
p=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}]
, and then

\displaystyle d_{([{(r_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(r_{n},a_{n}))}],r),p}=(\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}})_{[{((r_{1},a_{1}),r_{1}),\dots,((r_{n},a_{n}),r_{n})}],[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}]}

Notice that
\mathsf{L}([{((r_{1},a_{1}),r_{1}),\dots,((r_{n},a_{n}),r_{n})}],[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}])
contains exactly one element
h
such that

\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}^{h}\not=0
, namely the multiset
h=[{(((r_{1},a_{1}),r_{1}),a_{1}),\dots,(((r_{n},a_{n}),r_{n}),a_{n})}]
, and of
course
\operatorname{\mathsf{ev}}^{h}=1
.
If
r=0
we have
r_{1}=\cdots=r_{n}=0
and hence

\displaystyle d_{([{(r_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(r_{n},a_{n})}],r),p}=\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{p}{h}=1\,.

If
r=1
, there is exactly one
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that

r_{i}=1
, and we have
r_{j}=0
for
j\not=i
. Then we have

\displaystyle d_{([{(r_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(r_{n},a_{n})}],r),p}=\genfrac{[}{]}{0.0pt}{}{p}{h}=p(a_{i})\,.

To summarize

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\partial}_{X})_{m^{\prime},(r,m)}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }r=0\text{ and }m^{\prime}={0}\ast{m}\\
m(a)&\text{if }r=1,\ a\in\operatorname{\mathsf{supp}}({m})\text{ and }m^{\prime}=({0}\ast{(m-[{a}])})+[{(1,a)}]\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Let
t\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(X,Y)=\mathsf{P}{({\oc{X}}\multimap{Y})}
. Then
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}t\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}Y)
is defined as
(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}t)\,\operatorname{\partial}_{X}
, so we have

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}t)_{m^{\prime},(r,b)}=\begin{cases}t_{m,b}&\text{if }r=0\text{ and }m^{\prime}={0}\ast{m}\\
(m(a)+1)t_{m+[{a}],b}&\text{if }r=1\text{ and }m^{\prime}=({0}\ast{m})+[{(1,a)}]\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Notice that in the above trichotomy the multiset
m
is completely
determined by the condition on
m^{\prime}
: in the first case

m^{\prime}=[{(0,a_{1}),\dots,(0,a_{n})}]
and then
m=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}]
. In
the second case
m^{\prime}=[{(r_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(r_{n},a_{n})}]
and there is
exactly one index
i
such that
r_{i}=1
, and we have
r_{j}=0
for

j\not=i
. Then we have

m=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{i-1},a_{i+1},\dots,a_{n}}]
and
a=a_{i}
.

Let
t\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(X,Y)
, to describe the function
\widehat{\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}t}:\mathsf{P}{(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X)}\to\mathsf{P}{(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}Y)}
, remember first
that
\mathsf{P}{(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X)}
can be identified with the set of all pairs

(x,u)\in\mathsf{P}{X}^{2}
such that
x+u\in\mathsf{P}{X}
. With this
identification, the element
(x,u)^{!}
of

\mathsf{P}{(\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}X})}
is given by

\displaystyle(x,u)^{!}_{m^{\prime}}=\prod_{a\in|{X}|}x_{a}^{m^{\prime}(0,a)}\prod_{a\in|{X}|}u_{a}^{m^{\prime}(1,a)}\,.

If
m^{\prime}={0}\ast{m}
then
(x,u)^{!}_{m^{\prime}}=x^{m}

and if
m^{\prime}={0}\ast{m}+[{(1,a)}]
then
(x,u)^{!}_{m^{\prime}}=x^{m}u_{a}
.
It follows that

\displaystyle\widehat{\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}t}(x,u)=\left(\widehat{t}(x),\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X}),a\in|{X}|,b\in|{Y}|}(m(a)+1)t_{m+[{a}],b}x^{m}u_{a}\,\mathsf{e}_{b}\right)

and notice that the second component of this tuple is nothing but the

u
-linear component of the powerseries
\widehat{t}(x+u)

(see
[
Ehr19
]
). So, as expected, if we set
f=\widehat{t}
then

\widehat{\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}t}(x,u)=(f(x),f^{\prime}(x)\cdot u)

in the ordinary sense of mathematical differentiation.

\blacktriangleright
Example 1.

There is a morphism
t\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(1,1)
such that,
identifying
\mathsf{P}{1}
with
[0,1]
, one has

\widehat{t}(x)=1-\sqrt{1-x}=\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}t_{n}x^{n}=f(x)
for a sequence

(t_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of non-negative real numbers that we could
write explicitly. Then
\widehat{\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}t}(x,u)=(\widehat{t}(x),\sum_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}(n+1)t_{n+1}x^{n}u)=(f(x),f^{\prime}(x)u).
In this case it is interesting to notice that

f^{\prime}(x)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{1-x}}
is not defined for
x=1
but that

f^{\prime}(x)u
is defined even for
x=1
(and takes value
0
)
because of the constraint that
x+u=1
. And indeed we know that
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}t\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}1,\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}1)
. The function
f

is entirely defined by the equation
f(x)=\frac{1}{2}x+\frac{1}{2}f(x)^{2}

and by the fact that the corresponding series must have only
non-negative coefficients. It is easy to write in a probabilistic
version of
PCF
with a unit type a recursive program which is
interpreted as
t
.

\blacktriangleleft

4.
Syntax of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}

Our choice of notations for
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
is fully coherent with the
notations chosen to describe the model, suggesting a straightforward
denotational interpretation.
The types are
A,B,\dots\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}\iota\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,{A}\Rightarrow{B}
(with

d\in{\mathbb{N}}
) and then for any type
A
we define
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}
as follows:

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}\iota)}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}\iota
and

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{({A}\Rightarrow{B})}=({A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}})
.
Terms are given by

\displaystyle M,N,\dots\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}x

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\lambda x^{A}\,{M}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,({M})\,{N}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{Y}M\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\underline{n}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(M)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{if}_{A}^{d}(M,P,Q)

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{let}_{A}^{d}(x,M,P)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(M)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\theta^{d}(M)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(M)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,0^{A}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,M+N

where
n,d,l\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
i\in\{0,1\}
, so that our syntax has
countably many constructs.

4.1.
The typing system

The typing system uses a reduction relation
\to_{\mathsf{lin}}
expressing that
most constructs are linear wrt.
0
and addition of terms; it is specified in Figure
1
and is
based on the following notion of
linear context

\begin{split}L\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}[\ ]&\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\lambda x^{A}\,{L}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,({L})\,{N}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(L)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(L)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{if}^{d}(L,P,Q)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,L,P)\\
&\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{L}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(L)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(L)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\theta^{d}(L)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(L)\,.\end{split}

(6)

The
height

\mathsf{lh}(L)
of a linear context
L
is the distance
between its hole and its root, in other words
\mathsf{lh}([\ ])=0
,

\mathsf{lh}(\lambda x^{A}\,{L})=1+\mathsf{lh}(L)
,
\mathsf{lh}(\mathsf{if}^{d}(L,P,Q))=1+\mathsf{lh}(L)

etc
.

Remark 4.1
.

We have decorated the conditional and the let constructs with a type, which is
intended to be the type of its last parameter(s).
The only purpose of this decoration is to provide a type for the
resulting
0
in the linear reduction of
\mathsf{if}^{d}(0,P,Q)
and

\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,0,P)
in Figure
1
.
Most often, we will drop this type decoration which can easily be
retrieved from the context.

Lemma 4.2
.

For any linear context
L
we have
L[0]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}0
and
L[M_{0}+M_{1}]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}L[M_{0}]+L[M_{1}]
.

Proof 4.3
.

By induction on
\mathsf{lh}(L)
. If
\mathsf{lh}(L)=0
we use the fact that

R\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}R
.
Otherwise we have
L=K[L^{\prime}]
where
\mathsf{lh}(K)=1
and

\mathsf{lh}(L^{\prime})=\mathsf{lh}(L)-1
.
By inductive hypothesis
L^{\prime}[M_{0}+M_{1}]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}L^{\prime}[M_{0}]+L^{\prime}[M_{1}]
and hence by definition
of
\to_{\mathsf{lin}}
we have
L[M_{0}+M_{1}]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}K[L^{\prime}[M_{0}]+L^{\prime}[M_{1}]]
and by definition of

\to_{\mathsf{lin}}
again we have
K[L^{\prime}[M_{0}]+L^{\prime}[M_{1}]]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}L[M_{0}]+L[M_{1}]
. The case of
L[0]
is similar.

L[0]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}0

L[M_{0}+M_{1}]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}L[M_{0}]+L[M_{1}]

M\to_{\mathsf{lin}}M^{\prime}
L[M]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}L[M^{\prime}]

Figure 1.
Linear reduction,
L
must be a linear context of height
1
.

(
var
)

i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
{(x_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{k}:A_{k})}\vdash{x_{i}}:{A_{i}}

(
abs
)

{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{M}:{B}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\lambda x^{A}\,{M}}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}

(
app
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{A}
{\Gamma}\vdash{({M})\,{N}}:{B}

(
fix
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{{A}\Rightarrow{A}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{Y}M}:{A}

(
num
)

n\in{\mathbb{N}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\underline{n}}:{\iota}

(
suc
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}

(
prd
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(M)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}

(
if
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{P}:{A}
{\Gamma}\vdash{Q}:{A}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{if}_{A}^{d}(M,P,Q)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}

(
0
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{0^{A}}:{A}

(
proj1
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{A}}
i\in\{0,1\}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}

(
proj2
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{A}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(M)+\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(M)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}

(
projd
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{0}+M_{1}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{A}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(M_{0})+\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(M_{1})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}

(
lin
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{A}
M\to_{\mathsf{lin}}M^{\prime}
{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\prime}}:{A}

(
inj
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}
i\in\{0,1\}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(M)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{A}}

(
sum
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{A}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\theta^{d}(M)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{A}}

(
circ
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+l+2}{A}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(M)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+l+2}{A}}

(
diff
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}}

(
let
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
{\Gamma,x:\iota}\vdash{N}:{B}
{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{let}_{A}^{d}(x,M,N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}}

Figure 2.
Typing rules

One should think of a term of type
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{k}{A}
as a complete binary
tree of height
k
whose leaves have type
A
.
In constructs such as
\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M)
, the integer
d
represents the
“depth” at which the corresponding operation is performed in a tree
of type
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{k}{A}
with
k\geq d
.
The main intuitive feature of such a tree is that its leaves are
summable. When
d=0
we often drop the superscript.

We provide a typing system in Figure
2
allowing
one to prove typing judgments
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{A}
.
Notice that in general, when
{\Gamma}\vdash{N_{0}}:{A}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{N_{1}}:{A}
, it is not necessarily true that
{\Gamma}\vdash{N_{0}+N_{1}}:{A}
.

Some examples of terms are provided in Section
4.4

together with their relational semantics.
More examples will be provided in forthcoming articles.

Remark 4.4
.

We use the notation
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}
for the syntactic differentiation
of a term
M
instead of
\mathsf{D}M
as in an earlier version
of this paper, and similarly for types.
In that way we avoid a clash with the notations used in the
(categorical and additive) setting of cartesian differential
categories for denoting an operation which, in our syntax, would
correspond to
\operatorname{\pi}_{1}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M})
.
We use fully compatible notations in the final version
of
[
Ehr23
]
.

4.2.
Differential

Given a variable
x
and a term
N
, we define a term
\partial({x},{M})
in Figure
3
which is called the

differential
of
M
with respect to
x
.

As it is usual in the
\lambda
-calculus, this definition requires
some
\alpha
-conversions to be performed on the fly.

\displaystyle\partial({x},{y})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}x&\text{if }y=x\\
\operatorname{\iota}_{0}(y)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\lambda y^{B}\,{P}})

\displaystyle=\lambda y^{B}\,{\partial({x},{P})}\text{ if }x\not=y

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{M})})

\displaystyle\partial({x},{({P})\,{Q}})

\displaystyle={({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{P})})})\,{\partial({x},{Q})}}

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\mathsf{Y}M})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Y}(\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{M})}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\underline{n}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\iota}_{0}(\underline{n})

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{M}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(M)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{M}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,P,Q)})

\displaystyle=\theta(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{if}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{M}),\partial({x},{P}),\partial({x},{Q}))))\hskip-160.00024pt

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\mathsf{let}^{d}(y,P,Q)})

\displaystyle=\theta(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{let}^{d+1}(y,\partial({x},{P}),\partial({x},{Q}))))\hskip-160.00024pt

\displaystyle\partial({x},{0})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\partial({x},{M_{0}+M_{1}})

\displaystyle=\partial({x},{M_{0}})+\partial({x},{M_{1}})

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{M}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\theta^{d}(M)})

\displaystyle=\theta^{d+1}(\partial({x},{M}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(M)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{M}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(M)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d+1}_{l}(\partial({x},{M}))

Figure 3.
Inductive definition of the differential of a term

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
L
be a linear context. There is a linear context
\partial({x},{L})
such that, for any term
M
, we have
\partial({x},{L[M]})=\partial({x},{L})[\partial({x},{M})]
.

Proof 4.6
.

Simple analysis of the definition of
\partial({x},{M})
in
Figure
3
.
The definition of
\partial({x},{L})
is given in
Figure
4
.

\displaystyle\partial({x},{[\ ]})

\displaystyle=[\ ]

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\lambda y^{A}\,{L}})

\displaystyle=\lambda y^{A}\,{\partial({x},{L})}\text{ if }x\not=y

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{L}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{L})})

\displaystyle\partial({x},{({L})\,{N}})

\displaystyle={({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{L})})})\,{\partial({x},{N})}}

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(L)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{L}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(L)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{L}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\mathsf{if}^{d}(L,P,Q)})

\displaystyle=\theta(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{if}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{L}),\partial({x},{P}),\partial({x},{Q}))))\hskip-160.00024pt

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\mathsf{let}^{d}(y,L,Q)})

\displaystyle=\theta(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{let}^{d+1}(y,\partial({x},{L}),\partial({x},{Q}))))\hskip-160.00024pt

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(L)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{L}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\theta^{d}(L)})

\displaystyle=\theta^{d+1}(\partial({x},{L}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(L)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{L}))

\displaystyle\partial({x},{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(L)})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d+1}_{l}(\partial({x},{L}))

Figure 4.
Inductive definition of the differential of a context

Lemma 4.7
.

If
R\to_{\mathsf{lin}}R^{\prime}
and
L
is a linear context then
L[R]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}L[R^{\prime}]
. We also have
L[0]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}0
and

L[R_{0}+R_{1}]\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}L[R_{0}]+L[R_{1}]
.

Proof 4.8
.

Straightforward inductions on
\mathsf{lh}(L)
.

Lemma 4.9
.

If
R\to_{\mathsf{lin}}R^{\prime}
then
\partial({x},{R})\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}\partial({x},{R^{\prime}})
.

Proof 4.10
.

By induction on the derivation of
R\to_{\mathsf{lin}}R^{\prime}
.
Assume that
R=L[R_{0}+R_{1}]
and
R^{\prime}=L[R_{0}]+L[R_{1}]
with

\mathsf{lh}(L)=1
. Using Lemma
4.5
, we have

\displaystyle\partial({x},{R})

\displaystyle=\partial({x},{L})[\partial({x},{R_{0}+R_{1}})]

\displaystyle=\partial({x},{L})[\partial({x},{R_{0}})+\partial({x},{R_{1}})]

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}\partial({x},{L})[\partial({x},{R_{0}})]+\partial({x},{L})[\partial({x},{R_{1}})]

\displaystyle=\partial({x},{L[R_{0}]})+\partial({x},{L[R_{1}]})

\displaystyle=\partial({x},{L[R_{0}]+L[R_{1}]})

by Lemma
4.7
.

Assume now that
R=L[M]
,
R^{\prime}=L[M^{\prime}]
and
M\to_{\mathsf{lin}}M^{\prime}
. By
inductive hypothesis we know that
\partial({x},{M})\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}\partial({x},{M^{\prime}})
.
We have
\partial({x},{R})=\partial({x},{L})[\partial({x},{M})]
and
\partial({x},{R^{\prime}})=\partial({x},{L})[\partial({x},{M^{\prime}})]
by Lemma
4.5
and hence
\partial({x},{R})\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}\partial({x},{R^{\prime}})
by
Lemma
4.7
.

Lemma 4.11
.

If
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{M}:{B}
then
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{M})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
.

Proof 4.12
.

We consider the following cases, the others are left to the reader.

\blacktriangleright
Assume first that
M=\mathsf{if}^{d}(P,Q_{0},Q_{1})
and that the last typing
rule is
2
so that
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{P}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
and

{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{Q_{i}}:{B}
for
i=0,1
. By inductive hypothesis we
have
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{P})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{\iota}}
and
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{Q_{i}})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
for
i=0,1
. Applying the rule
2
we get

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\mathsf{if}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{P}),\partial({x},{Q_{0}}),\partial({x},{Q_{1}}))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}}
and hence we have

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{if}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{P}),\partial({x},{Q_{0}}),\partial({x},{Q_{1}})))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}}
. Therefore

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\theta(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{if}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{P}),\partial({x},{Q_{0}}),\partial({x},{Q_{1}}))))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}}\,.

Notice finally that
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}}
is exactly
the type expected for
\partial({x},{M})
in that case.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{P}
and that the last typing rule is
2
so that
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{P}:{{C}\Rightarrow{D}}
,

{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{M}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{D}}}
and

B=({\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{D}})
. By inductive hypothesis we have

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{P})}:{{C}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{D}}}
and hence

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{P})}}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{D}}}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{({\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}\Rightarrow{D})}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}
. It follows that

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{P})})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
as required.

\blacktriangleright
Assume next that
M=\lambda y^{C}\,{P}
and that the last typing rule is
2
so that
{\Gamma,x:A,y:C}\vdash{P}:{D}
(and hence

B=({C}\Rightarrow{D})
). By inductive hypothesis

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A},y:C}\vdash{\partial({x},{P})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{D}}
and hence

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\lambda y^{B}\,{\partial({x},{P})}}:{({C}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{D}})=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}

as required.

\blacktriangleright
Assume now that
M=({P})\,{Q}
and that the last typing rule is
2
with
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{P}:{{C}\Rightarrow{B}}
and

{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{Q}:{C}
. Then by inductive hypothesis we have

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{P})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{({C}\Rightarrow{B})}}=({C}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}})

and
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{Q})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}
. Therefore

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{P})}}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{B}}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{({\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}\Rightarrow{B})}}

and hence
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{P})})}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}}
so that
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{P})})})\,{\partial({x},{Q})}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}

by the rules
2
and
2
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(P)
and that the last typing rule is
2
with
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{P}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{l+d+2}{C}=B}
. By inductive
hypothesis we have
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{P})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{l+d+3}{C}}
and hence
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d+1}_{l}(\partial({x},{P}))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{l+d+3}{C}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
by
applying the rule
2
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\mathsf{Y}P
and that the last typing rule is
2
with
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{P}:{{B}\Rightarrow{B}}
so that

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{P})}:{{B}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}}
and hence

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{P})}}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{B}}}
by
2
and therefore

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{P})})}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}}
by
2
and finally

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\mathsf{Y}(\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x},{P})}))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
by
2
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that the last typing rule is
2
meaning that we have

M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(P)
and
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
with
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{P}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
. Then by inductive hypothesis we have
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{P})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{C}}
and hence
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{P}))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
by the
rule
2
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that the last typing rule is
2
so that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(P)+\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(P)
and
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
with
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{P}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
. By inductive hypothesis we have
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{P})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}}
and hence
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{P}))+\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{P}))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
by the rule
2
. That is
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{M})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
as expected.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that the last typing rule is
2
so that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(P_{0})+\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(P_{1})
with
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{P_{0}+P_{1}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
and
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
. By inductive hypothesis we have

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{P_{0}})+\partial({x},{P_{1}})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{C}}

and hence
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{P_{0}}))+\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d+1}(\partial({x},{P_{1}}))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
that is
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{M})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
as expected.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that the last typing rule is
2
so that
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{P}:{B}
and
P\to_{\mathsf{lin}}M
. By inductive hypothesis
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{P})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
and we have
\partial({x},{P})\to_{\mathsf{lin}}^{\ast}\partial({x},{M})
by Lemma
4.9

and hence
{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\vdash{\partial({x},{M})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
by the rule
2
.

4.3.
Reduction rules

We define a rewriting system
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
. The elements of
\underline{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}

are the terms of the syntax introduced above.
The main reduction rules are given in Figure
5
.

\displaystyle({\lambda x^{A}\,{M}})\,{N}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}{M}\left[{N}/{x}\right]

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{(}{\lambda x^{A}\,{M}})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{0}(\underline{n})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\underline{n+1}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{0}(\underline{0})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\underline{0}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{0}(\underline{n+1})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\underline{n}

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}^{0}(\underline{0},P,Q)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}P

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}^{0}(\underline{n+1},P,Q)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}Q

\displaystyle\mathsf{let}^{0}(x,\underline{n},P)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}{P}\left[{\underline{n}}/{x}\right]

\displaystyle\mathsf{Y}P

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}({P})\,{\mathsf{Y}P}

Figure 5.
Main reduction rules

A second series of reduction rules given in
Figure
6
specifies how the projections

\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{d}(M)
interact with the other constructs. They are crucially
used for “reading” the result of a computation by accessing leaves
of a “tree” of type
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}
(complete binary tree of height

d
; the leaves can themselves be trees if
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{B}
with

e>0
).

Remark 4.13
.

The two rules

\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{e}(M))\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M))
if
d<e
and
\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{e}(M))\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{e}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(M))
if
e\leq d
lead clearly to infinite sequences of computations so
it would be tempting to remove one of them from the rewriting
system. However both seem necessary in order to prove the soundness
of the stack machine that we introduce in Section
6.1
.

We also need the reduction rule

M\to_{\mathsf{lin}}M^{\prime}
M\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}M^{\prime}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\lambda x^{A}\,{M})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\lambda x^{A}\,{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M)}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(({M})\,{N})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}({\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M)})\,{N}

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }d<e

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }d<e

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\mathsf{if}^{e}(M,P,Q))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\mathsf{if}^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M),P,Q)\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }d<e

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\mathsf{if}^{e}(M,P,Q))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\mathsf{if}^{e}(M,\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d-e}(P),\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d-e}(Q))

\displaystyle\text{\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012pt\hskip 8.50012ptif }e\leq d

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\mathsf{let}^{e}(x,M,P))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\mathsf{let}^{e-1}(x,\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M),P)\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }d<e

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\mathsf{let}^{e}(x,M,P))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\mathsf{let}^{e}(x,M,\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d-e}(P))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }e\leq d

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(\theta^{d}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(M))

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(\theta^{d}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(M))+\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(M))

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\theta^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\theta^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }d<e

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\theta^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\theta^{e}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }e<d

\displaystyle\hskip-153.0022pt\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{l+1}}^{d}(\cdots\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{0}}^{d}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(M)))\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{0}}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{l+1}}^{d}(\cdots\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{1}}^{d}(M)))

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e}_{l}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e-1}_{l}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }d<e

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e}_{l}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e}_{l}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }e+l+2\leq d

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\iota}_{j}^{d}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}M\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }i=j

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\iota}_{j}^{d}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}0\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }i\not=j

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\iota}_{j}^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\iota}_{j}^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }d<e

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\iota}_{j}^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\iota}_{j}^{e}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d-1}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }e<d

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }d<e

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{e}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(M))\text{\hskip 8.50012ptif }e\leq d

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M)}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M)}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M})

Figure 6.
Projection reduction rules

Example 4.14
.

Let
T=\lambda f^{{A}\Rightarrow{A}}\,{\lambda x^{A}\,{({f})\,{({f})\,{x}}}}
so that

\displaystyle{}\vdash{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{T}}:{{({A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}})}\Rightarrow{{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}}}\,,

we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{T}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}\lambda f^{{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}}\,{\partial({f},{\lambda x^{A}\,{({f})\,{({f})\,{x}}}})}

\displaystyle=\lambda f^{{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}}\,{\lambda x^{A}\,{\partial({f},{({f})\,{({f})\,{x}}})}}

\displaystyle=\lambda f^{{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}}\,{\lambda x^{A}\,{({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({f},{f})})})\,{\partial({f},{({f})\,{x}})}}}

\displaystyle=\lambda f^{{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}}\,{\lambda x^{A}\,{({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{f})})\,{({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{f})})\,{\partial({f},{x})}}}}

\displaystyle=\lambda f^{{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}}\,{\lambda x^{A}\,{({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{f})})\,{({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{f})})\,{\operatorname{\iota}_{0}(x)}}}}

which shows that, contrarily to what happens in the differential

\lambda
-calculus, even if the variable
f
occurs twice, no
actual sum is created during this computation of the differential of

T
.

4.3.1.
Reducing sums, and the evaluation contexts

These reduction rules can be applied almost anywhere in a term (taking
care as usual of not binding free variables of
N
in the ordinary
substitution
{M}\left[{N}/{x}\right]
and in the differential substitution

\partial({x},{M})
).

However, in order to make the proof of subject reduction possible, we
forbid reductions within subterms of the shape
M_{0}+M_{1}
.
Indeed by the very nature of the coherence we want to implement in
this programming language, we have provided very restricted ways to
type sums.
For that reason allowing one for instance to reduce
M_{0}
to some

M^{\prime}_{0}
by performing, say, a
\beta
-reduction would lead to a term

M^{\prime}_{0}+M_{1}
whose typeability is not at all obvious (imagine for
instance that
M_{i}=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(M)
for some
M
such that

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}
).
One option would be to develop a theory of “parallel” reductions
generalizing the observation that in the example at hand the

\beta
-reduction performed in
M_{0}
is also available in
M_{1}

because both come from the same term
M
.
This kind of approach
will certainly be developed in further work.
For the time being we adopt a much simpler and conservative approach.
So here is the syntax of our evaluation contexts:

\displaystyle E\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}[\ ]

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\lambda x^{A}\,{E}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,({E})\,{N}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,({M})\,{E}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{Y}E\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(E)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(E)

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{if}^{d}(E,P,Q)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,E,Q)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,P,E)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,E,P)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,M,E)

\displaystyle\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{E}\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(E)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(E)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\theta^{d}(E)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(E)

and the associated inference rule is as usual

M\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}M^{\prime}
E[M]\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}E[M^{\prime}]

We will need however to perform reduction within sums at some point
otherwise our computations will remain stuck for artificial
reasons. So we do allow such reductions but only at “toplevel”: this
is precisely the purpose of the associated rewriting system
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})
defined in Section
2.2
.

4.3.2.
Term multiset typing

Let
S=[{M_{1},\dots,M_{k}}]\in\underline{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})}
,
\Gamma
be a
context and
A
be a type. We write
{\Gamma}\vdash{S}:{A}
if

{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{i}}:{A}
for
i=1,\dots,k
. This notion of typing for
multisets (which represent sums of terms) is quite weak:
{\Gamma}\vdash{[{M_{0},M_{1}}]}:{A}
does not imply

{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{0}+M_{1}}:{A}
.
It is only for that reason that we will be able to prove subject
reduction for
\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})}}
. This is not really an issue because we will prove that the semantics
is invariant by reduction (including the
\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})}}
reduction) — a
property which is called as usual
soundness
— so we know that
actually the terms that we obtain by performing the
\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})}}

reduction belong to the expected type even if we are not necessarily
able to prove it syntactically.

4.3.3.
Subject reduction

Lemma 4.15
.

If
{\Gamma,x:A}\vdash{M}:{B}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{A}
then

{\Gamma}\vdash{{M}\left[{N}/{x}\right]}:{B}
.

Proof 4.16
.

Straightforward induction on the typing derivation of
M
.

Theorem 4.17

(Subject reduction)
.

If
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{A}
and
M\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}M^{\prime}
then
{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\prime}}:{A}
.

Proof 4.18
.

The last possible typing rules for the derivation of
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{A}
cannot be any of the rules
2
,
2
or
2
since the reduction
\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}
does not apply to sums and to

0
. For the remaining typing rules, observe that the typing
system is syntax directed, we consider a few reductions. The proof
is by induction on the derivation of
M\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}M^{\prime}
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{(\lambda x^{B}\,{N})}
and

M^{\prime}=\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}\,{\partial({x},{N})}
so that the last typing rule is
2
, with
{\Gamma,x:B}\vdash{N}:{C}
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{\lambda x^{B}\,{N}}:{{B}\Rightarrow{C}}
and

A=({\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}})
. Then we have

{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}\vdash{\partial({x},{N})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}
by
Lemma
4.11
, and therefore

{\Gamma}\vdash{\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}\,{\partial({x},{N})}}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}}
by
2
. All the other reduction rules of Figure
5
are
dealt with as usual in the typed
\lambda
-calculus, using
Lemma
4.15
.

The fact that if
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{A}
and
M\to_{\mathsf{lin}}M^{\prime}
then

{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\prime}}:{A}
is by a straightforward application of rule
2
.

So we consider now some of the rules of Figure
6
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\lambda x^{B}\,{N})
with

{\Gamma,x:B}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
so that

A=({B}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}})=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{({B}\Rightarrow{C})}
. Then we
have
{\Gamma,x:B}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}}
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{\lambda x^{B}\,{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N)}}:{A}
. And on the other
hand
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\lambda x^{B}\,{N})}:{A}
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(({N})\,{P})
with
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{{B}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{({B}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}})}}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{P}:{B}
so that
{\Gamma}\vdash{({N})\,{P}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{A}
where
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
, and on the other hand

{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N)}:{{B}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}}}
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{({\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N)})\,{P}}:{A}
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{e}(N))
with
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{\iota}}
. For
M
to be typeable we need to have
d<e
and then
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e-1}{\iota}=A}
. Then we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e-1}{\iota}}
and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N))}:{A}
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\mathsf{if}^{e}(N,P,Q))
and
d<e
, with

{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{\iota}}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{P}:{B}
and

{\Gamma}\vdash{Q}:{B}
so that
{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{if}^{e}(N,P,Q)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{B}}
and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e-1}{B}}
, which means that
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e-1}{B}
. On the other hand we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e-1}{\iota}}
and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{if}^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N),P,Q)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e-1}{B}}
as
expected.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\mathsf{if}^{e}(N,P,Q))
and
e\leq d
, with
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{\iota}}
and assume that
{\Gamma}\vdash{P}:{B}
and

{\Gamma}\vdash{Q}:{B}
so that
{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{if}^{e}(N,P,Q)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{B}=A}
. For the term
M
to be typeable, we need
B
to be of
shape
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d-e+1}{C}
for some (uniquely defined) type
C
so that

A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}
and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}}
. On the other hand we have

{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d-e}(P)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d-e}{C}}
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{\mathsf{if}^{e}(N,\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d-e}(P),\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d-e}(Q))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}}
as expected.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(\theta^{d}(N))
with
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{C}}
, and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{\theta^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
and therefore
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(\theta^{d}(N))}:{C}
, so that
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
. Then we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
by
2
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(N))}:{A}
by
2
again.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(\theta^{d}(N))
with

{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{C}}
, and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{\theta^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
and therefore
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(\theta^{d}(N))}:{C}
, so that
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
. Then we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(N)+\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}}
by
2
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(N))+\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(N))}:{A}
by
2
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\theta^{e}(N))
with
d<e
. So we must have

{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+2}{A}}
so that

{\Gamma}\vdash{\theta^{e}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+1}{A}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e-d}{A}}}
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\theta^{e}(N))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{A}}
. We have

{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+1}{A}}
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{\theta^{e-1}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{A}}
as required.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\theta^{e}(N))
with
e<d
. We must have

{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+2}{A}}
so that

{\Gamma}\vdash{\theta^{e}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+1}{A}}
and for

\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\theta^{e}(N))
to be typeable we need
A
to be of shape

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d-e}{B}
(for a uniquely defined type
B
) so that

{\Gamma}\vdash{\theta^{e}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}}
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\theta^{e}(N))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{A}}
. We
have
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}}
and hence

{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}}
and therefore (using
the fact that
d>0
)

{\Gamma}\vdash{\theta^{e}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d+1}(N))}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{A}}
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{l+1}}^{d}(\cdots\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{0}}^{d}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(N)))
with
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{l+d+2}{C}}
and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}=A}
by
2
and
l+2
applications of
2
. Then we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{0}}^{d}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{l+1}}^{d}(\cdots\operatorname{\pi}_{i_{1}}^{d}(N)))}:{A}

by
l+2
applications of
2
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e}_{l}(N))
with
d<e
so that we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+l+2}{C}}
for a type
C
such that
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+l+1}{C}
. Then we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+l+1}{C}}
(since
d<e+l+1
) and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e-1}_{l}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N))}:{A}
since
e>0
and hence
e+l+1=(e-1)+l+2
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e}_{l}(N))
with
e+l+2\leq d
so that we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+l+2}{C}}
for a type
C
such that
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+l+1}{C}
, and moreover
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{D}
for some type
D
, meaning that
C=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d-e-l-1}{D}
(remember that
d-e-l-1>0
). Then we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+l+1}{C}=A}

by
2
and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e}_{l}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(N))}:{A}
by
2
which can be applied since
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+l+2}{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d-e-l-2}{D}}
.

The remaining cases are similar.

Given a derivation
\delta
in the typing system we use

\mathsf{sz}(\delta)
for the number of inference rule occurrences
\delta

contains.

Lemma 4.19
.

Let
\delta
be a typing derivation of
{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{0}+M_{1}}:{A}
. For
j=0,1
, there is a derivation
\delta_{j}
of
{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{j}}:{A}
such that
\mathsf{sz}(\delta_{j})\leq\mathsf{sz}(\delta)
.

Proof 4.20
.

By induction on
\delta
. The following cases can arise.

\blacktriangleright
The last rule of
\delta
is
2
so that
M_{j}=\operatorname{\pi}_{j}^{d}(M)
,
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}}
by a derivation
\delta^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime})=\mathsf{sz}(\delta)-1
that we can extend with a
rule
2
to get the required derivation
\delta_{j}
of
{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{j}}:{A}
which satisfies
\mathsf{sz}(\delta_{j})=\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime})+1=\mathsf{sz}(\delta)
.

\blacktriangleright
The last rule of
\delta
is
2
so that
M_{0}=\operatorname{\pi}_{1}^{d}(N_{0})
,
M_{1}=\operatorname{\pi}_{0}^{d}(N_{1})
,
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{N_{0}+N_{1}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}}
by a derivation
\delta^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime})=\mathsf{sz}(\delta)-1
and hence by inductive hypothesis, for
j=0,1
, we have a
derivation
\delta^{\prime}_{j}
of
{\Gamma}\vdash{N_{j}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}}
such that
\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime}_{j})\leq\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime})
that we can extend with
a
2
rule to get a derivation
\delta_{j}
of
{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{j}}:{A}
. We have

\mathsf{sz}(\delta_{j})=\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime}_{j})+1\leq\mathsf{sz}(\delta)
.

\blacktriangleright
The last rule of
\delta
is
2
so that there is a linear
context
L
of height
1
and terms
N_{0}
,
N_{1}
such that
M_{j}=L[N_{j}]
and
{\Gamma}\vdash{L[N_{0}+N_{1}]}:{A}
by a derivation
\delta^{\prime}
such that
\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime})=\mathsf{sz}(\delta)-1
. This implies (by a simple inspection of the various possibilities for

L
which has height
1
) that for some context
\Delta
and some
type
B
one has
{\Delta}\vdash{N_{0}+N_{1}}:{B}
by a derivation
\delta^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime\prime})=\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime})-k_{L}
where
k_{L}\in{{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}

depends only on
L
(if for instance

L=\mathsf{if}^{d}([\ ],P_{0},P_{1})
then
k_{L}=1+k_{0}+k_{1}
where
k_{i}
is
the size of the typing derivation of
P_{i}
). So that by inductive hypothesis we have derivations
\delta^{\prime\prime}_{j}

of
{\Delta}\vdash{N_{j}}:{B}
for
j=0,1
such that
\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime\prime}_{j})\leq\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime\prime})
. We can extend
\delta^{\prime\prime}_{j}
with exactly the typing rule associated
with
L
to get a derivation
\delta_{j}
of
{\Gamma}\vdash{L[N_{j}]}:{A}
such that
\mathsf{sz}(\delta_{j})=\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime\prime}_{j})+k_{L}\leq\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime\prime})+k_{L}=\mathsf{sz}(\delta^{\prime})=\mathsf{sz}(\delta)-1
and hence
\mathsf{sz}(\delta_{j})<\mathsf{sz}(\delta)
.

Theorem 4.21

(Subject reduction for multisets)
.

Assume that
{\Gamma}\vdash{S}:{A}
where
S\in\underline{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})}
and that
S\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})}}S^{\prime}
. Then
{\Gamma}\vdash{S^{\prime}}:{A}
.

Proof 4.22
.

The following cases are possible.

\blacktriangleright

S=S_{0}+[{M}]
,
M\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}0
and
S^{\prime}=S_{0}
. We have
{\Gamma}\vdash{S^{\prime}}:{A}
since all elements of

S^{\prime}
belong to
S
.

\blacktriangleright

S=S_{0}+[{M}]
,
M\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}M^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime}=S_{0}+[{M^{\prime}}]
. Then we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{M^{\prime}}:{A}
by
Theorem
4.17
and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{S^{\prime}}:{A}
.

\blacktriangleright

S=S_{0}+[{M}]
,
M\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}M_{0}+M_{1}
and
S^{\prime}=S_{0}+[{M_{0},M_{1}}]
. Since
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{A}
, we have
{\Gamma}\vdash{M_{i}}:{A}
for
i=0,1
by Lemma
4.19

and hence
{\Gamma}\vdash{S^{\prime}}:{A}
.

4.4.
Examples of terms

Given
M
such that
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
we set

\displaystyle{\mathsf{D}}M=\operatorname{\pi}_{1}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M})

so that
{\Gamma}\vdash{{\mathsf{D}}M}:{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{B}}
and

{\mathsf{D}}M
can be understood as the derivative of
M
taking in

\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}
an argument which combines the point at which it is
computed and the “direction” to which it is (linearly) applied.
Given moreover
N
such that
{\Gamma}\vdash{N}:{A}
we set

\displaystyle\left\langle{M}\right\rangle{N}=({{\mathsf{D}}M})\,{\operatorname{\iota}_{1}(N)}

so that
{\Gamma}\vdash{\left\langle{M}\right\rangle{N}}:{B}
.
This application of
M
to
N
will be “successful” only if

M
uses its argument linearly. Otherwise, it will produce the
result
0
.

Remark 4.23
.

We can understand the term
0
(of any type) as an
uncatchable exception which is raised when some constraint of
linearity expressed by the use of the
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{}
operator is not
respected.
In the semantics, it does not seem possible to distinguish

0^{A}
from
\mathsf{Y}\lambda x^{A}\,{x}
(an ever-looping term) so
that this exception cannot be caught
within
the language,
but, in the implementation, nothing forces us to enter into an
infinite loop when a term reduces to
0
: we can simply abort
the execution of the program and return the information that it
failed (and possibly more information about this failure: this a
mere matter of implementation).

Syntactically, whether these two kinds of zero have necessarily to
be identified remains an open question, but since our approach is
strongly based on denotational semantics and since none of our
models allows to distinguish them, we will stick to this
identification for the time being.
Nevertheless we could even imagine that the execution of our
“differential programs” is controlled by an external
“meta-program” which is allowed to handle such exceptions.

To try to provide some intuitions about the operational behavior of
our example terms, we use the intersection typing system of
Section
6.3
even if we are aware that this
system cannot be fully understood by the reader at this stage. We
believe nevertheless that most of its rules convey an intuitive
meaning which can be useful to understand these terms.

The only typing derivations for
{\mathsf{D}}f
in the intersection
typing system of Section
6.3
(see in
particular Figure
11
) lead to judgments of
shape

\displaystyle 0_{\Gamma},f:[{([{a_{1},\dots,a_{k},a}],b)}]:{A}\Rightarrow{B}\vdash{\mathsf{D}}f:([{0\cdot a_{1},\dots,0\cdot a_{1},1\cdot a}],b):{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{B}

where the notation
i\cdot a
is defined in
Section
6.3.2
.

The only typing derivations for
\left\langle{f}\right\rangle{x}
in the intersection typing
system of Figure
11
lead to judgments of shape

\displaystyle 0_{\Gamma},f:[{([{a}],b)}]:{A}\Rightarrow{B},x:[{a}]:A\vdash\left\langle{f}\right\rangle{x}:b:B

where
a\in\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
and
b\in\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
whereas the typings of

({f})\,{x}
are

\displaystyle 0_{\Gamma},f:[{(m,b)}]:{A}\Rightarrow{B},x:m:A\vdash({f})\,{x}:b:B

for all
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})
and
b\in\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
.

To illustrate this construction we define a term
M
such that
{}\vdash{M}:{{({\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota})}\Rightarrow{{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}}}
by

\displaystyle M=\mathsf{Y}\lambda F^{{({\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota})}\Rightarrow{{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}}}\,{\lambda f^{{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}}\,{\lambda x^{\iota}\,{\mathsf{let}^{0}(y,x,\mathsf{if}^{0}(y,\underline{0},\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}(({F})\,{f\,\left\langle{f}\right\rangle{y}})))}}}\,.

The intuition is that
({M})\,{f\,{\underline{\nu}}}
iterates

f:{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}
starting from
\nu
until the value
0

is reached, and returns the number of steps. Moreover, the program is
written in such a way that the argument
f
is used as a linear
morphism.
In the PCS model of Section
3.4
we can see
M

as implementing a random walk in a Markov chain (on the integers)
represented by its first argument (which is used as a

{\mathbb{N}}\times{\mathbb{N}}
sub-stochastic matrix thanks to the linear
application
\left\langle{f}\right\rangle{y}
).

In the intersection typing system of
Figure
11
, we can derive

\displaystyle\vdash M:([{([{\nu_{1}}],\nu_{2}),([{\nu_{2}}],\nu_{3}),\dots,([{\nu_{k}}],0)}],([{\nu_{1}}],k)):{({\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota})}\Rightarrow{{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}}

if
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{k}\in{{\mathbb{N}}^{+}}
.
For
k=0
this means in particular that

\vdash M:([{\,}],([{0}],0)):{({\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota})}\Rightarrow{{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}}
.
It is important to notice that the linearity of
M
in its second
parameter (called
x
in the definition of
M
) comes from our use
of the
\mathsf{let}^{0}(y,x,\_)
construct.
Then we can take the derivative of
M
, typed as follows:

\displaystyle{}\vdash{{\mathsf{D}}M}:{{({\iota}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\iota}})}\Rightarrow{{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}}}\,.

We describe now a way of building an argument for such a derivative.
Let
\mathcal{T}\subseteq{\mathbb{N}}

be recursive and implemented by some term of type

{}\vdash{T}:{\iota\to\iota}
, in the sense that

\displaystyle\llbracket{T}\rrbracket=\{([{\nu}],0)\mid\nu\in\mathcal{T}\}\cup\{([{\nu}],1)\mid\nu\notin\mathcal{T}\}\,.

Given terms
M_{0},M_{1}
such that

({\Gamma}\vdash{M_{i}}:{{\iota}\Rightarrow{A}})_{i=0,1}
(for some type
A
and
typing context
\Gamma
) we define a term
\langle{M_{0},M_{1}}\rangle^{T}
such
that
{\Gamma}\vdash{\langle{M_{0},M_{1}}\rangle^{T}}:{{\iota}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}}
given by

\displaystyle\langle{M_{0},M_{1}}\rangle^{T}=\lambda x^{\iota}\,{\mathsf{let}^{0}(y,x,\mathsf{if}^{0}(({T})\,{y},\operatorname{\iota}_{0}(({M_{0}})\,{y}),\operatorname{\iota}_{1}(({M_{1}})\,{y})))}

so that

\nu\in\mathcal{T}_{i}
f_{i}:[{(k[{\nu}],a)}]:{\iota}\Rightarrow{A},f_{1-i}:[{\,}]:{\iota}\Rightarrow{A}\vdash\langle{f_{0},f_{1}}\rangle^{T}:([{\nu}],i\cdot a):{\iota}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}

where
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1}={\mathbb{N}}\setminus\mathcal{T}
.
Let us take
A=\iota
so that

{}\vdash{({{\mathsf{D}}M})\,{\langle{M_{0},M_{1}}\rangle^{T}}}:{{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}}
.
Then one has

f_{0}:[{([{\nu_{1}}],\nu_{2}),([{\nu_{2}}],\nu_{3}),\dots,([{\nu_{i-1}}],\nu_{i}),([{\nu_{i+1}}],\nu_{i+2}),\dots,([{\nu_{k}}],0)}]:{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota},\\
f_{1}:[{([{\nu_{i}}],\nu_{i+1})}]:{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}\vdash{({{\mathsf{D}}M})\,{\langle{f_{0},f_{1}}\rangle^{T}}}:([{\nu_{1}}],k):{\iota}\Rightarrow{\iota}

as soon as
\nu_{j}\in\mathcal{T}\setminus\{0\}
for

j\in\{1,\dots,i_{i-1},i_{i+1},\dots,k\}
,
\nu_{i}\notin\mathcal{T}

and
\nu_{i}\not=0
.
In other words, in order to induce a successful computation, the
stochastic matrices
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
in
({{\mathsf{D}}M})\,{\langle{M_{0},M_{1}}\rangle^{T}}\,\underline{\nu}
need to satisfy the property that the iteration of
M_{0}
from
\nu

will leave
\mathcal{T}
exactly once, and
M_{1}
will return in
\mathcal{T}

from that external value.
During the computation,
M_{1}
is used exactly once whereas there are
no restrictions on the number of uses of
M_{0}
.

More interesting examples should arise by considering typically a term

M
such that

{\Gamma,g:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}},x:A}\vdash{M}:{B}\,.

Then we have

\displaystyle{\Gamma,f:{A}\Rightarrow{B},x:A}\vdash{{M}\left[{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{f}}/{g}\right]}:{B}

and hence

\displaystyle{\Gamma}\vdash{P=\mathsf{Y}(\lambda f^{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}\,{\lambda x^{A}\,{{M}\left[{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{f}}/{g}\right]}})}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}

meaning that we are defining a term
P
such that

{\Gamma}\vdash{P}:{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
in terms of its own derivative, something
which looks a bit like a “differential equation”.
The study of such definitions, typical of our new coherent
differential setting, seems complex and is postponed to further work.

5.
Semantics

Before explaining in Section
5.6
how types
and terms are interpreted in the Kleisli category of a coherent
differential summable resource category, we provide more information
about the categorical constructions that we will need. These
Sections
5.1
–
5.5

can be
seen as a complement to Section
3.1

and
3.2
.

5.1.
Partial derivatives

We assume to be given a summable resource category
\mathcal{L}
which is
closed (wrt. its symmetric monoidal structure) and is equipped with a
differentiation
\operatorname{\partial}
, see Section
3.1
.
We generalize the lax monoidality of the
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
functor to a natural
transformation

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{n}\in\mathcal{L}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}{X_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}{X_{n}},\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}(X_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n}))
by induction on
n
(there are
various possible definitions, all leading to the same morphisms), for
instance
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{0}=\operatorname{\iota}_{0}
and

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{n+1}=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{X_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n},X_{n+1}}\,({\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{n}}\otimes{\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}{X_{n+1}}})
. The resulting
morphism is fully characterized by the following property.

Lemma 5.1
.

\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{n}=\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\operatorname{\pi}_{0}
and

\operatorname{\pi}_{1}\,\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{n}=\operatorname{\pi}_{1}\otimes\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\operatorname{\pi}_{0}+\cdots+\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes\operatorname{\pi}_{0}\otimes\operatorname{\pi}_{1}
.

5.1.1.
Additive strength

We define morphisms

\psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}^{0}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X_{0}\mathrel{\&}X_{1},\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}\left({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}\right))
and

\psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}^{1}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X_{0}\mathrel{\&}\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X_{1},\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}\left({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}\right))
of
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
as

\displaystyle\psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}^{0}=\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}\left({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}\right)\text{\quad and\quad}\psi_{X_{0},X_{1}}^{1}=\mathsf{Lin}_{\mathord{\oc}}\left({\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X_{1}}\right)\,.

Lemma 5.2
.

The morphism

\psi^{0}_{X_{0},X_{1}}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}({\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}},\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}\left({X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}\right))
is natural in
X_{0},X_{1}
and
similarly for
\psi^{1}_{X_{0},X_{1}}
.

Proof 5.3
.

Let
f_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(X_{i},Y_{i})
for
i=0,1
, we must show that the two following morphisms are equal:

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}\left({f_{0}}\mathrel{\&}^{\mathsf{K}}{f_{1}}\right)\mathrel{\circ}\psi^{0}_{X_{0},X_{1}}

\displaystyle=\left({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}f_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}f_{1}}\right)\,\langle{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{0}},\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}}\rangle\,\operatorname{\partial}_{{X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}}\,\oc{\left({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}{X_{0}}}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}\right)}

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=\psi_{{Y_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{Y_{1}}}^{0}\mathrel{\circ}\left({\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}f_{0}}\mathrel{\&}^{\mathsf{K}}{f_{1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}Y_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}\right)\,\left({(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}{f_{0}})\,\operatorname{\partial}_{X_{0}}}\mathrel{\&}{f_{1}}\right)\,\langle{\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{0}},\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}Y_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}\right)\,\left({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}{f_{0}}}\mathrel{\&}{f_{1}}\right)\,\left({\operatorname{\partial}_{X_{0}}}\mathrel{\&}{\oc{X_{1}}}\right)\,\langle{\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{0}},\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}}\rangle

and for this it suffices to prove that
\mathsf{pr}_{i}\,g=\mathsf{pr}_{i}\,h
for
i=0,1
. We have

\displaystyle\mathsf{pr}_{0}\,g

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}f_{0})\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{0}})\,\operatorname{\partial}_{{X_{0}}\mathrel{\&}{X_{1}}}\,\oc{\left({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}{X_{0}}}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}\right)}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}f_{0})\,\operatorname{\partial}_{X_{0}}\,\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{0}}\,\oc{\left({\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}{X_{0}}}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}\right)}\text{\hskip 10.22217ptby naturality of }\operatorname{\partial}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}f_{0})\,\operatorname{\partial}_{X_{0}}\,\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{0}}=\mathsf{pr}_{0}\,h

and, by a similar computation

\displaystyle\mathsf{pr}_{1}\,g=(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}f_{1})\,\operatorname{\partial}_{X_{1}}\,\oc{\operatorname{\iota}_{0}}\,\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}=(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}f_{1})\,\operatorname{\iota}_{0}\,\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}=\operatorname{\iota}_{0}\,f_{1}\,\oc{\mathsf{pr}_{1}}

by
3.2
and by naturality of
