We will see indeed in Section
7
that these sums can
be controlled (see Theorem
7.9
which uses
Theorem
5.67
), and even disposed of in
Section
7.3
.

6.
Completeness of the reduction rules

6.1.
A differential abstract machine

Our goal now is to define a specific reduction strategy within the
rewriting system
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
. We do this by means of an “abstract
machine” which has no environment (just as in the work of Krivine on
classical realizability). In further papers we will certainly develop
an environment machine more suitable for implementations.

So a state of our machine will consist of a term and a stack. It will
also contain an
access word
which will be a finite sequence of

0
and
1
describing which component of the output is sought:
the machine will allow terms of type
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
to be
evaluated and the word, of length
d
, specifies which leaf of the
associated tree of height
d
has to be computed by the machine.

Let us say that a type
A
is
sharp
if it is not of shape
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}
; in our type language, this is equivalent to
A=({A_{1}}\Rightarrow{\cdots{A_{k}}\Rightarrow{\iota}})
for some uniquely
determined types
A_{1},\dots,A_{k}
. Any type
A
can be written uniquely
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{E}
where
E
is sharp and
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
We use the letters
E,F,\dots
for sharp types.

We say that a term is
sum-implicit
if it does not contain the
following constructs:
0
and
M_{0}+M_{1}
.

A
word
is an element
\alpha
of
\{0,1\}^{<\omega}
, we
refer to Section
2.1
for notations concerning
words.
Then we define the stacks as follows:

\displaystyle s,t,\dots\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}()\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{arg}(M)\cdot s\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{succ}\cdot s\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{pred}\cdot s\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{if}(\alpha,M_{0},M_{1})\cdot s\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{let}(\alpha,x,M)\cdot s\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s

where
\alpha
is a word,
i\in\{0,1\}
, and
M
,
M_{0}
and
M_{1}
are
sum-implicit. Stacks are typed by judgments of shape
s:E\vdash\iota
where

E
is a sharp type.
The typing rules for stacks are given in Figure
8
.

():\iota\vdash\iota

s:\iota\vdash\iota
\mathsf{succ}\cdot s:\iota\vdash\iota

s:\iota\vdash\iota
\mathsf{pred}\cdot s:\iota\vdash\iota

s:E\vdash\iota
{}\vdash{M_{0}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{E}}
{}\vdash{M_{1}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{E}}
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}
\mathsf{if}(\delta,M_{0},M_{1})\cdot s:\iota\vdash\iota

s:E\vdash\iota
{x:\iota}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{E}}
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}
\mathsf{let}(\delta,x,M)\cdot s:\iota\vdash\iota

{}\vdash{M}:{A}
s:E\vdash\iota
\mathsf{arg}(M)\cdot s:{A}\Rightarrow{E}\vdash\iota

s:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{E}\vdash\iota
i\in\{0,1\}
\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s:{A}\Rightarrow{E}\vdash\iota

Figure 8.
Typing rules for stacks

6.1.1.
The machine

We define the states of our machine as follows:

\displaystyle c,c_{0},c_{1},\dots\mathrel{\mathord{:}\mathord{=}}(\delta,M,s)\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,0\,\,\mathord{|}\,\,c_{0}+c_{1}

where
\delta
is a word,
M
is a sum-implicit term and
s
is a
stack. The state
(\delta,M,s)
is well typed if

\displaystyle s:E\vdash\iota\text{\quad and\quad}{}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{len}(\delta)}{E}}

for some sharp type
E
(uniquely determined by
M
). The state

0
is always well typed and
c_{0}+c_{1}
is well typed if
c_{0}

and
c_{1}
are. We define a rewriting system

\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}
such that
\underline{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}
is the set of all states. A state is sum-implicit if it is not of shape
0
or
c_{1}+c_{2}
.

The associated reduction relation
\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}
is defined in
Figure
9
. It is a deterministic reduction
relation on states: determinism results from the fact that the rule to
be applied on
(\alpha,M,s)
is completely determined by the shape
of
M
(actually, by the outermost construct of
M
). Notice that
states which are not sum-implicit (that is, which are of shape
0

or
c_{0}+c_{1}
) cannot be reduced by the system
\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}
. The
associated reduction system
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})
, see
Section
2.2
, is precisely designed to reduce such
sums.

\displaystyle(\delta,({M})\,{N},s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\delta,M,\mathsf{arg}(N)\cdot s)

\displaystyle(\delta,\lambda x^{A}\,{M},\mathsf{arg}(N)\cdot s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\delta,{M}\left[{N}/{x}\right],s)

\displaystyle(\delta i,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M},s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\delta,M,\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s)

\displaystyle(\delta,\lambda x^{A}\,{M},\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\delta i,\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})},s)

\displaystyle(\delta,\mathsf{Y}M,s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\delta,M,\mathsf{arg}(\mathsf{Y}M)\cdot s)

\displaystyle(\delta,\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\delta,M,\mathsf{succ}\cdot s)

\displaystyle(\delta,\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\delta,M,\mathsf{pred}\cdot s)

\displaystyle(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{n},\mathsf{succ}\cdot s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{n+1},s)

\displaystyle(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{0},\mathsf{pred}\cdot s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{0},s)

\displaystyle(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{n+1},\mathsf{pred}\cdot s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{n},s)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\delta,\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,P_{0},P_{1}),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\delta,M,\mathsf{if}(\varepsilon,P_{0},P_{1})\cdot s)

\displaystyle(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{0},\mathsf{if}(\varepsilon,P_{0},P_{1})\cdot s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\varepsilon,P_{0},s)

\displaystyle(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{n+1},\mathsf{if}(\varepsilon,P_{0},P_{1})\cdot s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\varepsilon,P_{1},s)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\delta,\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,N,M),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\delta,N,\mathsf{let}(\varepsilon,x,N)\cdot s)

\displaystyle(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{n},\mathsf{let}(\varepsilon,x,N)\cdot s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\varepsilon,{N}\left[{\underline{n}}/{x}\right],s)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon i\delta,\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\varepsilon\delta,M,s)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon i\delta,\operatorname{\iota}_{1-i}^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}0

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\alpha\delta,\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(M),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\varepsilon\underrightarrow{\alpha}\delta,M,s)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon\delta,\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\varepsilon i\delta,M,s)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon 0\delta,\theta^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\varepsilon 00\delta,M,s)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon 1\delta,\theta^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}(\varepsilon 10\delta,M,s)

\displaystyle\hskip 51.00073pt+(\varepsilon 01\delta,M,s)

Figure 9.
Reduction rules for states. Convention:
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
,

e=\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)
.

Proposition 6.1
.

If
c\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}c^{\prime}
and
c
is a well typed state, then
c^{\prime}
is a well
typed state.

Proof 6.2
.

Since
c\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}c^{\prime}
we must have
c=(\alpha,M,s)
with
s:E\vdash\iota
and

{}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{len}(\alpha)}{E}}
. We have to consider each rewrite
rule of Figure
9
so we reason by cases on
the shape of
M
, we focus on the most interesting cases, the other
ones are similar and easier.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\mathsf{if}^{d}(N,P_{0},P_{1})
so that we must have
{}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
,
{}\vdash{P_{i}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{E}}
for
i=0,1
and hence
{}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+e}{E}}
,

s:E\vdash\iota
and

\mathsf{len}(\alpha)=e+d
so that we can write
\alpha=\varepsilon\delta
with
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
and
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
. Then we have
\mathsf{if}(\varepsilon,P_{0},P_{1})\cdot s:\iota\vdash\iota
and hence

c^{\prime}=(\delta,N,\mathsf{if}(\varepsilon,P_{0},P_{1})\cdot s)
is well typed.

\blacktriangleright
Assume
M=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{N}
so that we have
\mathsf{len}(\alpha)>0
and
{}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{len}(\alpha)-1}{E}=({A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{len}(\alpha)-1}{F}})}

for some type
A
, and
E=({A}\Rightarrow{F})
where
F
is sharp. We have

{}\vdash{M}:{({\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{len}(\alpha)}{F}})=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{len}(\alpha)}{({A}\Rightarrow{F})}}

and we can write
\alpha=\delta i
and with this notation
c^{\prime}=(\delta,N,\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s)
is well typed since
s:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{F}\vdash\iota
and hence
\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s:{A}\Rightarrow{F}\vdash\iota
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\theta^{d}(N)
then we have
{}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}}
for some type
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{E}
where
E
is sharp and
s:E\vdash\iota
. And
{}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+e+1}{E}}
and hence we can write
\alpha=\varepsilon i\delta
with
i\in\{0,1\}
,
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
and
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
. If
i=0
we have
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon 00\delta,N,s)
which is well typed since
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+e+2}{E}=\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon 00\delta)
. If
i=1
we have
c^{\prime}=c_{0}+c_{1}
with
c_{j}=(\varepsilon(1-j)j\delta,N,s)
for

j=0,1
, and
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
are well typed for the same reason.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(N)
with
i\in\{0,1\}
. Then we have
{}\vdash{N}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}}
for some type
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{E}
where
E
is
sharp,
s:E\vdash\iota
and
{}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+d+1}{E}}
. So we can
write
\alpha=\varepsilon j\delta
where
j\in\{0,1\}
. If
j\not=i
we have
c^{\prime}=0
and hence
c^{\prime}
is the sum of the empty
family of well typed states. If
j=i
then
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon\delta,N,s)
which is well typed since
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+d}{E}
.

Proposition 6.3
.

If
c
is a sum-implicit well typed state which is
\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}
-normal
then there is
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

c=(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())
.

Proof 6.4
.

We have
c=(\alpha,M,s)
with
{}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{\mathsf{len}(\alpha)}{E}}

and
s:E\vdash\iota
, we reason by cases on the last typing rule of
M

which is sum-implicit, that is, on the structure of
M
.

\blacktriangleright

M
cannot be a variable because it is closed.

\blacktriangleright
If
M=\underline{\nu}
then
E=\iota
and
\alpha=\langle\,\rangle
and hence

s:\iota\vdash\iota
. According to the typing rule for stacks,
s
must
be of one of the following shapes:
()
,
\mathsf{succ}\cdot t
,

\mathsf{pred}\cdot t
,
\mathsf{if}(\varepsilon,P_{0},P_{1})\cdot t
,
\mathsf{let}(\varepsilon,x,P)\cdot t
for
some stack
t
and the first case only is possible since
c
is
normal.

\blacktriangleright

M
cannot be
({N})\,{P}
or
M=\mathsf{Y}N
since
c
is normal.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\lambda x^{A}\,{N}
so that
E=({A}\Rightarrow{F})
and we must have
s:{A}\Rightarrow{F}\vdash\iota
. According to
the typing rule for stacks we must have
s=\mathsf{arg}(P)\cdot t
or

s=\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot t
. In both cases a reduction rule applies, contradicting
the assumption that
c
is normal. This case is impossible.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\mathsf{if}^{d}(N,P_{0},P_{1})
so that we must have
{}\vdash{N}:{\iota}
and
{}\vdash{P_{i}}:{B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{E}}
for
i=0,1
where
E
is a sharp
type, and
s:E\vdash\iota
, and
{}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+d}{E}}
. Since
c
is well typed we must have
\mathsf{len}(\alpha)=e+d
with
e=\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)
,
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
and

\alpha=\varepsilon\delta
. It follows that
c
is not normal,
reducing to
(\delta,N,\mathsf{if}(\varepsilon,P_{0},P_{1})\cdot s)
.

\blacktriangleright
The case
M=\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,P,M)
is completely similar to the previous one.

\blacktriangleright
The remaining cases are dealt with as in the proof of
Proposition
6.1
: in each case it appears
that, because
c
is well typed, it cannot be normal.

6.1.2.
Context associated with a stack, term associated with a state

Given
\delta\in\{0,1\}^{d}
,
e\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a term
M
, we define a term
\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}^{e}(M)
by
\operatorname{\pi}_{\langle{}\rangle}^{e}(M)=M
and
\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta i}^{e}(M)=\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}^{e}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}^{e}(M))
.

Given a stack
s
such that
s:E\vdash\iota
we define a context
\langle{s}\rangle
in
Figure
10
. Notice that this context is closed (it has
no free occurrences of variables). Remember that the notion of linear
context is defined in Equation (
6
).

\displaystyle\langle{()}\rangle

\displaystyle=[\ ]

\displaystyle\langle{\mathsf{succ}\cdot s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{0}([\ ])]

\displaystyle\langle{\mathsf{pred}\cdot s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{0}([\ ])]

\displaystyle\langle{\mathsf{if}(\delta,M_{0},M_{1})\cdot s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\mathsf{if}^{0}([\ ],M_{0},M_{1}))]

\displaystyle\langle{\mathsf{let}(\delta,x,M)\cdot s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\mathsf{let}^{0}(x,[\ ],M))]

\displaystyle\langle{\mathsf{arg}(M)\cdot s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{s}\rangle[({[\ ]})\,{M}]

\displaystyle\langle{\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{[\ ]})]

Figure 10.
Context associated with a stack

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
s
be a well typed state such that
s:E\vdash\iota
. Then the
context
\langle{s}\rangle
is linear and satisfies

{x:E}\vdash{\langle{s}\rangle[x]}:{\iota}
.

Proof 6.6
.

Straightforward induction on the typing derivation of
s
.

Lemma 6.7
.

If
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+e}{A}}
then
{\Gamma}\vdash{\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}^{e}(M)}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{A}}
if
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
.

The proof is straightforward. We set

\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)=\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}^{0}(M)
.
Given a state
c
of shape
c=(\delta,M,s)
with
s:E\vdash\iota
we set
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)]
. We extend this definition to all
states by

\displaystyle\langle 0\rangle

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\langle c_{0}+c_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle c_{0}\rangle+\langle c_{1}\rangle\,.

Lemma 6.8
.

If
c
is a well typed state which is not of shape
c_{0}+c_{1}

then
{}\vdash{\langle c\rangle}:{\iota}
.

Theorem 6.9
.

If
c
is a well typed state and
c\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}c^{\prime}
then
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

Proof 6.10
.

We must have
c=(\delta,Q,s)
with
s:E\vdash\iota
and
{}\vdash{Q}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{E}}
where
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
so that
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(Q)]
and we reason by considering
the various cases in the transition
c\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}c^{\prime}
as listed in
Figure
9
.

\blacktriangleright

Q=({M})\,{N}
so that
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(({M})\,{N})]
, and
c^{\prime}=(\delta,M,\mathsf{arg}(N)\cdot s)
and hence
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{\mathsf{arg}(N)\cdot s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)]=\langle{s}\rangle[({\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)})\,{N}]
.
We have
\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(({M})\,{N})\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}({\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)})\,{N}
(in
\mathsf{len}(\delta)
steps)
and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
since
\langle{s}\rangle
is an evaluation context.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\lambda x^{A}\,{M}
and
s=\mathsf{arg}(N)\cdot t
so that
\langle c\rangle=\langle\mathsf{arg}(N)\cdot t\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\lambda x^{A}\,{M})]=\langle t\rangle[({\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\lambda x^{A}\,{M})})\,{N}]
. And
c^{\prime}=(\delta,{M}\left[{N}/{x}\right],t)
so that
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle t\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}({M}\left[{N}/{x}\right])]
. We have

\displaystyle({\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\lambda x^{A}\,{M})})\,{N}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}({\lambda x^{A}\,{\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)}})\,{N}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}{\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)}\left[{N}/{x}\right]=\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}({M}\left[{N}/{x}\right])

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}
and
\delta=\varepsilon i
so that
\langle c\rangle=\langle s\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon i}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M})]
. And
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon,M,\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s)
so that
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M)]=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M)})]
. We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon i}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M)})

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\lambda x^{A}\,{M}
and
s=\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot t
with
i\in\{0,1\}
so that we have
\langle c\rangle=\langle{\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot t}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\lambda x^{A}\,{M})]=\langle{t}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{(}{\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\lambda x^{A}\,{M})}))]
. And

c^{\prime}=(\delta i,\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})},t)
, so
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{t}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta i}(\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})})]
. We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{(}{\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\lambda x^{A}\,{M})}))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}^{1}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{(}{\lambda x^{A}\,{M}})))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{(}{\lambda x^{A}\,{M}})))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})}))=\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta i}(\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})})

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\mathsf{Y}M
so that
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\mathsf{Y}M)]
and

\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{\mathsf{arg}(\mathsf{Y}M)\cdot s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)]=\langle{s}\rangle[({\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)})\,{\mathsf{Y}M}]
. We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\mathsf{Y}M)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(({M})\,{\mathsf{Y}M})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}({\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)})\,{\mathsf{Y}M}

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M)
with
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
, so
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M))]
. And
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{\mathsf{succ}\cdot s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M)]=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{0}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M))]
. We have

\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M))\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{0}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\delta}(M))
in
d
steps
and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright
The case
Q=\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(M)
is similar and the cases
Q=\underline{n}
are
straightforward (and then
\delta=\langle\,\rangle
and there are several
cases to consider as to the shape of
s
, they are all easy).

\blacktriangleright

Q=\mathsf{if}^{e}(M,P_{0},P_{1})
and
\delta=\eta\varepsilon
with
e=\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)
. We have
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\eta\varepsilon}(\mathsf{if}^{e}(M,P_{0},P_{1}))]
and
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle\mathsf{if}(\eta,P_{0},P_{1})\cdot s\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M)]=\langle s\rangle[\operatorname{\pi}_{\eta}(\mathsf{if}^{0}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M),P_{0},P_{1}))]
. We have

\operatorname{\pi}_{\eta\varepsilon}(\mathsf{if}^{e}(M,P_{0},P_{1}))=\operatorname{\pi}_{\eta}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{if}^{e}(M,P_{0},P_{1})))\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\pi}_{\eta}(\mathsf{if}^{0}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M),P_{0},P_{1}))

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright
The case
Q=\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,N,M)
is similar.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{e}(M)
and
\delta=\alpha i\varepsilon
so that
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha i\varepsilon}(\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{e}(M))}]
and
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha\varepsilon}(M)}]
. We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha i\varepsilon}(\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{e}(M))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{e}(M))))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha}(\operatorname{\pi}_{i}(\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{0}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M))))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M))=\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha\varepsilon}(M)

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{e}(M)
and
\delta=\alpha{(1-i)}\varepsilon
so that
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha{(1-i)}\varepsilon}(\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{e}(M))}]
and
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle 0\rangle=0
. We have

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha(1-i)\varepsilon}(\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{e}(M))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha}(\operatorname{\pi}_{1-i}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{e}(M))))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha}(\operatorname{\pi}_{1-i}(\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{0}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M))))\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}0

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e}_{l}(M)
and
\delta=\alpha i_{l+1}\cdots i_{0}\varepsilon
with
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
.
Then
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha i_{l+1}\cdots i_{0}\varepsilon}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e}_{l}(M))}]
and
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha i_{0}i_{l+1}\cdots i_{1}\varepsilon}(M)}]
.
Next observe that

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha i_{l+1}\cdots i_{0}\varepsilon}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{e}_{l}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha i_{l+1}\cdots i_{0}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{0}_{l}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M)))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha i_{0}i_{l+1}\cdots i_{1}}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M))=\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha i_{0}i_{l+1}\cdots i_{1}\varepsilon}(M)

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\theta^{e}(M)
and
\delta=\alpha 0\varepsilon
with
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
.
Then we have
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 0\varepsilon}(\theta^{e}(M))}]
and
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 00\varepsilon}(M)}]
. And

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 0\varepsilon}(\theta^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha}(\operatorname{\pi}_{0}(\theta^{0}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M))))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 00\varepsilon}(M)

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

\blacktriangleright

Q=\theta^{e}(M)
and
\delta=\alpha 1\varepsilon
with
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
.
Then we have
\langle c\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 1\varepsilon}(\theta^{e}(M))}]
and
\langle c^{\prime}\rangle=\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 10\varepsilon}(M)}]+\langle{s}\rangle[{\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 01\varepsilon}(M)}]
.
And

\displaystyle\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 1\varepsilon}(\theta^{e}(M))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha}(\operatorname{\pi}_{1}(\theta^{0}(\operatorname{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(M))))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 10\varepsilon}(M)+\operatorname{\pi}_{\alpha 01\varepsilon}(M)

and hence
\langle c\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}^{\ast}\langle c^{\prime}\rangle
.

For
C=[{c_{1},\dots,c_{k}}]\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\underline{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}})
we define
\langle C\rangle=[{\langle c_{1}\rangle,\dots,\langle c_{k}\rangle}]\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\underline{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}})
. We say
that
C
is well typed if
c_{1},\dots,c_{k}
are well typed.

Theorem 6.11
.

If
C\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\underline{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}})
is well typed and
C\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}}C^{\prime}
then
\langle C\rangle\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})}}\langle C^{\prime}\rangle
.

This is an immediate corollary of Theorem
6.9
and
of the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})
and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})
follow the
same pattern. Let us say that

C=[{c_{1},\dots,c_{k}}]\in\underline{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}
is
\mathcal{L}
-summable if

C
is well typed and the family of terms

(\langle c_{1}\rangle,\dots,\langle c_{k}\rangle)
is
\mathcal{L}
-summable (see
Definition
5.65
).

Theorem 6.12
.

If
C\in\underline{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}
is
\mathcal{L}
-summable and

C\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}}C^{\prime}
then
C^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{L}
-summable.

Proof 6.13
.

Use Theorem
5.67
.

6.2.
Soundness

Let
s
be a stack such that
s:E\vdash\iota
and a variable
x
we
know that
\langle{s}\rangle[x]
is a term such that
{x:E}\vdash{\langle{s}\rangle[x]}:{\iota}
.
So we can define the semantics of
s
by

\llbracket{s}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\langle{s}\rangle[x]}\rrbracket_{x:E}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}(\llbracket{E}\rrbracket,\mathsf{N})
.

Lemma 6.14
.

If
s:E\vdash\iota
then
\llbracket{s}\rrbracket
is linear.

This is due to the fact that the context
\langle{s}\rangle
is always
linear.
The proof is straightforward.

If
c
is a well typed state, we set

\llbracket{c}\rrbracket=\llbracket{\langle c\rangle}\rrbracket\in\mathcal{L}(1,\mathsf{N})
and if
C=[{c_{1},\dots,c_{k}}]\in\underline{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}
is
\mathcal{L}
-summable we
set
\llbracket{C}\rrbracket=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\in\llbracket{c_{i}}\rrbracket
which is a well defined
element of
\mathcal{L}(1,\mathsf{N})
by definition of
\mathcal{L}
-summability.

Theorem 6.15
.

If
C\in\underline{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}
is
\mathcal{L}
-summable and
C\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}}C^{\prime}
then
\llbracket{C^{\prime}}\rrbracket=\llbracket{C}\rrbracket
.

This makes sense by Theorem
6.12
which
entails that
C^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{L}
-summable. The proof is a
straightforward application of the definition of

\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}
.

6.3.
Intersection typing system for terms

We present the interpretation of terms in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
(see
Section
3.3
) as a deduction system so as to make the
statement and the proofs of our normalization result and of the lemmas
it uses (see Section
6.3.3
) more readable and
natural.
Indeed these proofs are most naturally presented as proofs by
induction on derivations in this typing system.
The main idea is to consider the elements of the sets interpreting
types as intersection types, which are non-idempotent since we use
multisets and not sets in the definition of
\oc{X}
.

6.3.1.
Complements about
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}

Before describing the relational interpretation of types and terms, we
need to complement Section
3.3
with a few words about
the generalized flip of Lemma
5.27
,
the additive strength and the basic operations on integers in

\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
.

For
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the generalized flip of length
l+2
is
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(l)=\{((\alpha,a),(\underrightarrow{\alpha},a))\mid\alpha\in\mathbb{D}^{l+2}\text{ and }a\in X\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}^{l+2}X,\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}^{l+2}X)
. We prove this property this by induction on
l
. For
l=0
this is due to the fact that
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(0)=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}
. Assume that this property holds for
l
and remember that by definition
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(l+1)=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(l))
. We have
\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(l)=\{(r\alpha,a),(r\underrightarrow{\alpha},a))\mid r\in\mathbb{D},\ a\in X\text{ and }\alpha\in\mathbb{D}^{l+2}\}
and hence

\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}_{\mathbb{D}}\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(l))=\{((r\alpha,a),(\underrightarrow{r\alpha},a)\mid r\in\mathbb{D},\alpha\in\mathbb{D}^{l+1}\text{ and }a\in X\}=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(l+1)
.

The additive strength (see Section
5.1.1
) of the
monad
\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}
on
{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}
is
\psi^{i}_{X_{0},\dots,X_{n}}\in{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\oc}(X_{0}\mathrel{\&}\cdots\mathrel{\&}\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}X_{i}\mathrel{\&}\cdots\mathrel{\&}X_{n},\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}(X_{0}\mathrel{\&}\cdots\mathrel{\&}X_{n})
given by

\displaystyle\psi^{i}_{X_{0},\dots,X_{n}}

\displaystyle=\{([{(j,a)}],(0,j,a))\mid j\in\{0,\dots,n\}\setminus\{i\}\text{ and }a\in X_{j}\}

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\cup\{([{(i,0,a)}],(0,i,a))\mid a\in X_{i}\}

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\cup\{([{(i,1,a)}],(1,i,a))\mid a\in X_{i}\}\,.

Using the notations of Section
5.4
we set

\mathsf{N}={\mathbb{N}}
and then
\overline{\mathsf{zero}}=\{(\ast,0)\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(1,\mathsf{N})
,
\overline{\mathsf{suc}}=\{(\nu,\nu+1)\mid n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{N})
and
19
19

19

To avoid confusions with integers used as indices or
multiplicities, we use Greek letters
\kappa,\nu
to denote
numerals.

\overline{\mathsf{pred}}=\{(0,0)\}\cup\{(\nu+1,\nu)\mid\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
.
The
\oc
-coalgebra structure of
\mathsf{N}
is given by
h_{\mathsf{N}}=\{(\nu,k[{\nu}])\mid k,\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\mathsf{N},\oc{\mathsf{N}})
.
Given a set
X
, the morphism
\overline{\mathsf{let}}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}({\mathsf{N}}\otimes{({\oc{\mathsf{N}}}\multimap{X})},X)
is given by
\overline{\mathsf{let}}=\{(\nu,(k[{\nu}],a),a)\mid\nu,k\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{ and }a\in X\}
and the morphism
\overline{\mathsf{if}}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{N}\otimes({X}\mathrel{\&}{X}),X)
is given by
\overline{\mathsf{if}}=\{(0,(0,a),a)\mid a\in X\}\cup\{(\nu+1,(1,a),a)\mid a\in X\}
.

6.3.2.
The interpretation in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
as a deduction system

The interpretation
\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
of a type
A
in
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
is given by
\llbracket{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}\iota}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}=\mathbb{D}^{d}\times{\mathbb{N}}
and
\llbracket{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}=\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})\times\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
.
We consider the elements of
\mathbb{D}^{d}
as sequences of length
d
of elements of

\mathbb{D}={1}\mathrel{\&}{1}=\{0,1\}
, that is, as sequences of bits of
length
d
.
Given
\delta\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
a\in\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
one defines
\delta\cdot a\in\llbracket{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
by induction on
A
: if
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}\iota
then
a=(\varepsilon,\nu)
where
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{D}^{e}
and we set
\delta\cdot a=(\delta\varepsilon,\nu)\in\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+e}\iota
, and if
A=({B}\Rightarrow{C})
then
a=(p,c)
where
p\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})
and
c\in\llbracket{C}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
and we set
\delta\cdot a=(p,\delta\cdot c)\in\llbracket{{B}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}=\llbracket{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{({B}\Rightarrow{C})}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
.
Any type
A
can be written uniquely
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}
where
F
is sharp, and then any element
a\in\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
can be
written uniquely
a=\delta\cdot f
where
\delta\in\mathbb{D}^{d}
and
f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
.

An intersection typing context is a sequence
\Phi=(x_{1}:m_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:m_{n}:A_{n})
where the
x_{i}
’s are pairwise distinct variables, and
m_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})
for each
i
. Then we use
\underline{\Phi}
for the underlying typing context defined
as
\underline{\Phi}=(x_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:A_{n})
. If
\underline{\Phi}=\underline{\Phi^{\prime}}
, so that

\Phi=(x_{1}:m_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:m_{n}:A_{n})
and
\Phi^{\prime}=(x_{1}:m^{\prime}_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:m^{\prime}_{n}:A_{n})
, then we set
\Phi+\Phi^{\prime}=(x_{1}:m_{1}+m^{\prime}_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:m_{n}+m^{\prime}_{n}:A_{n})
and when we use this notation we always implicitly assume that all
the intersection typing contexts involved have the same underlying
typing context. Given a typing context
\Gamma=(x_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:A_{n})
we set
0_{\Gamma}=(x_{1}:[{\,}]:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:[{\,}]:A_{n})
.

The intersection typing rules are given in
Figure
11
.

(
i-var
)

0_{\Gamma},x:[{a}]:A\vdash x:a:A

(
i-abs
)

\Phi,x:m:A\vdash M:b:B
\Phi\vdash\lambda x^{A}\,{M}:(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}

(
i-app
)

\Phi^{0}\vdash M:([{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}],b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}
(\Phi^{j}\vdash N:a_{j}:A)_{j=1}^{n}
\sum_{j=0}^{n}\Phi^{j}\vdash({M})\,{N}:b:B

(
i-fix
)

\Phi^{0}\vdash M:([{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}],a):{A}\Rightarrow{A}
(\Phi^{j}\vdash\mathsf{Y}M:a_{j}:A)_{j=1}^{n}
\sum_{j=0}^{n}\Phi^{j}\vdash\mathsf{Y}M:a:A

(
i-num
)

\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
0_{\Gamma}\vdash\underline{\nu}:\nu:\iota

(
i-suc
)

\Phi\vdash M:\delta\cdot\nu:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\Phi\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M):\delta\cdot(\nu+1):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}

(
i-prd
0
)

\Phi\vdash M:\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\Phi\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(M):\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}

(
i-prd
+
)

\Phi\vdash M:\delta\cdot(\nu+1):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\Phi\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(M):\delta\cdot\nu:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}

(
i-if
0
)

\Phi^{0}\vdash M:\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
\Phi^{1}\vdash P:a:A
{\underline{\Phi^{0}}}\vdash{Q}:{A}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\Phi^{0}+\Phi^{1}\vdash\mathsf{if}_{A}^{d}(M,P,Q):\delta\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}

(
i-if
+
)

\Phi^{0}\vdash M:\delta\cdot(\nu+1):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
{\underline{\Phi^{0}}}\vdash{P}:{A}
\Phi^{1}\vdash Q:a:A
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\Phi^{0}+\Phi^{1}\vdash\mathsf{if}_{A}^{d}(M,P,Q):\delta\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}

(
i-proj
)

\Phi\vdash M:\delta r\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{A}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\Phi\vdash\operatorname{\pi}_{r}^{d}(M):\delta\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}

(
i-add
)

\Phi\vdash M_{i}:a:A
{\underline{\Phi}}\vdash{M_{0}+M_{1}}:{A}
i\in\mathbb{D}
\Phi\vdash M_{0}+M_{1}:a:A

(
i-inj
)

\Phi\vdash M:\delta\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
r\in\mathbb{D}
\Phi\vdash\operatorname{\iota}_{r}^{d}(M):\delta r\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{A}

(
i-sum
)

\Phi\vdash M:\delta r_{0}r_{1}\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{A}
r,r_{0},r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }r=r_{0}+r_{1}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\Phi\vdash\theta^{d}(M):\delta r\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{A}

(
i-diff
)

\Phi\vdash M:(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}
r\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }(m^{\prime},(r,m))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket}
\Phi\vdash\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}:(m^{\prime},r\cdot b):{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}

(
i-circ
)

\Phi\vdash M:\delta\alpha\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+l+2}{A}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d,\ \mathsf{len}(\alpha)=l+2
\Phi\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(M):\delta\underrightarrow{\alpha}\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+l+2}{A}

(
i-let
)

\Phi^{0}\vdash M:\delta\cdot\nu:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
\Phi^{1},x:k[{\nu}]:\iota\vdash N:b:B
k,\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}\text{ and }\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\Phi^{0}+\Phi^{1}\vdash\mathsf{let}_{B}^{d}(x,M,N):\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}

Figure 11.
Intersection typing rules for terms

():\nu:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota

s:\kappa+1:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota
\mathsf{succ}\cdot s:\kappa:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota

s:0:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota
\mathsf{pred}\cdot s:0:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota

s:\kappa:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota
\mathsf{pred}\cdot s:\kappa+1:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota

s:f:F\vdash\nu:\iota
\vdash M_{0}:\delta\cdot f:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}
{}\vdash{M_{1}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\mathsf{if}(\delta,M_{0},M_{1})\cdot s:0:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota

s:f:F\vdash\nu:\iota
{}\vdash{M_{0}}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}}
\vdash M_{1}:\delta\cdot f:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\mathsf{if}(\delta,M_{0},M_{1})\cdot s:\kappa+1:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota

s:f:F\vdash\nu:\iota
x:k[{\kappa}]:\iota\vdash M:\delta\cdot f:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
\mathsf{let}(\delta,x,M)\cdot s:\kappa:\iota\vdash\nu:\iota

(\vdash M:a_{i}:A)_{i=1}^{k}
s:f:F\vdash\nu:\iota
\mathsf{arg}(M)\cdot s:([{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}],f):{A}\Rightarrow{F}\vdash\nu:\iota

s:(m^{\prime},f):{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{F}\vdash\nu:\iota
r\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }(m^{\prime},(r,m))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket}
\mathsf{D}(r)\cdot s:(m,f):{A}\Rightarrow{F}\vdash\nu:\iota

\vdash M:\delta\cdot f:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}
s:f:F\vdash\nu:\iota
\vdash(\delta,M,s):\nu:\iota

Figure 12.
Intersection typing rules for stacks and states

We also define an intersection typing system for stacks in
Figure
12
. An intersection stack typing
judgment is an expression
s:f:F\vdash\nu:\iota
where
s
is a stack,
F
is a sharp type,

f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
and
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
The intuition is that
s
produces
\nu
if it receives
f
as a
linear argument.

The main feature of this system is the following result which is
obtained by a simple induction structured exactly as the proof of
Theorem
5.55
, and expresses that the rules of
Figure
12
are a simple reformulation of
the denotational interpretation of Figure
7
in the
case of the model
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
.

Theorem 6.16
.

For any term
M
such that
{x_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:A_{n}}\vdash{M}:{B}
and any
(m_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}}))_{i=1}^{n}
and
b\in\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
, the two
following properties are equivalent.

•

(m_{1},\dots,m_{n},b)\in\llbracket{M}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\Gamma}

•

the judgment

x_{1}:m_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:m_{n}:A_{n}\vdash M:b:B
is provable in the system of Figure
11
.

For any stack
s
such that
s:F\vdash\iota
and any
f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
one has
(f,n)\in\llbracket{s}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
iff
s:f:F\vdash n:\iota
. For any well-typed state
c
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
one has
\vdash c:n:\iota
iff
n\in\llbracket{c}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
.

Of course
\llbracket{M}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\Gamma}
is the interpretation of
M
in

\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
, and similarly for stacks and states.

Given a typing context
\Gamma=(x_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{n}:A_{n})
and
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
we define
\operatorname{\partial}(j,\Gamma)
as the set of all triples
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)
where
r\in\mathbb{D}
,
\underline{\Phi}=\underline{\Phi^{\prime}}=\Gamma
and, if we set
\Phi=(x_{i}:m_{i}:A_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and
\Phi^{\prime}=(x_{i}:m^{\prime}_{i}:A^{\prime}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}

then we have

\displaystyle\begin{cases}A^{\prime}_{i}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}_{i}\text{ and }(m^{\prime}_{j},(r,m_{j}))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{\llbracket{A_{j}}\rrbracket}&\\
A^{\prime}_{i}=A_{i}\text{ and }m^{\prime}_{i}=m_{i}&\text{ if }i\not=j\,.\end{cases}

By Lemma
5.61
we have

\llbracket{\partial({x},{M})}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\Gamma,x:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}=\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}_{1}\llbracket{M}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\Gamma,x:A}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{\oc}({\llbracket{\Gamma}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}}\mathrel{\&}{\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}}},\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}{\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})\,.

If we rephrase this property in the model
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
, we get the
following.

Theorem 6.17
.

Assume that
\Phi\vdash M:b:B
and

(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(i,\Gamma)
. Then

\Phi^{\prime}\vdash\partial({x_{i}},{M}):r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}
.

Proof 6.18
.

By Theorem
6.16
.

We set
\partial^{0}({x},{M})=M
and

\partial^{d+1}({x},{M})=\partial({x},{\partial^{d}({x},{M})})
.

6.3.3.
Normalization

Given
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
we define
\Bot(\nu)
as the set of all well-typed states
c
such that
[{c}]\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}^{\ast}}C+[{(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())}]
for some
C\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\underline{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}})
.

With any type
A
and
a\in\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
, we associate a set
|a|_{A}
of terms
M
such that
{}\vdash{M}:{A}
.
If
F
is a sharp type,
f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
and
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
we also
define a set
\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
of stacks
s
such that
s:F\vdash\iota
.
The definition is by mutual induction on types, and more precisely on
the number of “
{}\Rightarrow{}
” in types. Remember that
\overline{\delta}
is
the reversed word of
\delta
.

•

|\delta\cdot f|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}}=\{M\mid{}\vdash{M}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{F}}\text{ and }\forall\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}\,\forall s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}\ (\overline{\delta},M,s)\in\Bot(\nu)\}

•

\|\kappa\vdash\nu\|_{\iota}=\{s\mid s:\iota\vdash\iota\text{ and }(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\kappa},s)\in\Bot(\nu)\}

•

let
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})
and
f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
, then

\displaystyle\|(m,f)\vdash\nu\|_{{A}\Rightarrow{F}}

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{D}(r_{1})\cdot\cdots\mathsf{D}(r_{d})\cdot\mathsf{arg}(M)\cdot s\mid d\in{\mathbb{N}},\,r_{1},\dots,r_{d}\in\mathbb{D},

\displaystyle\quad\quad\exists(m_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{i}{A}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}}))_{i=0}^{d}\ m_{0}=m

\displaystyle\quad\quad\text{and }((m_{i},(r_{i},m_{i-1}))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{\llbracket{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{i-1}{A}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})_{i=1}^{d},

\displaystyle\quad\quad M\in|m_{d}|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}\text{ and }s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}\}.

In this definition, we use the following notation: if

m=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}]\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})
with
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
, then
|m|_{A}=\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}|a_{j}|_{A}
.

Given an intersection typing context
\Phi=(x_{i}:m_{i}:A_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
we
define
|\Phi|
as the set of all substitutions
\vec{P}=(P_{1}/x_{1},\dots,P_{k}/x_{k})
where
(P_{i}\in|m_{i}|_{A_{i}})_{i=1}^{k}
. Given also
b\in\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
we define a set
|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n)}
of terms
M
such that
{\underline{\Phi}}\vdash{M}:{B}
, the definition is by induction on
n
:

\displaystyle|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\{M\mid{\underline{\Phi}}\vdash{M}:{B}\text{ and }\forall\vec{P}\in|\Phi|\ {M}[{\vec{P}}]\in|b|_{B}\}

\displaystyle|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=\{M\mid{\underline{\Phi}}\vdash{M}:{B}\text{ and }\forall r\in\mathbb{D}\,\forall i\in\{1,\dots,k\},

\displaystyle\hskip 60.00009pt\forall\Phi^{\prime}\ (\Phi^{\prime},(r,\Phi))\in\operatorname{\partial}(i,\underline{\Phi})\Rightarrow\partial({x_{i}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}|^{(n)}\}

and last we set

\displaystyle|\Phi\vdash b:B|=\bigcap_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n)}\,.

Remark 6.19
.

Given
M
,
\Gamma
and
B
such that
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{B}
,
saying that
M\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n)}
for all
\Phi
such that

\underline{\Phi}=\Gamma
and all
b\in\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
intuitively means
that
M
is
n
times differentiable with respect to its free
variables, and
M\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|
for all
\Phi
and
b

means that
M
is infinitely differentiable. This definition bears
some similarity with the definition of
C^{\infty}
maps in
Analysis.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
X
be a set, let
r\in\mathbb{D}
and let
(m^{\prime},(r,m))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{X}
. Let
m_{1},\dots,m_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X})
be such that
m=m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k}
. There are
r_{1},\dots,r_{k}\in\mathbb{D}
and
m^{\prime}_{1},\dots,m^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({X})

such that
r=r_{1}+\cdots+r_{k}
,
m^{\prime}=m^{\prime}_{1}+\cdots+m^{\prime}_{k}
and
((m^{\prime}_{i},(r_{i},m_{i}))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{X})_{i=1}^{k}
.

Proof 6.21
.

Categorically this is a simple consequence of the fact that the
morphism
\tilde{\partial}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(\mathbb{D},\oc{\mathbb{D}})
of
Section
3.3
is a
\oc
-coalgebra structure, and
can also be checked directly as we do now. Setting

m=[{a_{j}\mid j\in J}]
, the assumption
(m^{\prime},(r,m))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{X}
means that
m^{\prime}=[{(r^{\prime}_{j},a_{j})\mid j\in J}]
with
(r^{\prime}_{j}\in\mathbb{D})_{j=1}^{k}

and
r=\sum_{j\in J}r^{\prime}_{j}
. Since
m=m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k}
we can find
pairwise disjoint
(J_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
with
J=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}J_{i}

and
m_{i}=[{a_{j}\mid j\in J_{i}}]
. Then we take

m^{\prime}_{i}=[{(r^{\prime}_{j},a_{j})\mid j\in J_{i}}]
and

r_{i}=\sum_{j\in J_{i}}r^{\prime}_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,k
. We have

r_{i}\in\mathbb{D}
because
\sum_{j\in J}r^{\prime}_{j}=r\in\mathbb{D}
.

Now we prove a series of lemmas (from
6.23
to
6.57
)
which express that the sets

|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n)}
have some stability properties with respect to
the syntactic constructs of the language. They will make the proof of
Theorem
6.59
essentially trivial.

Remark 6.22
.

These lemmas are proven by induction on
n
(the superscript in

|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n)}
) and it is essential to notice that the
statement we prove by induction on
n
is
universally
quantified
on
\Phi
,
B
and
b
because, when proving the
implication for
M
, we have to apply the inductive hypothesis to

\partial({x},{M})
for all the variables
x
in the context.

Lemma 6.23
.

If

M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\alpha\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+h+2}{B}|^{(n)}
with
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
and
h+2=\mathsf{len}(\alpha)
then we have
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{h}(M)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\underrightarrow{\alpha}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+h}{B}|^{(n)}
.

Proof 6.24
.

By induction on
n
. For
n=0
: let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|

and let us set
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
. We can write
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
where
F
is sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f
where
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
and

f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
, we have

\displaystyle(\overline{\delta\underrightarrow{\alpha}\varepsilon},\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{h}(M^{\prime}),s)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\varepsilon}\underleftarrow{(\overline{\alpha})}\overline{\delta},\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{h}(M^{\prime}),s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon}\underrightarrow{\underleftarrow{(\overline{\alpha})}}\overline{\delta},M^{\prime},s)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\delta\alpha\varepsilon},M^{\prime},s)\in\Bot(\nu)

since
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\alpha\varepsilon\cdot f:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+h+e+2}{F}|^{(0)}
.

For the inductive step, we assume that the implication holds for

n
and we prove it for
n+1
so assume that
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\alpha\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+h+2}{B}|^{(n+1)}
and let
r\in\mathbb{D}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\Phi)\}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
be such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
, we have

\partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\delta\alpha\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+h+3}{b}|^{(n)}

and hence, by inductive hypothesis,
\partial({x_{l}},{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{h}(M)})=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d+1}_{h}(\partial({x_{l}},{M}))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\delta\underrightarrow{\alpha}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+h+3}{b}|^{(n)}
. Since we have this property for all
l
and
r,\Phi^{\prime}
such that

(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
, we have proven
that
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{h}(M)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\underrightarrow{\alpha}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+h+2}{B}|^{(n+1)}
as required.

Lemma 6.25
.

If
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}|^{(n)}
and

r\in\mathbb{D}
then
\operatorname{\iota}_{r}^{d}(M)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}|^{(n)}
where
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
.

Proof 6.26
.

By induction on
n
. For
n=0
: let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|

and let us set
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
. We can write
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
where
F
is sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f
where
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
and

f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
, we have
(\overline{\delta r\varepsilon},\operatorname{\iota}_{r}^{d}(M^{\prime}),s)=(\overline{\varepsilon}r\overline{\delta},\operatorname{\iota}_{r}^{d}(M^{\prime}),s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon}\overline{\delta},M^{\prime},s)=(\overline{\delta\varepsilon},M^{\prime},s)\in\Bot(\nu)
by our assumption about
M
, hence
\operatorname{\iota}_{r}^{d}(M^{\prime})\in|\delta r\cdot b|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}}

as required.

For the inductive step we assume that
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}|^{(n+1)}
. Let
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\Phi)\}
, and
\Phi^{\prime}
be
such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r^{\prime},\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
. We have
\partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r^{\prime}\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}|^{(n)}
and hence, by inductive hypothesis,
\partial({x_{l}},{\operatorname{\iota}_{r}^{d}(M)})=\operatorname{\iota}_{r}^{d+1}(\partial({x_{l}},{M}))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r^{\prime}\delta r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}
since
\mathsf{len}(r^{\prime}\delta)=d+1
.

Lemma 6.27
.

If
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r_{0}r_{1}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}
with
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
then
\theta^{d}(M)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}
if
r=r_{0}+r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}
.

Proof 6.28
.

By induction on
n
. For
n=0
: let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|

and let us set
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
. We can write
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
where
F
is sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f
where
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
and

f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
. Assume first
r=0
so that
r_{0}=r_{1}=0
,
we have
(\overline{\delta 0\varepsilon},\theta^{d}(M^{\prime}),s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\delta}00\varepsilon,M^{\prime},s)\in\Bot(\nu)
by our assumption about
M
, hence
\theta^{d}(M^{\prime})\in|\delta 0\cdot b|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}}

as required. Assume now
r=1
, we have

(\overline{\delta 1\varepsilon},\theta^{d}(M^{\prime}),s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\delta 01\varepsilon},M^{\prime},s)+(\overline{\delta 10\varepsilon},M^{\prime},s)
and since one of these summands belongs to
\Bot(\nu)
by our assumption about
M
, we get

(\overline{\delta 1\varepsilon},\theta^{d}(M^{\prime}),s)\in\Bot(\nu)
.

For the inductive step we assume that
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r_{0}r_{1}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n+1)}
. Let
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\Phi)\}
, and
\Phi^{\prime}
be
such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r^{\prime},\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
. We have
\partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r^{\prime}\delta r_{0}r_{1}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+3}{B}|^{(n)}
and hence, by inductive hypothesis,
\partial({x_{l}},{\theta^{d}(M)})=\theta^{d+1}(\partial({x_{l}},{M}))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r^{\prime}\delta r_{0}r_{1}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+3}{B}|^{(n)}
since
\mathsf{len}(r^{\prime}\delta)=d+1
.

Lemma 6.29
.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
r\in\mathbb{D}
and
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}|^{(n)}

then
\operatorname{\pi}_{r}^{d}(M)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}|^{(n)}

Proof 6.30
.

By induction on
n
. For
n=0
: let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|

and let us set
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
. We can write
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
where
F
is sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f
where
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
and

f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
, we have
(\overline{\delta\varepsilon},\operatorname{\pi}_{r}^{d}(M^{\prime}),s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\delta r\varepsilon},M^{\prime},s)\in\Bot(\nu)
by our assumption about
M
, hence
\operatorname{\pi}_{r}^{d}(M^{\prime})\in|\delta\cdot b|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}}

as required.

For the inductive step we assume that
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}|^{(n+1)}
. Let
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\Phi)\}
, and
\Phi^{\prime}
be
such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r^{\prime},\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
. We have
\partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r^{\prime}\delta r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}
and hence, by inductive hypothesis,
\partial({x_{l}},{\operatorname{\pi}_{r}^{d}(M)})=\operatorname{\pi}_{r}^{d+1}(\partial({x_{l}},{M}))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r^{\prime}\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{B}|^{(n)}
since
\mathsf{len}(r^{\prime}\delta)=d+1
.

Lemma 6.31
.

If
M\in|\Phi\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|^{(n)}
and
(m^{\prime},(r,m))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}}
then
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}\in|\Phi\vdash(m^{\prime},r\cdot b):{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}|^{(n)}
.

Proof 6.32
.

By induction on
n
. For
n=0
: let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|

and let us set
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
. We can write
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
where
F
is sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f
where
\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)=e
and

f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Let
s\in\|(m^{\prime},f)\vdash\nu\|_{{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{F}}
, we have
\mathsf{D}(r)\cdot s\in\|(m,f)\vdash\nu\|_{{A}\Rightarrow{F}}
since
(m^{\prime},(r,m))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}}
, and hence
(\overline{\varepsilon},M^{\prime},\mathsf{D}(r)\cdot s)\in\Bot(\nu)
and therefore
(\overline{r\varepsilon},\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M^{\prime}},s)\in\Bot(\nu)
since
(\overline{r\varepsilon},\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M^{\prime}},s)=(\overline{\varepsilon}r,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M^{\prime}},s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon},M^{\prime},\mathsf{D}(r)\cdot s)
. It follows that
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M^{\prime}}\in|(m^{\prime},r\varepsilon\cdot F)|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e+1}{F}}

as required.

For the inductive step, assume that
M\in|\Phi\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|^{(n+1)}
and let
r^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\Phi)\}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
be such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r^{\prime},\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
, we have

\partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash(m,r^{\prime}\cdot b):{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}|^{(n)}
and hence, by inductive hypothesis,
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})}\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash(m^{\prime},rr^{\prime}\cdot b):{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{B}}|^{(n)}
since
(m^{\prime},(r,m))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}}
, and hence
\partial({x_{l}},{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash(m^{\prime},r^{\prime}r\cdot b):{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{B}}|^{(n)}
by Lemma
6.23
. Since we have this property for all

l
and
r^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime}
such that

(\Phi^{\prime},r^{\prime},\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
, we have proven that
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}\in|\Phi\vdash(m^{\prime},r\cdot b)):{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}|^{(n+1)}
as required.

Lemma 6.33
.

Given
\Phi=(x_{1}:m_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{k}:m_{k}:A_{k})
and
l\in\{1,\dots,k\}
such that
m_{j}=[\,]
if
j\not=l
and
m_{l}=[{a}]
, one has
x_{l}\in|\Phi\vdash a:A|^{(n)}
.

Proof 6.34
.

By induction on
n
. For
n=0
, let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|

so that
P_{l}\in|a|_{A_{l}}
so that
x_{l}\in|\Phi\vdash a:A_{l}|^{(0)}
. We prove the property for

n+1
assuming that it holds for
n
. So with the notations of
the lemma we must prove that
x_{l}\in|\Phi\vdash a:A_{l}|^{(n+1)}
. Let

j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
r\in\mathbb{D}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
be such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(j,\underline{\Phi})
. We set

\Phi^{\prime}=(x_{p}:m^{\prime}_{p}:A^{\prime}_{p})_{p=1}^{k}
, we must prove that
\partial({x_{j}},{x_{l}})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A_{l}}|^{(n)}
. There are two cases.

•

If
j\not=l
then
m_{j}=[\,]
. We have
m^{\prime}_{j}=[\,]
and
A^{\prime}_{j}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A_{j}}
, and
m^{\prime}_{p}=m_{p}
and
A^{\prime}_{p}=A_{p}
for
p\not=j
. It follows that
r=0
.
We have
x_{l}\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash a_{l}:A_{l}|^{(n)}
by inductive hypothesis and hence
\partial({x_{j}},{x_{l}})=\operatorname{\iota}_{0}(x_{l})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash 0\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A_{l}}|^{(n)}
by Lemma
6.25
, that
is
\partial({x_{j}},{x_{l}})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\cdot a:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A_{i}}|^{(n)}
as required.

•

Assume now that
j=l
. We have
m^{\prime}_{p}=[\,]
and

A^{\prime}_{p}=A_{p}
if
p\not=l
, and
m^{\prime}_{l}=[{r\cdot a_{l}}]

and
A^{\prime}_{l}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}_{l}
. Then by inductive hypothesis we have
x_{l}\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\cdot a_{l}:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A_{l}}|^{(n)}
which
is exactly what we need since
\partial({x_{j}},{x_{l}})=x_{l}
in that
case.

Lemma 6.35
.

Given
\Phi=(x_{1}:[\,]:A_{1},\dots,x_{k}:[\,]:A_{k})
and
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
one has
\underline{\kappa}\in|\Phi\vdash\kappa:\iota|^{(n)}
.

Proof 6.36
.

By induction on
n
. For
n=0
it suffices to observe that

\underline{\kappa}\in|\kappa|_{\iota}
which results obviously from the
definition of
\|\kappa\vdash\nu\|_{\iota}
. We prove the property for

n+1
assuming that it holds for
n
. So with the notations of
the lemma we must prove that

\underline{\kappa}\in|\Phi\vdash a:A_{l}|^{(n+1)}
. Let

l\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
r\in\mathbb{D}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
be such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(j,\underline{\Phi})
. Setting

\Phi^{\prime}=(x_{j}:m^{\prime}_{j}:A^{\prime}_{j})_{j=1}^{k}
, this means that

m^{\prime}_{j}=[\,]
for each
j
, that
A^{\prime}_{j}=A_{j}
for
j\not=l
and that
A^{\prime}_{l}=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A_{l}}
. This also implies that
r=0
. By inductive hypothesis we have
\underline{\kappa}\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash\kappa:\iota|^{(n)}
and hence
\partial({x_{l}},{\underline{\kappa}})=\operatorname{\iota}_{0}(\underline{\kappa})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash(0,\kappa):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\iota}|^{(n)}
by Lemma
6.25
.

Lemma 6.37
.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n)}
then
\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot(\kappa+1):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n)}

where
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
.

Proof 6.38
.

By induction on
n
. Assume that
n=0
, let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|
,
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\in\|\kappa+1\vdash\nu\|_{\iota}
. We have
(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\kappa},\mathsf{succ}\cdot s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\kappa+1},s)\in\Bot(\nu)
and hence
\mathsf{succ}\cdot s\in\|\kappa\vdash\nu\|_{\iota}
.
Since
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|
we have
M^{\prime}\in|\delta\cdot\kappa|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
and hence
(\overline{\delta},\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M^{\prime}),s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\delta},M^{\prime},\mathsf{succ}\cdot s)\in\Bot(\nu)
. This shows that
\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M^{\prime})\in|\delta\cdot(\kappa+1)|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
as required.

For the inductive step, we assume that the implication holds for

n
and we prove it for
n+1
, so we assume that

M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n+1)}
. Let
r\in\mathbb{D}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\Phi)\}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
such
that
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
. We have
\partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi\vdash r\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{\iota}|^{(n)}
and hence, by inductive hypothesis,

\partial({x_{l}},{\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M)})=\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d+1}(\partial({x_{l}},{M}))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\delta\cdot(\kappa+1):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{\iota}|^{(n)}

and so we have shown that
\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(M)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot(\kappa+1):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n+1)}
.

Lemma 6.39
.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n)}
then
\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(M)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{\iota}|^{(n)}

where
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
.

Proof 6.40
.

Similar to that of Lemma
6.37
.

Lemma 6.41
.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot(\kappa+1):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n)}
then
\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(M)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{\iota}|^{(n)}

where
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
.

Proof 6.42
.

Similar to that of Lemma
6.37
.

Lemma 6.43
.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
M\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n)}
,
Q_{0}\in|\Phi_{1}\vdash b:B|^{(n)}
and
{\underline{\Phi}}\vdash{Q_{1}}:{B}
then
\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,Q_{0},Q_{1})\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}|^{(n)}
, where
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
and
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}
.

Proof 6.44
.

By induction on
n
. Assume that
n=0
, let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|
,
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
and
Q_{i}^{\prime}={Q_{i}}[{\vec{P}}]
for
i=0,1
. We can write
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
where
F
is sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f

where
f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
and
e=\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
. We have
(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{0},\mathsf{if}(\overline{\varepsilon},Q^{\prime}_{0},Q^{\prime}_{1})\cdot s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon},Q^{\prime}_{0},s)
. Since
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|\subseteq|\Phi_{1}|
we have
Q^{\prime}_{0}\in|\varepsilon\cdot f|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}}
and hence
(\overline{\varepsilon},Q^{\prime}_{0},s)\in\Bot(\nu)
and therefore
\mathsf{if}(\overline{\varepsilon},Q^{\prime}_{0},Q^{\prime}_{1})\cdot s\in\|0\vdash\nu\|_{\iota}
. Since
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|\subseteq|\Phi_{0}|
we have
M^{\prime}\in|\delta\cdot 0|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
and hence
(\overline{\delta\varepsilon},\mathsf{if}^{d}(M^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{0},Q^{\prime}_{1}),s)=(\overline{\varepsilon}\overline{\delta},\mathsf{if}^{d}(M^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{0},Q^{\prime}_{1}),s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\delta},M^{\prime},\mathsf{if}(\overline{\varepsilon},Q^{\prime}_{0},Q^{\prime}_{1})\cdot s)\in\Bot(\nu)
. This shows that
\mathsf{if}^{d}(M^{\prime},Q^{\prime}_{0},Q^{\prime}_{1})\in|\delta\varepsilon\cdot f|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+e}{F}}
as required.

For the inductive step, we assume that the implication holds for

n
and we prove it for
n+1
, so we assume that

M\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n+1)}
,
Q_{0}\in|\Phi_{1}\vdash b:B|^{(n+1)}
and
{\underline{\Phi}}\vdash{Q_{1}}:{B}
. Let
r\in\mathbb{D}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\Phi)\}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
such
that
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
. By Lemma
6.20
, since
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}
, we can find
r_{0},r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}
such that
r=r_{0}+r_{1}
as well as
\Phi^{\prime}_{0},\Phi^{\prime}_{1}
such that
(\Phi^{\prime}_{i},r_{i},\Phi_{i})\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
for
i=0,1
, and
\Phi^{\prime}=\Phi^{\prime}_{0}+\Phi^{\prime}_{1}
. If follows that
\partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime}_{0}\vdash r_{0}\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{\iota}|^{(n)}
and
\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{0}})\in|\Phi^{\prime}_{1}\vdash r_{1}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}|^{(n)}
. Since

{\underline{\Phi^{\prime}}}\vdash{\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{1}})}:{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}
, we have
\mathsf{if}^{d+1}(\partial({x_{l}},{M}),\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{0}}),\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{1}}))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r_{0}\delta r_{1}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}
by inductive hypothesis. By Lemma
6.23
we have
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{if}^{d+1}(\partial({x_{l}},{M}),\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{0}}),\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{1}})))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash\underrightarrow{r_{0}\delta r_{1}}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}
, that is

\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{if}^{d+1}(\partial({x_{l}},{M}),\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{0}}),\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{1}})))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r_{1}r_{0}\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\partial({x_{l}},{\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,Q_{0},Q_{1})})=\theta(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{if}^{d+1}(\partial({x_{l}},{M}),\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{0}}),\partial({x_{l}},{Q_{1}}))))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}

by Lemma
6.27
, since
r=r_{0}+r_{1}
.

Remark 6.45
.

In some sense this proof motivates syntactically the introduction of
the cyclic permutation combinator
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\_)
in the definition
of
\partial({x},{\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,Q_{0},Q_{1})})
in Figure
3
:
it allows the
\theta(\_)
combinator to act at the right level. We have
already motivated it denotationally by
Theorem
5.63
.

Lemma 6.46
.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
M\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash\delta\cdot(\kappa+1):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n)}
,
Q_{1}\in|\Phi_{1}\vdash b:B|^{(n)}
for some
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\underline{\Phi}}\vdash{Q_{0}}:{B}
then
\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,Q_{0},Q_{1})\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}|^{(n)}
, where
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
and
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}
.

Proof 6.47
.

Similar to that of Lemma
6.43
.

Lemma 6.48
.

For all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
M\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n)}
and
N\in|\Phi_{1},x:k[{\kappa}]\vdash b:B|^{(n)}

where
k,\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
, then
\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,M,N)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}|^{(n)}
where
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
and
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}
.

Proof 6.49
.

By induction on
n
. Assume that
n=0
, let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|
,
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
and
N^{\prime}={N}[{\vec{P}}]
. We can write
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
where
F
is sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f

where
f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
and
e=\mathsf{len}(\varepsilon)
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
. We have
(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\kappa},\mathsf{let}(\overline{\varepsilon},x,N^{\prime})\cdot s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon},{N^{\prime}}\left[{\underline{\kappa}}/{x}\right],s)
. Since
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|\subseteq|\Phi_{0}|
and
\underline{\kappa}\in|k[{\kappa}]|_{\iota}

we have
{N^{\prime}}\left[{\underline{\kappa}}/{x}\right]\in|\varepsilon\cdot f|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}}
and hence
(\overline{\varepsilon},{N^{\prime}}\left[{\underline{\kappa}}/{x}\right],s)\in\Bot(\nu)
and therefore
\mathsf{let}(\overline{\varepsilon},x,N^{\prime})\cdot s\in\|\kappa\vdash\nu\|_{\iota}
. Since
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|\subseteq|\Phi_{1}|
we have
M^{\prime}\in|\delta\cdot\kappa|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}
and hence
(\overline{\delta\varepsilon},\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,M^{\prime},N^{\prime}),s)=(\overline{\varepsilon}\overline{\delta},\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,M^{\prime},N^{\prime}),s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\delta},M^{\prime},\mathsf{let}(\overline{\varepsilon},x,N^{\prime})\cdot s)\in\Bot(\nu)
. This shows that
\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,M^{\prime},N^{\prime})\in|\delta\varepsilon\cdot f|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+e}{F}}
as required.

For the inductive step, we assume that the implication holds for

n
and we prove it for
n+1
, so we assume that

M\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|^{(n+1)}
, and
N\in|\Phi_{1},x:k[{\kappa}]\vdash b:B|^{(n+1)}
. Let
r\in\mathbb{D}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\Phi)\}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
be such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
. By Lemma
6.20
, since
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}
, we can find
r_{0},r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}

such that
r=r_{0}+r_{1}
as well as
\Phi^{\prime}_{0},\Phi^{\prime}_{1}
such that
(\Phi^{\prime}_{0},r_{0},\Phi_{0})\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
so that
((\Phi^{\prime}_{0},x:k[{\kappa}]:\iota),r_{0},(\Phi_{0},x:k[{\kappa}]:\iota))\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,(\underline{\Phi},x:\iota))
, and
(\Phi^{\prime}_{1},r_{1},\Phi_{1})\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
and

\Phi^{\prime}=\Phi^{\prime}_{0}+\Phi^{\prime}_{1}
. If follows that
\partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime}_{0}\vdash r_{0}\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{\iota}|^{(n)}
and
\partial({x_{l}},{N})\in|\Phi^{\prime}_{1},x:k[{\kappa}]:\iota\vdash r_{1}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}|^{(n)}
. We have
\mathsf{let}^{d+1}(x,\partial({x_{l}},{M}),\partial({x_{l}},{N}))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r_{0}\delta r_{1}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}

by inductive hypothesis. By Lemma
6.23
we have
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{let}^{d+1}(x,\partial({x_{l}},{M}),\partial({x_{l}},{N})))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash\underrightarrow{r_{0}\delta r_{1}}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}
, that is

\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{let}^{d+1}(x,\partial({x_{l}},{M}),\partial({x_{l}},{N})))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r_{1}r_{0}\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\partial({x_{l}},{\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,M,N)})=\theta(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}_{d}(\mathsf{let}^{d+1}(x,\partial({x_{l}},{M}),\partial({x_{l}},{N}))))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{B}|^{(n)}

by Lemma
6.27
, since
r=r_{0}+r_{1}
.

Lemma 6.50
.

If
M\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|^{(n)}
with
m=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{k}}]
and
(N\in|\Phi_{j}\vdash a_{j}:A|^{(n)})_{j=1}^{k}
then
({M})\,{N}\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n)}
where
\Phi=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\Phi_{j}
(so that

\forall j\ \underline{\Phi_{j}}=\underline{\Phi}
).

Proof 6.51
.

By induction on
n
. Assume that
n=0
. So let

\vec{P}\in|\Phi|
. Since for each
j=0,\dots,k
we have

|\Phi|\subseteq|\Phi_{j}|
by the assumption that
\Phi=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\Phi_{j}
, we have
M^{\prime}\in|(m,b)|_{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
(setting

M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
, remember that we use this convention
systematically) and
N^{\prime}\in\bigcap_{j=1}^{k}|a_{j}|_{A}
. We can write uniquely
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
with
F
sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f
with
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{D}^{e}
and

f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
, we have
(\overline{\varepsilon},({M^{\prime}})\,{N^{\prime}},s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon},M^{\prime},\mathsf{arg}(N^{\prime})\cdot s)\in\Bot(\nu)
since
\mathsf{arg}(N^{\prime})\cdot s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
.

Now assume that the implication holds for
n
and let us prove it
for
n+1
so assume that
M\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|^{(n+1)}
and
(N\in|\Phi_{j}\vdash a_{j}:A|^{(n+1)})_{j=1}^{k}
. Let
r\in\mathbb{D}
and let
\Phi^{\prime}
be such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
for some
1\leq l\leq\mathsf{len}(\Phi)
. By Lemma
6.20
we can
find
(r_{i}\in\mathbb{D})_{i=0}^{k}
such that
r=\sum_{i=0}^{k}r_{i}
as well
as
(\Phi^{\prime}_{i})_{i=0}^{k}
such that
\Phi^{\prime}=\sum_{i=0}\Phi^{\prime}_{i}
and
((\Phi^{\prime}_{i},r_{i},\Phi_{i})\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi}))_{i=0}^{k}
. So
by our assumptions we have
\partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime}_{0}\vdash(m,r_{0}\cdot b):{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}|^{(n)}
and
\partial({x_{l}},{N})\in\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}|\Phi^{\prime}_{i}\vdash r_{j}\cdot a_{j}:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}|^{(n)}
. Let
r^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}\in\mathbb{D}
and
m^{\prime}=[{r_{1}\cdot a_{1},\dots,r_{k}\cdot a_{k}}]
.
By Lemma
6.31
we have
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})}\in|\Phi^{\prime}_{0}\vdash(m^{\prime},r^{\prime}r_{0}\cdot b):{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{2}{B}}|^{(n)}
and hence by Lemma
6.27
we have
\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})})\in|\Phi^{\prime}_{0}\vdash(m^{\prime},(r^{\prime}+r_{0})\cdot b):{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}|^{(n)}
. By inductive hypothesis we get
\partial({x_{l}},{({M})\,{N}})=({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})})})\,{\partial({x_{l}},{N})}\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}|^{(n)}
since
r=r^{\prime}+r_{0}
.
Since we have proven this for all choices of
l
,
r
and

\Phi^{\prime}
, our contention follows.

Lemma 6.52
.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
({M})\,{\mathsf{Y}M}\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n)}

then
\mathsf{Y}M\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n)}
.

Proof 6.53
.

By induction on
n
. For
n=0
, let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|

and let
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
. We write
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
where

F
is sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f
where
f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
, we have
(\overline{\varepsilon},\mathsf{Y}M^{\prime},s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon},M^{\prime},\mathsf{arg}(\mathsf{Y}M^{\prime})\cdot s)\in\Bot(\nu)
since we have
(\overline{\varepsilon},({M^{\prime}})\,{\mathsf{Y}M^{\prime}},s)\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon},M^{\prime},\mathsf{arg}(\mathsf{Y}M^{\prime})\cdot s)

and we have
({M^{\prime}})\,{\mathsf{Y}M^{\prime}}\in|b|_{B}
by our assumption. So we have shown that
\mathsf{Y}M\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(0)}
.

Assume that the implication holds for
n
and let us prove it for

n+1
. So we assume that
({M})\,{\mathsf{Y}M}\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n+1)}
. Let
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\Phi)\}
,
r\in\mathbb{D}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
be
such that
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
. We know that
\partial({x_{l}},{({M})\,{\mathsf{Y}M}})\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}|^{(n)}
. On the other hand
\partial({x_{l}},{({M})\,{\mathsf{Y}M}})=({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})})})\,{\partial({x_{l}},{\mathsf{Y}M})}
, see Figure
3
. On the same figure we see that
\partial({x_{l}},{\mathsf{Y}M})=\mathsf{Y}(\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})}))
. So we have
({\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})})})\,{\mathsf{Y}(\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})}))}\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}|^{(n)}
and hence
\mathsf{Y}(\theta(\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{\partial({x_{l}},{M})}))\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash r\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}|^{(n)}
by inductive hypothesis.

Remark 6.54
.

The next lemma has a different structure: the hypothesis in the
implication we prove by induction is stronger. This feature is used
only in the base case where it is absolutely crucial.

Lemma 6.55
.

For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if

M\in\bigcap_{h\geq n}|\Phi,x:m:A\vdash b:B|^{(h)}
then

\lambda x^{A}\,{M}\in|\Phi\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|^{(n)}
.

Proof 6.56
.

We prove the statement by induction on
n
. For
n=0
our
assumption is

\forall h\in{\mathbb{N}}\ M\in|\Phi,x:m:A\vdash b:B|^{(h)}
and we prove

\lambda x^{A}\,{M}\in|\Phi\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|^{(0)}
.
So let
\vec{P}\in|\Phi|
and let
M^{\prime}={M}[{\vec{P}}]
. We can write uniquely
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{e}{F}
with
F
sharp and
b=\varepsilon\cdot f
with
f\in\llbracket{F}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
. Let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}

and
s\in\|(m,f)\vdash\nu\|_{{A}\Rightarrow{F}}
. Then, by the typing
