rules for stacks, we have
s=\mathsf{D}(r_{1})\cdot\cdots\mathsf{D}(r_{d})\cdot\mathsf{arg}(P)\cdot t
where
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
r_{1},\dots,r_{d}\in\mathbb{D}
and there are
m_{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}}),\dots,m_{d}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\llbracket{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})
such that
((m_{i},(r_{i},m_{i-1}))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{\llbracket{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{i-1}{A}}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}})_{i=1}^{d}
,
where we set
m_{0}=m
. And last
P\in|m_{d}|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}
and
t\in\|f\vdash\nu\|_{F}
. By our assumption
20
20

20

It is here that the special form of the hypothesis is
crucial.
about
M
we have in particular
M\in|\Phi,x:m:A\vdash b:B|^{(d)}
and hence
\partial^{d}({x},{M})\in|\Phi,x:m_{d}:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}\vdash r_{d}\cdots r_{1}\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}|^{(0)}
so that
{\partial^{d}({x},{M^{\prime}})}\left[{P}/{x}\right]={\partial^{d}({x},{M})}[{\vec{P},P/x}]\in|r_{d}\cdots r_{1}\cdot b|_{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}}
.
So we have

\displaystyle(\overline{\varepsilon},\lambda x^{A}\,{M^{\prime}},s)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon}r_{1},\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M^{\prime}})},\mathsf{D}(r_{2})\cdot\cdots\mathsf{D}(r_{d})\cdot\mathsf{arg}(P)\cdot t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}\cdots\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{\varepsilon}r_{1}\cdots r_{d},\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{A}}\,{\partial^{d}({x},{M^{\prime}})},\mathsf{arg}(P)\cdot t)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}(\overline{r_{d}\cdots r_{1}\varepsilon},{\partial^{d}({x},{M^{\prime}})}\left[{P}/{x}\right],t)\in\Bot(\nu)

which entails that
(\overline{\varepsilon},\lambda x^{A}\,{M^{\prime}},s)\in\Bot(\nu)
and hence
\lambda x^{A}\,{M^{\prime}}\in|(m,b)|_{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}
as required.

For the inductive step we assume that the implication holds for

n
and prove it for
n+1
. Remember that in this implication,
M
,
\Phi
,
m
,
A
,
b
and
B
are universally quantified. So we
assume that
\forall h\geq n+1\ M\in|\Phi,x:m:A\vdash b:B|^{(h)}
and prove
that
\lambda x^{A}\,{M}\in|\Phi\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|^{(n+1)}
. We set
\Phi=(x_{1}:m_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{k}:m_{k}:A_{k})
.
Let
r\in\mathbb{D}
,
l\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
be such
that
(\Phi^{\prime},r,\Phi)\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,\underline{\Phi})
. We have
((\Phi^{\prime},x:m:A),r,(\Phi,x:m:A))\in\operatorname{\partial}(l,(\underline{\Phi},x:A))
and hence
\forall h\geq n\ \partial({x_{l}},{M})\in|\Phi^{\prime},x:A\vdash(m,r\cdot b):{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}|^{(h)}
by our assumption about
M
. It follows by inductive hypothesis that
\partial({x_{l}},{\lambda x^{A}\,{M}})=\lambda x^{A}\,{\partial({x_{l}},{M})}\in|\Phi^{\prime}\vdash(m,r\cdot b):{A}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{B}}|^{(n)}
. Therefore
\lambda x^{A}\,{M}\in|\Phi\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|^{(n+1)}
as required.

Lemma 6.57
.

If

M\in|\Phi,x:m:A\vdash b:B|
then

\lambda x^{A}\,{M}\in|\Phi\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|
.

Proof 6.58
.

Apply Lemma
6.55
.

Theorem 6.59
.

If
\Phi\vdash M:b:B
then
M\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|
.

Proof 6.60
.

By induction on the derivation of
\Phi\vdash M:b:B
.
We write
\Phi=(x_{1}:m_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{k}:m_{k}:A_{k})
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=x_{l}
for some
l\in\{1,\dots,k\}
so that the derivation consists of a rule
11

B=A_{l}
and
m_{l}=[{b}]
and we have

m_{j}=[\,]
for
j\not=l
. So we can apply
Lemma
6.33
which gives us
x_{l}\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|^{(n)}
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\underline{\kappa}
so that the derivation consists of a
rule
11
and hence
B=\iota
and
b=\kappa
, and we have

m_{j}=[\,]
for each
j
. By Lemma
6.35

we get
M\in|\Phi\vdash\underline{\kappa}:\iota|^{(n)}
for each

n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\lambda x^{A}\,{N}
so that
B=({A}\Rightarrow{C})
and
b=(m,c)
and we have
\Phi,x:m:A\vdash M:c:C
and hence
M\in|\Phi,x:m:A\vdash c:C|
by inductive hypothesis from
which we get
\lambda x^{A}\,{M}\in|\Phi\vdash(m,c):{A}\Rightarrow{C}|
by
Lemma
6.57
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=({N})\,{Q}
with
\Phi_{0}\vdash N:(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}
with
m=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}]
and
(\Phi_{j}\vdash Q:a_{j}:A)_{j=1}^{n}
, and
\Phi=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\Phi_{j}
. By inductive hypothesis we have
N\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash(m,b):{A}\Rightarrow{B}|
and
(Q\in|\Phi_{j}\vdash a_{j}:A|)_{j=1}^{n}
so we get
({N})\,{Q}\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|
by Lemma
6.50
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{N}
with
B=({\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}})
,

b=(m^{\prime},r\cdot c)
,

(m^{\prime},(r,m))\in\operatorname{\partial}_{\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}}
,

\Phi\vdash N:(m,c):{A}\Rightarrow{C}
.
By inductive hypothesis we have
N\in|\Phi\vdash(m,c):{A}\Rightarrow{C}|
and hence
M\in|\Phi\vdash(m^{\prime},r\cdot c):{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\Rightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{C}}|
by Lemma
6.31
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\mathsf{Y}N
with
\Phi_{0}\vdash N:(m,b):{B}\Rightarrow{B}
with
m=[{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}}]
,
(\Phi_{j}\vdash\mathsf{Y}N:b_{j}:B)_{j=1}^{n}
and
\Phi=\sum_{j=0}^{n}\Phi_{j}
. By inductive hypothesis we get
N\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash(m,b):{B}\Rightarrow{B}|
and
(\mathsf{Y}N\in|\Phi_{j}\vdash b_{j}:B|)_{j=1}^{n}
and hence

({N})\,{\mathsf{Y}N}\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|
by
Lemma
6.50
. It follows that

M=\mathsf{Y}N\in|\Phi\vdash b:B|
by Lemma
6.52
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\mathsf{succ}}^{d}(N)
with

\Phi\vdash N:\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
where

\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
. By inductive hypothesis we have
N\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|
and hence
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot\kappa+1:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|
by Lemma
6.37
.

\blacktriangleright
The cases where
M=\operatorname{\mathsf{pred}}^{d}(N)
are similar, using
Lemmas
6.39
and
6.41
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\mathsf{if}^{d}(N,Q_{0},Q_{1})
and that

\Phi_{0}\vdash N:\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
(with

d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
),
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
,
b=\delta\cdot c
with

\Phi_{1}\vdash Q_{0}:c:C
,
{\underline{\Phi}}\vdash{Q_{1}}:{C}
and
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}
. By inductive hypothesis we have
N\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash\delta\cdot 0:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|
and
Q_{0}\in|\Phi_{1}\vdash c:C|
and hence
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}|
by Lemma
6.43
.

\blacktriangleright
The case
M=\mathsf{if}^{d}(N,Q_{0},Q_{1})
,

\Phi_{0}\vdash N:\delta\cdot(\kappa+1):\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
(with
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
),
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
,

b=\delta\cdot c
with
\Phi_{1}\vdash Q_{1}:c:C
,

{\underline{\Phi}}\vdash{Q_{0}}:{C}
and
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}
is similar to the previous one, using
Lemma
6.46
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,N,Q)
with
\Phi_{0}\vdash N:\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}
and
\Phi_{1},x:k[{\kappa}]:\iota\vdash Q:b:B
,
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
and
\Phi=\Phi_{0}+\Phi_{1}
. By inductive hypothesis
we have
N\in|\Phi_{0}\vdash\delta\cdot\kappa:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}|
and
Q\in|\Phi_{1},x:k[{\kappa}]:\iota\vdash b:B|
and hence by
Lemma
6.48
we have
\mathsf{let}^{d}(x,N,Q)\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot b:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{B}|
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\iota}_{r}^{d}(N)
with
\Phi\vdash N:\delta\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
so that
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}
and
b=\delta r\cdot c
(of course

d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
). By inductive hypothesis we have
N\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}|
and hence
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}|

by Lemma
6.25
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\theta^{d}(N)
with
\Phi\vdash N:\delta r_{0}r_{1}\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{C}
so that
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}
and
b=\delta r\cdot c
with

r=r_{0}+r_{1}\in\mathbb{D}
(of course
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
). By inductive
hypothesis we have
N\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r_{0}r_{1}\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+2}{C}|
and hence
M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}|
by
Lemma
6.27
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\pi}_{r}^{d}(N)
with
\Phi\vdash N:\delta r\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}
so that
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}
and
b=\delta\cdot c
(of course

d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)
). By inductive hypothesis we have
N\in|\Phi\vdash\delta r\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+1}{C}|
and hence

M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{C}|
by Lemma
6.29
.

\blacktriangleright
Assume that
M=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}^{d}_{l}(N)
with
\Phi\vdash N:\delta\alpha\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+l+2}{C}
so
that
B=\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+l+2}{C}
and
b=\delta\underrightarrow{\alpha}\cdot c
(of
course
\mathsf{len}(\delta)=d
and
\mathsf{len}(\alpha)=l+2
). By inductive hypothesis we have
N\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\alpha\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+l+2}{C}|
and hence

M\in|\Phi\vdash\delta\underrightarrow{\alpha}\cdot c:\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d+l+2}{C}|
by Lemma
6.23
.

Theorem 6.61
.

Let
M
be a closed term and let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
be such that

\vdash M:\nu:\iota
. Then
[{(\langle\,\rangle,M,())}]\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}^{\ast}}C=C_{0}+[{(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())}]
for some multiset of well typed states
C_{0}
such that
C
is

\mathcal{L}
-summable in any model
\mathcal{L}
.

7.
Determinism and probabilities

Our goal is to refine Theorem
6.61
by showing that
none of the elements of
C_{0}
reduces to a value.
To this end we use the model of probabilistic coherence spaces that we
have presented in Section
3.4
.

7.1.
Integers and fixpoints in
\mathbf{Pcoh}

The category
\mathbf{Pcoh}
is Scott in the sense of
Section
5.5.1
. The order relation
\leq
on
morphisms in
\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
is given by
s\leq t
iff

\forall(a,b)\in|{{X}\multimap{Y}}|\ s_{a,b}\leq t_{a,b}
. It is a
standard fact that for any PCS
X
the poset
\mathsf{P}{X}
(equipped
with the pointwise order of
|{X}|
-indexed families of
non-negative real numbers) is a cpo and that all the operations
(composition of morphisms, tensor product,
\oc{\_}
functor) preserve
the lubs of directed families of morphisms, that is
\mathbf{Pcoh}
is
Scott, see for instance
[
DE11
]
.

As a consequence for any PCS
X
we have a fixpoint operator

\mathcal{Y}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({X}\Rightarrow{X},X)
which is characterized by
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}(t)=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\widehat{t}^{n}(0)
. Concerning derivatives,
this operator satisfies Theorem
5.52
.

For the categorical axiomatization of integers we refer to
Section
5.4
. We define
\mathsf{N}
by
|{\mathsf{N}}|={\mathbb{N}}
and
u\in\mathsf{P}{\mathsf{N}}
if
u\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{{\mathbb{N}}}

satisfies
\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}}u_{\nu}\leq 1
. This structure
\mathsf{N}

is a PCS such that
\mathsf{P}{\mathsf{N}^{\mathord{\perp}}}=[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
as easily
checked. Notice that
\|{u}\|_{\mathsf{N}}=\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}}u_{\nu}
. This is an
\ell^{1}
norm whereas in
\mathsf{N}^{\mathord{\perp}}
the norm is

\|{u^{\prime}}\|_{\mathsf{N}^{\mathord{\perp}}}=\sup_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}}u^{\prime}_{\nu}
which is an

\ell^{\infty}
norm. The matrix
\chi\in(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{|{{{1}\oplus{\mathsf{N}}}\multimap{\mathsf{N}}}|}
defined by
\chi_{(0,\ast),n}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{0},{n}}
and
\chi_{(1,n^{\prime}),n}=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n^{\prime}+1},{n}}
satisfies
\chi\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({1}\oplus{\mathsf{N}},\mathsf{N})
and is an isomorphism between
these two PCSs. Then, given
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({1}\oplus{X},X)
, let

(s_{n}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathsf{N},X))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be the sequence of morphisms
defined by

\displaystyle s_{0}=0\text{\quad and\quad}s_{n+1}=t\,({1}\oplus{s_{n}})\,{\chi}^{-1}\,.

An easy induction shows that
\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}}\ s_{n}\leq s_{n+1}
and
so
s=\sup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}s_{n}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathsf{N},X)
satisfies
s=t\,({1}\oplus{s})\,{\chi}^{-1}
. This means that

(\mathsf{N},\chi)
is an initial algebra for the functor

{1}\oplus{\_}:\mathbf{Pcoh}\to\mathbf{Pcoh}
and so
\mathbf{Pcoh}
satisfies
5.4
.

The associated morphisms
\overline{\mathsf{suc}},\overline{\mathsf{pred}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathsf{N},\mathsf{N})
are
characterized by
{\overline{\mathsf{suc}}}\cdot{u}=\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}}u_{\nu}\mathsf{e}_{\nu+1}
and
{\overline{\mathsf{pred}}}\cdot{u}=u_{0}\mathsf{e}_{0}+\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}}u_{\nu+1}\mathsf{e}_{\nu}
. The morphism
\overline{\mathsf{if}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\mathsf{N}}\otimes{\left({X}\mathrel{\&}{X}\right)},X)
is
characterized by

{\overline{\mathsf{if}}}\cdot{({u}\otimes{\langle{x^{0},x^{1}}\rangle})}=u_{0}x^{0}+\big(\sum_{\nu=1}^{\infty}u_{\nu}\big)x^{1}\,.

Last the morphism
\overline{\mathsf{let}}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}({\mathsf{N}}\otimes{({\oc{\mathsf{N}}}\multimap{X})},X)
is
characterized by

{\overline{\mathsf{let}}}\cdot{({u}\otimes{t})}=\sum_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}}u_{\nu}\widehat{t}(\mathsf{e}_{\nu})\,.

So we have an interpretation of terms and states in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
which is
sound, that is, invariant by reduction.
More precisely, following the general pattern of
Section
5.6
, we associate with each type

A
an object
\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}
of
\mathbf{Pcoh}
in such a way that
\llbracket{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}^{d}{\iota}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}=\operatorname{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}}^{d}\mathsf{N}
,
\llbracket{{A}\Rightarrow{B}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}=({\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}}\Rightarrow{\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}})
.

And with any term
M
such that
{x_{1}:A_{1},\dots,x_{k}:A_{k}}\vdash{M}:{B}
we can associate the morphism
\llbracket{M}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\Gamma}\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(\mathop{\&}_{i=1}^{k}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}},\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}})
and this interpretation satisfies that if
M\mathrel{\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}}M^{\prime}
then
\llbracket{M}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\Gamma}=\llbracket{M^{\prime}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\Gamma}
.
Remember that the reduction relation
\mathrel{\to_{\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}}}
can be extended into the
reduction relation
\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})}
on
\mathbf{Pcoh}
-summable multisets of terms, that
is to multisets
S=[{M_{1},\dots,M_{n}}]
such that

({\Gamma}\vdash{M_{j}}:{B})_{j=1}^{n}
and
\llbracket{S}\rrbracket_{\Gamma}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\llbracket{M_{j}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\Gamma}\in{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\oc}(\mathop{\&}_{i=1}^{k}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}},\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}})
and this
extended relation satisfies
S\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}})}}S^{\prime}\Rightarrow\llbracket{S}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\Gamma}=\llbracket{S^{\prime}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\Gamma}
.

7.2.
A forgetful functor

Given
s\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
, we set

\mathsf{Q}s=\{(a,b)\in|{X}|\times|{Y}|\mid s_{a,b}\not=0\}\in\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}(|{X}|,|{Y}|)
.

Theorem 7.1
.

The operation
\mathsf{Q}
extended to objects by
\mathsf{Q}X=|{X}|
is a functor
\mathbf{Pcoh}\to\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
which preserves
all the structure of model of
LL
.

Proof 7.2
.

This is essentially trivial. Let us prove functoriality: let

s\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
and
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(Y,Z)
. If

(a,c)\in\mathsf{Q}({t}\,{s})
then

\sum_{b\in|{Y}|}s_{a,b}t_{b,c}\not=0
and so there must be

b\in|{Y}|
such that
s_{a,b}\not=0
and
t_{b,c}\not=0
and
hence
(a,c)\in{(\mathsf{Q}t)}\,{(\mathsf{Q}s)}
. Conversely assume that
(a,c)\in{(\mathsf{Q}t)}\,{(\mathsf{Q}s)}

and let
b\in|{Y}|
be such that
s_{a,b}\not=0
and

t_{b,c}\not=0
. Since all coefficients are non-negative we have

\sum_{b^{\prime}\in|{Y}|}s_{a,b^{\prime}}t_{b^{\prime},c}\geq s_{a,b}t_{b,c}>0
and
hence
(a,c)\in\mathsf{Q}({t}\,{s})
.

As another example, let us prove that if
s\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
then
\mathsf{Q}(\oc{s})=\oc({\mathsf{Q}s})
. Let

(m,p)\in\mathsf{Q}(\oc{s})
, so let
r\in\mathsf{L}(m,p)
be such that

s^{r}=\prod_{(a,b)\in|{X}|\times|{Y}|}s_{a,b}^{r(a,b)}\not=0
. This implies that

\operatorname{\mathsf{supp}}({r})\subseteq\mathsf{Q}s
and hence we have

r\in\oc({\mathsf{Q}s})
. Conversely if
(m,p)\in\oc({\mathsf{Q}s})
we can write
m=[{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}}]
and
p=[{b_{1},\dots,b_{n}}]
in such a way that
((a_{i},b_{i})\in\mathsf{Q}s)_{i=1}^{k}
which means that
\prod_{i=1}^{k}s_{a_{i},b_{i}}\not=0
. Now setting
r=[{(a_{1},b_{1}),\dots,(a_{k},b_{k})}]
we have
r\in\mathsf{L}(m,p)
and
s^{r}\not=0
and hence
(m,p)\in\mathsf{Q}(\oc{s})
.

To prove that
\mathsf{Q}
applied to the
\mathcal{Y}
operator of

\mathbf{Pcoh}
yields the
\mathcal{Y}
operator of
\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}
, it suffices to
observe that the functor
\mathsf{Q}
is locally continuous.

Theorem 7.3
.

For any type
A
we have
\mathsf{Q}\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}=\llbracket{A}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}
and for
any term
M
such that
{\Gamma}\vdash{M}:{B}
we have

\mathsf{Q}\llbracket{M}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\Gamma}=\llbracket{M}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}_{\Gamma}
, and similarly for
stacks and states.

Theorem 7.4
.

For any term
M
such that
{(x_{i}:A_{i})_{1=1}^{k}}\vdash{M}:{B}
all
coefficients of the matrix

\llbracket{M}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}_{\Gamma}\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(\mathop{\&}_{i=1}^{k}\llbracket{A_{i}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}},\llbracket{B}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}})

belong to
{\mathbb{N}}
. The same holds for stacks and states.

Proof 7.5
.

It suffices to observe that if
t\in\mathbf{Pcoh}(X,Y)
belongs to

{\mathbb{N}}^{|{X}|\times|{Y}|}
, then

\oc{t}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{|{\oc{X}}|\times|{\oc{Y}}|}
and that

\operatorname{\partial}_{X}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{|{\oc{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}X}}|\times|{\operatorname{\mathsf{S}}{\oc{X}}}|}
.

Remark 7.6
.

Of course the above property is lost if we extend the
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
with
probabilistic choice, which is perfectly possible and compatible
with the
\mathbf{Pcoh}
semantics.

Theorem 7.7
.

If
[{c_{1},\dots,c_{n}}]
is a
\mathbf{Pcoh}
-summable multiset of
states, we have only the two following possibilities:

•

either
\llbracket{c_{i}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}=0
for all
i=1,\dots,n

•

or there is exactly one
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and one

\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\llbracket{c_{i}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}=\mathsf{e}_{\nu}
, and

\llbracket{c_{j}}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}=0
for
j\not=i
.

Proof 7.8
.

Observe that if
u\in\mathsf{P}{\mathsf{N}}
belongs to
{\mathbb{N}}^{\mathbb{N}}
then we
have either
u=0
of
u=\mathsf{e}_{\nu}
for a uniquely determined

\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Theorem 7.9
.

Let
M
be a closed term and let
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
be such that

\vdash M:\nu:\iota
. Then
[{(\langle\,\rangle,M,())}]\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}^{\ast}}C=C_{0}+[{(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())}]
for some multiset of well typed states
C_{0}
such that
C
is

\mathcal{L}
-summable in any model
\mathcal{L}
, and all the elements
c
of

C_{0}
satisfy
\llbracket{c}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}=\emptyset
.

Proof 7.10
.

Using Theorem
6.61
we have
[{(\langle\,\rangle,M,())}]\mathrel{\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}^{\ast}}C=C_{0}+[{(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())}]
and by Theorem
6.12
we know that
C
is
\mathbf{Pcoh}
-summable and hence by
Theorem
7.7
we know that all the elements

c
of
C_{0}
satisfy
\llbracket{c}\rrbracket^{\mathbf{Pcoh}}=0
and hence

\llbracket{c}\rrbracket^{\operatorname{\mathbf{Rel}}}=\emptyset
by Theorem
7.3
.

7.3.
A deterministic machine

We slightly modify the Krivine machine presented in
Section
6.1
so as to make it fully deterministic.
Guillaume Geoffroy must be credited for the key idea of this
determinization which consists in making the access word component of
the Krivine machine writable. To this end we introduce the set
\check{\mathbb{D}}=\mathbb{D}\uplus\{\check{n}\mid n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
where for each

n\in{\mathbb{N}}
the symbol
\check{n}
represents a “writable cell of name
n
”.

A state of the deterministic machine is
0
or a tuple

\displaystyle\gamma=(\zeta,k,M,\sigma)

where
\zeta\in\check{\mathbb{D}}^{d}
for some
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
M

is a
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
-term and
\sigma
is a stack (defined exactly as in
Section
6.1
apart that words are now taken in

{\check{\mathbb{D}}}^{<\omega}
). We use
\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)
for the set of all

n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
\check{n}
occurs in
\zeta
or
\sigma
.
When
k
is
>
than all the elements of
\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)
, we say
that
\gamma
is
well-formed
.

We use letters
u,v
to denote general
elements of
\check{\mathbb{D}}
, letters
i,j
for elements of

\mathbb{D}\subset\check{\mathbb{D}}
and
\check{\Theta}_{\mathsf{cd}}
for the set of states of this
new machine. The main novelty is that that the
+
operation on
states is no more required. The second component of a state of the
machine is used as a
\mathtt{gensym}
for creating cell names
on request, which are fresh by the well-formedness condition.

The typing rules for stacks and states are exactly the same as in
Figure
8
, replacing
\mathbb{D}
with
\check{\mathbb{D}}
. Most
transition rules are the same as in
Figure
9
. The modified transition rules are
given in Figure
13
. Notice that the rule
producing a
0
is still present
(
(\eta i\zeta,\operatorname{\iota}_{1-i}^{d}(M),\sigma)\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}0
for

i\in\mathbb{D}
and
d=\mathsf{len}(\zeta)
) but that the rule producing a sum
has been replaced by the deterministic rule (
18
)
so that the machine
\check{\Theta}_{\mathsf{cd}}
equipped with the transitions

\to_{\mathsf{w}}
is fully deterministic. In rule (
16
),
namely

(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\operatorname{\iota}_{1}^{d}(M),\sigma){\to_{\mathsf{w}}(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma)}\left[{0}/{\check{n}}\right]
, notice
that the cell
\check{n}
can occur in
\eta\zeta
and in

\sigma
. The intuition behind this rule is that we give the value

1
to the occurrence of
\check{n}
singled out in its left
member, so we know that all the other occurrences must take value

0
, see Remark
7.11
below.
Rule (
15
) means intuitively that the occurrence of

\check{n}
singled out in its left member receives value
0
and
we do not know for the others; what we know however is that exactly
one will receive value
1
, see Remark
7.11

below, so at least one occurrence of
\check{n}
must be present in

\gamma
.
This explains why the proviso in the application of this rule makes
sense; it will be essential in the proof of
Lemma
7.16
.

Notice that if
\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma^{\prime}
and
\gamma
is well-formed
then
\gamma^{\prime}
is also well-formed thanks to
rule (
18
) which is the only one which introduces
a new writable cell: it advances the gensym counter by
1
.

\displaystyle(\zeta u,k,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M},\sigma)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{w}}(\zeta,k,M,\mathsf{D}(u)\cdot\sigma)

(13)

\displaystyle(\zeta,k,\lambda x^{A}\,{M},\mathsf{D}(u)\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{w}}(\zeta u,k,\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})},\sigma)

(14)

\displaystyle(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\operatorname{\iota}_{0}^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma=(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma)\text{\quad if }n\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)

(15)

\displaystyle(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\operatorname{\iota}_{1}^{d}(M),\sigma)

\displaystyle{\to_{\mathsf{w}}(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma)}\left[{0}/{\check{n}}\right]

(16)

\displaystyle(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\theta^{d}(M),\sigma)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{w}}(\eta\check{n}\check{n}\zeta,k,M,\sigma)

(17)

\displaystyle(\eta 1\zeta,k,\theta^{d}(M),\sigma)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{w}}(\eta\check{k}\check{k}\zeta,k+1,M,\sigma)

(18)

Figure 13.
Deterministic reduction rules, with the convention that

d=\mathsf{len}(\zeta)

Given
\zeta\in\check{\mathbb{D}}^{<\omega}
we use
\mathsf{w}_{0}(\zeta)
for the set of all
\delta\in\mathbb{D}^{\mathsf{len}(\zeta)}

such that, for all
l\in\{1,\dots,\mathsf{len}(\zeta)\}
,

\zeta_{l}\in\mathbb{D}\Rightarrow\delta_{l}=\zeta_{l}
.
For a stack
\sigma
of
\check{\Theta}_{\mathsf{cd}}
we define a set of stacks

\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)
of
\underline{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}
in
Figure
14
. Last given a
\check{\Theta}_{\mathsf{cd}}
-state

\gamma=(\zeta,k,M,\sigma)
we define a set of

\underline{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}
-states
\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma)
by

\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma)=\{(\delta,M,s)\mid\delta\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\zeta)\text{
and }s\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)\}\,.

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{0}(\mathsf{arg}(M)\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{arg}(M)\cdot s\mid s\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{0}(\mathsf{succ}\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{succ}\cdot s\mid s\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{0}(\mathsf{pred}\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{pred}\cdot s\mid s\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{0}(\mathsf{if}(\zeta,M_{1},M_{2})\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{if}(\delta,M_{1},M_{2})\cdot s\mid\delta\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\zeta)\text{ and }s\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{0}(\mathsf{let}(\zeta,x,M)\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{let}(\delta,x,M)\cdot s\mid\delta\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\zeta)\text{ and }s\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{0}(\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s\mid s\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}_{0}(\mathsf{D}(\check{n})\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\{\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s\mid i\in\mathbb{D}\text{ and }s\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)\}

Figure 14.
The set of
\underline{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}
-stacks associated with a

\check{\Theta}_{\mathsf{cd}}
-stack

Next given
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\delta\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\zeta)
we define
\Sigma_{n}(\delta:\zeta)\in{\mathbb{N}}
, which is the sum of all values taken
by the cell
\check{n}
in
\delta
, by

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\langle\,\rangle:\langle\,\rangle)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\delta i:\zeta i)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n}(\delta:\zeta)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\delta i:\zeta\check{k})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n},{k}}i+\Sigma_{n}(\delta:\zeta)\text{\quad where }\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n},{k}}\text{ is the Kronecker symbol.}

Given
s\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\sigma)
we define
\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)\in{\mathbb{N}}
as
follows.

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\mathsf{arg}(M)\cdot s:\mathsf{arg}(M)\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\mathsf{succ}\cdot s:\mathsf{succ}\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\mathsf{pred}\cdot s:\mathsf{pred}\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\mathsf{if}(\delta,M_{1},M_{2})\cdot s:\mathsf{if}(\zeta,M_{1},M_{2})\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n}(\delta:\zeta)+\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\mathsf{let}(\delta,x,M)\cdot s:\mathsf{let}(\zeta,x,M)\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n}(\delta:\zeta)+\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s:\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s:\mathsf{D}(\check{k})\cdot\sigma)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{k},{n}}i+\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)\,.

Last, for states
\gamma=(\zeta,k,M,\sigma)
and

c=(\delta,M,s)
such that
c\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma)
we set

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n}(c:\gamma)=\Sigma_{n}(\delta:\zeta)+\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)\,.

Finally we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{w}(\gamma)=\{c\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma)\mid\forall n\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)\quad\Sigma_{n}(c:\gamma)=1\}\,.

Remark 7.11
.

Intuitively
\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
is the set of all states of the
machine
\underline{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}
obtained from
\gamma\in\check{\Theta}_{\mathsf{cd}}
as
follows:
for each
n
such that
\check{n}
occurs at some place in

\gamma
, we choose one occurrence of
\check{n}
and replace it
with
1
, and we replace all the other occurrences of
\check{n}

with
0
’s.

Let
\to_{\mathsf{nd}}
be the transition relation on
\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}^{0}
, the set
of all sum-implicit elements of
\underline{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}c^{\prime}\text{\quad if\quad}\begin{cases}&c\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}c^{\prime}\\
\text{or }&c\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}c^{\prime}+c^{\prime\prime}\\
\text{or }&c\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}c^{\prime\prime}+c^{\prime}\end{cases}

so that now

\displaystyle(\varepsilon 1\delta,\theta^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{nd}}(\varepsilon 10\delta,M,s)

\displaystyle(\varepsilon 1\delta,\theta^{d}(M),s)

\displaystyle\to_{\mathsf{nd}}(\varepsilon 01\delta,M,s)

and
\to_{\mathsf{nd}}
is defined exactly as
\mathrel{\to_{\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}}}
in the other cases.

Lemma 7.12
.

Assume that
c\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma)
and that
\Sigma_{n}(c:\gamma)=0
for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then
c\in\mathsf{w}_{0}({\gamma}\left[{0}/{\check{n}}\right])
.

Proof 7.13

(Sketch of the proof)
.

By the assumption that
\Sigma_{n}(c:\gamma)=0
we know that at all
the places in
c
corresponding to occurrences of
\check{n}
in

\gamma
we have the value
0
. The conclusion follows readily.

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
\gamma\in\check{\Theta}_{\mathsf{cd}}
and
c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
. If

c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}c^{\prime}\not=0
then
\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma^{\prime}
for
\gamma^{\prime}
such that

c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma^{\prime})
.

Proof 7.15
.

We use our convention that
d=\mathsf{len}(\delta)=\mathsf{len}(\zeta)
. Most cases
are straightforward, we deal first with one of these to illustrate its
triviality.

\blacktriangleright

c=(\varepsilon\delta,\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,N_{1},N_{2}),s)
and
c^{\prime}=(\delta,M,\mathsf{if}(\varepsilon,N_{1},N_{2})\cdot s)
. Since
c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
we can write
\gamma=(\eta\zeta,k,\mathsf{if}^{d}(M,N_{1},N_{2}),\sigma)
and we
have
\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma^{\prime}=(\zeta,k,M,\mathsf{if}(\eta,N_{1},N_{2})\cdot\sigma)

so that
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma^{\prime})
because for each
n\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
we have
\Sigma_{n}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)=1
.

Next we consider the more interesting cases.

\blacktriangleright

c=(\delta i,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M},s)
and

c^{\prime}=(\delta,M,\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s)
. Then
\gamma=(\zeta u,k,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M},\sigma)
and we have

\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma^{\prime}=(\zeta,M,k,\mathsf{D}(u)\cdot\sigma)
and hence

c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma^{\prime})
. To be more explicit we should consider
the two possible cases for
u
.

•

If
u\in\mathbb{D}
we have
u=i
because
c\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma)

and hence
\gamma^{\prime}=(\zeta,k,M,\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot\sigma)
. For each
n\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
we have
\Sigma_{n}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=\Sigma_{n}(\delta:\zeta)+\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)=\Sigma_{n}(c:\gamma)=1
so that
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma^{\prime})
.

•

If
u=\check{n}
for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
we know that
\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\delta)=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n^{\prime}},{n}}i+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\delta:\zeta)+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)=1

for each
n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
and since we also
have
\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n^{\prime}},{n}}i+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\delta:\zeta)+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)
it follows that
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma^{\prime})
.

\blacktriangleright

c=(\delta,\lambda x^{A}\,{M},\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s)
and
c^{\prime}=(\delta i,\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})},s)
. Then
\gamma=(\zeta,k,\lambda x^{A}\,{M},\mathsf{D}(u)\cdot\sigma)
and we have

\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma^{\prime}=(\zeta u,k,\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})},\sigma)
so that
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma^{\prime})
. We check easily as above that
\Sigma_{n}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=\Sigma_{n}(c:\gamma)
for all

n\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
and hence

c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma^{\prime})
.

\blacktriangleright

c=(\varepsilon i\delta,\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(M),s)
and
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon\delta,M,s)
with
i=0
. Assume that

\gamma=(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\operatorname{\iota}_{0}^{d}(M),\sigma)
and let
\gamma^{\prime}=(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma)
. Clearly

c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma^{\prime})
. Moreover

\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)=1
for all

n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})\subseteq\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)
, including when

n^{\prime}=n
in which case we use the fact that
i=0
— assuming that

n\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
which is not only possible but necessary as
we see now —. Since
\Sigma_{n}(c:\gamma)=1
and
i=0
,
\check{n}

must occur in
\eta
,
\zeta
or
\sigma
so that

n\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
and hence
\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma^{\prime}
(see in
Figure
13
the restrictive condition for
this reduction (
15
)).

The case where
\gamma=(\eta 0\zeta,k,\operatorname{\iota}_{0}^{d}(M),\sigma)

is dealt with straightforwardly.

\blacktriangleright

c=(\varepsilon i\delta,\operatorname{\iota}_{i}^{d}(M),s)
and
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon\delta,M,s)
with
i=1
.
Assume that

\gamma=(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\operatorname{\iota}_{1}^{d}(M),\sigma)
so that we have

\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma^{\prime}={(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma)}\left[{0}/{\check{n}}\right]
and we
clearly have
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma^{\prime})
. Since
i=1
, for
n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)
we have
\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)=\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n},{n^{\prime}}}+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\delta:\eta\zeta)+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)

and we know that
\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)=1
.
It follows that
\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\delta:\eta\zeta)+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)=1
for all

n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)\setminus\{n\}
and moreover
that

\Sigma_{n}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=\Sigma_{n}(\varepsilon\delta:\eta\zeta)+\Sigma_{n}(s:\sigma)=0
. By Lemma
7.12
it follows that
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma^{\prime})
, and hence that
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma^{\prime})

as expected.

The case where
\gamma=(\eta 1\zeta,k,\operatorname{\iota}_{0}^{d}(M),\sigma)

is dealt with straightforwardly.

\blacktriangleright

c=(\varepsilon 1\delta,\theta^{d}(M),s)
and
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon i_{1}i_{2}\delta,M,s)
for some

i_{1},i_{2}\in\mathbb{D}
such that
i_{1}+i_{2}=1
.

Assume first that

\gamma=(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\theta^{d}(M),\sigma)
. Then

\gamma^{\prime}=(\eta\check{n}\check{n}\delta,k,M,\sigma)
.
We clearly have
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma^{\prime})
, and if
n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
we have

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\delta:\eta\zeta)+\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n^{\prime}},{n}}(i_{1}+i_{2})+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\delta:\eta\zeta)+\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n^{\prime}},{n}}+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)=1.

Assume next that

\gamma=(\eta 1\zeta,k,\theta^{d}(M),\sigma)
. Then

\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma^{\prime}=(\eta\check{k}\check{k}\zeta,k+1,M,\sigma)
so that we clearly have
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma^{\prime})
. For
n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)\uplus\{k\}
(since
\gamma
is well-formed) we have

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\delta:\eta\zeta)+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)=1\text{ if }n^{\prime}\not=k

and

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\delta:\eta\zeta)+i_{1}+i_{2}+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)=0+1+0\text{ if }n^{\prime}=k

since
i_{1}+i_{2}=1
and by our assumption that
\gamma
is
well-formed which implies that
\Sigma_{k}(c:\gamma)=0
.

The case where
c=(\varepsilon 0\delta,\theta^{d}(M),s)
, so that
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon 00\delta,M,s)
, is easy.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\check{\Theta}_{\mathsf{cd}}
with
\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}\gamma^{\prime}
. If

c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma^{\prime})
then there is
c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
such
that
c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}c^{\prime}
.

Proof 7.17
.

We consider only a few cases, the other ones being straightforward.

\blacktriangleright

\gamma=(\zeta u,k,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M},\sigma)
and

\gamma^{\prime}=(\zeta,M,k,\mathsf{D}(u)\cdot\sigma)
. Then we have
c^{\prime}=(\delta,M,\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s)
for some

i\in\mathbb{D}
(with
i=u
is
u\in\mathbb{D}
) and then

c=(\delta i,\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M},s)
satisfies the required
conditions.

\blacktriangleright

\gamma=(\zeta,k,\lambda x^{A}\,{M},\mathsf{D}(u)\cdot\sigma)
and

\gamma^{\prime}=(\zeta u,k,\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})},\sigma)
. Then we have
c^{\prime}=(\delta i,\lambda x^{\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}}\,{\partial({x},{M})},s)
for some

i\in\mathbb{D}
(with
i=u
if
u\in\mathbb{D}
) and then

c=(\delta,\lambda x^{A}\,{M},\mathsf{D}(i)\cdot s)
satisfies the required
conditions.

\blacktriangleright

\gamma=(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\operatorname{\iota}_{0}^{d}(M),\sigma)
and
\gamma^{\prime}=(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma)
applying
rule (
15
), so that we know that

n\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
.
Then we have
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon\delta,M,s)
.
Taking
c=(\varepsilon 0\delta,\operatorname{\iota}_{0}^{d}(M),s)
we have

c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}c^{\prime}
and
c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
since for all

n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
we have

\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=1
.
It is here that the restrictive condition in the
rule (
15
) of
Figure
13
is essential.

\blacktriangleright

\gamma=(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\operatorname{\iota}_{1}^{d}(M),\sigma)
and

\gamma^{\prime}={(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma)}\left[{0}/{\check{n}}\right]
. Then we have
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon\delta,M,s)
and we take

c=(\varepsilon 1\delta,\operatorname{\iota}_{1}^{d}(M),s)
so that
c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}c^{\prime}
. For

n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})\uplus\{n\}
we have
\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=1
if

n^{\prime}\not=n
. Since
c^{\prime}\in\mathsf{w}_{0}({(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma)}\left[{0}/{\check{n}}\right])
we have

\Sigma_{n}(c^{\prime}:(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma))=0
(that is, all
the occurrences of
\check{n}
in

(\eta\zeta,k,M,\sigma)
are filled with
0
’s in

c^{\prime}
) and hence
\Sigma_{n}(c:\gamma)=1
.

\blacktriangleright

\gamma=(\eta\check{n}\zeta,k,\theta^{d}(M),\sigma)
and

\gamma^{\prime}=(\eta\check{n}\check{n}\delta,k,M,\sigma)
. Then

c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon i_{1}i_{2}\delta,M,s)
for some

i_{1},i_{2}\in\mathbb{D}
such that
i=i_{1}+i_{2}\in\mathbb{D}
. Let
c=(\varepsilon i\delta,\theta^{d}(M),s)
so that
c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}c^{\prime}
and
c\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma)
. Then for
n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})
we have

\displaystyle\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\delta:\eta\zeta)+\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n^{\prime}},{n}}i+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(\varepsilon\delta:\eta\zeta)+\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n^{\prime}},{n}}i_{1}+\boldsymbol{\delta}_{{n^{\prime}},{n}}i_{2}+\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(s:\sigma)

\displaystyle=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=1

and hence
c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
.

\blacktriangleright

\gamma=(\eta 1\zeta,k,\theta^{d}(M),\sigma)
and

\gamma^{\prime}=(\eta\check{k}\check{k}\delta,k+1,M,\sigma)
.
Then
c^{\prime}=(\varepsilon i_{1}i_{2}\delta,M,s)
for some

i_{1},i_{2}\in\mathbb{D}
such that
i_{1}+i_{2}=1
: this is due to the fact
that we must have
\Sigma_{k}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=1
and we know that

\check{k}
does not occur in
\eta
,
\zeta
and
\sigma

since
\gamma
is well-formed. Let
c=(\varepsilon 1\delta,\theta^{d}(M),s)
so that
c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}c^{\prime}
and
c\in\mathsf{w}_{0}(\gamma)
. Then for
n^{\prime}\in\mathsf{ws}(\gamma)=\mathsf{ws}(\gamma^{\prime})\setminus\{k\}
we
have
\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c:\gamma)=\Sigma_{n^{\prime}}(c^{\prime}:\gamma^{\prime})=1

and hence
c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
.

Theorem 7.18
.

Let
M
be a term such that
{}\vdash{M}:{\iota}
and let

\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then

\displaystyle[\exists k\in{\mathbb{N}}\ (\langle\,\rangle,0,M,())\to_{\mathsf{w}}^{\ast}(\langle\,\rangle,k,\underline{\nu},())]\Leftrightarrow(\langle\,\rangle,M,())\to_{\mathsf{nd}}^{\ast}(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())

Moreover when one of these two reduction converges, the other one
does, with the same number of steps.

Proof 7.19
.

\blacktriangleright
(
\Leftarrow
) By an obvious induction on the length of the

\to_{\mathsf{nd}}
-reduction using Lemma
7.14
one
proves the following statement: if
(\langle\,\rangle,M,())\to_{\mathsf{nd}}^{\ast}c\not=0
then
(\langle\,\rangle,0,M,())\to_{\mathsf{w}}^{\ast}\gamma
with
c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
. We apply this statement to the case where
c=(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())
and obtain that
(\langle\,\rangle,0,M,())\to_{\mathsf{w}}^{\ast}\gamma
with
(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
, which means that
\gamma=(\langle\,\rangle,k,\underline{\nu},())
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

\blacktriangleright
(
\Rightarrow
) By an obvious induction on the length of the

\to_{\mathsf{w}}
-reduction using Lemma
7.16

one proves the following statement: if
\gamma\to_{\mathsf{w}}^{\ast}(\langle\,\rangle,k,\underline{\nu},())
for some

k\in{\mathbb{N}}
then there is
c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
such that
c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}^{\ast}(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())
. We apply this
statement to the case where
\gamma=(\langle\,\rangle,0,M,())
and
obtain
c
such that

c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}^{\ast}(\langle\,\rangle,\underline{\nu},())
and

c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
. This latter property means that

c=(\langle\,\rangle,M,())
.

Remark 7.20
.

The fact that the lengths of the deterministic
\to_{\mathsf{w}}
-reduction
and of the non-deterministic
\to_{\mathsf{nd}}
-reduction in
Theorem
7.18
are equal is of course
essential since it is always possible to simulate a
non-deterministic reduction by a deterministic one using
interleaving techniques. One can use the simulation and
co-simulation Lemmas
7.14

and
7.16
for proving various
generalizations of that theorem, using the fact that if

c\in\mathsf{w}(\gamma)
and
\gamma
contains no
\check{n}
’s then

c
and
\gamma
are essentially the same thing (the only
difference is the counter contained in
\gamma
).

Theorem 7.21
.

Let
M
be a term such that
{}\vdash{M}:{\iota}
and let

\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}
. Then we have
\vdash M:\nu:\iota
iff
(\langle\,\rangle,0,M,())\to_{\mathsf{w}}^{\ast}(\langle\,\rangle,k,\underline{\nu},())

for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Proof 7.22
.

By Theorems
7.18
,
6.15

and
7.9
, observing that
c\to_{\mathsf{nd}}^{\ast}c^{\prime}
is equivalent to the existence of
C
such that
[{c}]\to_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{fin}}({\Theta_{\mathsf{cd}}})}^{\ast}[{c^{\prime}}]+C
.

Conclusion

Building on the categorical axiomatization of coherent differentiation
introduced in
[
Ehr23
]
we have defined a differential
extension
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
of the standard Turing complete functional
programming language
PCF
.

The rewriting system of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
has many reduction rules and
therefore it would be probably rather difficult to prove this
determinism property syntactically (as a Church-Rosser property) so
our use of denotational semantics for this purpose seems really
crucial.
We also use semantics for proving that our rewriting system is
complete in the sense that it allows any closed term of type
\iota

whose interpretation in the relational model contains
\nu
to be
reduced to
\underline{\nu}
.
This proof is based on the use of a Krivine machine which is a way of
extracting from the general
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
rewriting system a fairly simple
though sufficiently expressive subsystem.
The completeness proof is based on a reducibility method that we have
been obliged to modify drastically in order to adapt it to this
differential setting.
To make the proof more readable, we present the relational semantics
of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
as a non-idempotent intersection typing system and
completeness can be understood as a normalization property for this
typing system.

One major novelty
21
21

21

And improvement in some sense.
of coherent
differentiation wrt.
\mathsf{DiLL}
is the fact that it is
deterministic.
This was already clear in
[
Ehr23
]
where we showed that
coherent differentiation admits deterministic models, that is models
where the superposition of the values
\mathbf{t}
and
\mathbf{f}
in the
type
\mathbf{Bool}
is rejected.
The present article provides a syntactic evidence of this determinism
via the fully deterministic version of our machine which is shown to
be sound and complete wrt. the execution of closed
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
-terms of
type
\iota
.

Future work

Our Krivine machine has no environment and uses actual substitutions
in terms for implementing
\beta
-reduction, as well as a syntactic
differential operation
\partial({x},{M})
defined by induction on
M
to
implement the differential reduction of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
.
From the viewpoint of efficiency this is of course not satisfactory
and we will present in a forthcoming paper a machine using a stack as
well as a (differential) environment not invoking any external
operation defined by induction on terms for executing the expressions
of our language.

The most puzzling questions however remain of a theoretical nature and
concern the exact operational meaning of our language
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
22
22

22

Or of its variants, we can of course expect to design more
syntactically elegant versions of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
in the next few months;
the version presented in this paper has been chosen for its
relatively straightforward denotational semantics.
, which has now fully satisfactory deterministic operational and
denotational semantics.
From a programming point of view, what is
exactly the meaning of the type construction
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{A}
and in what
kind of programming situation could it be useful, as well as the
syntactic term construction
\widetilde{\mathsf{D}}{M}
?
The few examples provided in Section
4.4
do
clearly not answer this question.
One way to address it could be to consider a probabilistic
extension of
\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
, for which differentiation has a clear
mathematical meaning easily expressed in
\mathbf{Pcoh}
as we have seen in
Section
3.4
.

Another interesting direction, which might require the extension of

\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
with richer types
23
23

23

Most models of
LL
support inductive and coinductive
definitions so such extensions should not be problematic.
, would be to understand if it has connection with incremental
programming where syntactic constructs of a differential nature are
also used. Such a connection remains however highly conjectural.
More specifically, in
[
EL10
,
EL07
]
, we
have suggested possible connections between
\mathsf{DiLL}
and various
process calculi, it might be worthwhile to understand if such
connections could be related to incremental computing and benefit from
coherent differentiation.

Last, as suggested in Section
4.4
, the fact that

\Lambda_{\mathsf{cd}}
has at the same time a general fixpoint construct and a
differential construct means that it is possible to define programs by
some kind of “differential equations” (recursive definitions of
functions whose body contains the possibly higher derivatives of the
functions being defined) and that such programs can be executed in our
Krivine machine(s); this is a very exciting feature of our setting
which justifies investigations
per se
.
